A consolidated index of the kernel source files each chapter works from. These lists were previously scattered through the chapters as end-of-section “Source Trail” blocks; gathering them here keeps the narrative uninterrupted and makes the files easy to look up in one place.

Chapter 1 — Introduction

One row per major topic of the chapter. Chapter 1 is a tour, so each row points at the one or two files where the idea it introduces is easiest to see in the source; the chapters that cover the topic in depth carry the fuller lists.

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
LicensingCOPYING, LICENSES/preferred/GPL-2.0, LICENSES/exceptions/Linux-syscall-noteThe whole licensing story in three files. COPYING is twenty lines that state the kernel’s SPDX identifier, GPL-2.0 WITH Linux-syscall-note; the syscall note is the short exception that keeps user programs which merely call into the kernel outside the GPL’s reach.
Version numberingMakefileThe first four lines of the top-level Makefile — VERSION, PATCHLEVEL, SUBLEVEL, EXTRAVERSION — are where 7.0.12 is defined and the only place it is. Everything else, from uname -r to the module vermagic, is derived from them.
Extension mechanismskernel/module/main.c, kernel/bpf/, rust/kernel/, kernel/livepatch/, kernel/sched/ext.cOne entry point for each way Figure 1-4 shows code being added to a monolithic kernel: loadable modules (Appendix B), eBPF programs (Chapter 23), Rust abstractions (Chapter 26), live patches, and BPF-defined scheduling classes (Chapter 7). All of them run in the same address space with full privilege, which is why each comes with its own trust mechanism.
Preemption modelskernel/Kconfig.preempt, kernel/sched/core.cThe Preemption Model choice in Kconfig.preempt is the authority for Table 1-6. Read the depends on lines: PREEMPT_NONE requires ARCH_NO_PREEMPT and PREEMPT_VOLUNTARY requires !ARCH_HAS_PREEMPT_LAZY, so on x86_64 and ARM64 only full and lazy preemption remain, with lazy as the default. PREEMPT_RT is a separate bool outside the choice. setup_preempt_mode() in core.c handles the preempt= boot parameter that PREEMPT_DYNAMIC kernels accept.
Users, groups, and capabilitiesinclude/linux/cred.h, include/uapi/linux/capability.hstruct cred is where a process’s user and group IDs actually live, separately from task_struct, so that they can be shared and replaced atomically. The UAPI header lists every capability bit that has replaced the single all-powerful root check.
Files and the VFSinclude/linux/fs.h, fs/open.c, fs/namei.c, fs/read_write.cstruct inode, struct file, and the operation tables that make up the VFS are all in fs.h. do_sys_open(), path walking, and vfs_read()/vfs_write() are the system calls the chapter’s file-handling examples go through (Chapter 12).
Entering the kernelarch/x86/entry/entry_64.S, arch/x86/entry/syscall_64.c, arch/arm64/kernel/entry-common.c, arch/arm64/kernel/syscall.cThe two system-call paths of the arch-compare box. On x86_64, entry_SYSCALL_64 calls do_syscall_64(), which dispatches through x64_sys_call(), a generated switch; the comment above it explains why sys_call_table[] is no longer used for dispatch. On ARM64, el0t_64_sync_handler() calls el0_svc() and then invoke_syscall(), which clamps the number with array_index_nospec() before indexing the table (Chapter 10).
Processes and their statesinclude/linux/sched.hstruct task_struct and the state bits of Table 1-16. The defines near the top of the file show that TASK_IDLE and TASK_KILLABLE are not new states but combinations of existing bits, which is why the table lists them with combined values.
Process address spaceinclude/linux/mm_types.h, mm/mmap_lock.c, lib/maple_tree.cstruct mm_struct holds the VMAs in a maple tree (mm_mt) rather than the old red-black tree and linked list. lock_vma_under_rcu() in mmap_lock.c is the per-VMA locking that lets most page faults avoid mmap_lock altogether (Chapter 9).
Synchronizationinclude/linux/spinlock.h, include/linux/seqlock.h, include/linux/rcupdate.h, kernel/time/timekeeping.cThe three primitives the chapter introduces. timekeeping.c holds the seqcount example the chapter uses in place of the old xtime_lock: tk_core.seq, which readers retry around while the timer interrupt updates the clock (Chapter 5).
Process creation and terminationkernel/fork.c, fs/exec.c, kernel/exit.c, include/linux/pid.hcopy_process(), begin_new_exec(), and do_exit() are the life cycle in three functions, and enum pid_type in pid.h is the source of Table 1-22 (Chapters 3 and 20).
Process schedulingkernel/sched/core.c, kernel/sched/fair.c, kernel/sched/ext.c, include/asm-generic/vmlinux.lds.h__schedule() in core.c asks each scheduling class in turn for a task. The order is fixed at link time by the SCHED_DATA macro in vmlinux.lds.h: stop, deadline, real-time, fair, ext, idle. fair.c is now the EEVDF implementation, and ext.c is sched_ext (Chapter 7).
Signals and IPCkernel/signal.c, ipc/send_signal_locked() and get_signal() are the two halves of signal delivery (Chapter 11). ipc/ holds the System V message queues, semaphores, and shared memory, and the POSIX message queues, of Table 1-24 (Chapter 19).
Memory managementinclude/linux/mmzone.h, mm/page_alloc.c, mm/slub.c, mm/filemap.c, fs/fs-writeback.cmmzone.h defines the zones of Table 1-26. The buddy allocator and SLUB sit in the next two files (Chapter 8). The page cache is now an XArray of folios in filemap.c, and per-device bdi_writeback work in fs-writeback.c has replaced the old pdflush threads (Chapter 15).
Device driversinclude/linux/device.h, include/linux/device/driver.h, include/linux/kobject.h, drivers/base/core.cstruct device, struct device_driver, and the kobject under both are the device model the chapter sketches. drivers/base/ is where devices and drivers are matched and published in sysfs (Chapter 13).
Configuration and buildscripts/kconfig/, scripts/Makefile.build, fs/ext2/Makefile, fs/ext2/Kconfigscripts/kconfig/ turns the Kconfig files into .config. Makefile.build is Kbuild proper: it interprets obj-y, obj-m, <module>-y, and always-y. The ext2 Makefile and Kconfig are the chapter’s worked example of a multi-file object whose optional parts are selected by Kconfig symbols.
Checking, testing, and toolchainsMakefile, scripts/checkpatch.pl, scripts/coccicheck, scripts/coccinelle/, tools/testing/selftests/, tools/testing/kunit/kunit.py, lib/kunit/, kernel/kcov.c, include/uapi/linux/kcov.h, Documentation/kbuild/llvm.rst, arch/Kconfigmake C=1 (Sparse), W= levels, and coccicheck modes; checkpatch rules; kselftest TARGETS and the KUnit runner; __sanitizer_cov_trace_pc() and the KCOV_INIT_TRACE/KCOV_ENABLE ioctls; LLVM=1, HAS_LTO_CLANG, CFI, and SHADOW_CALL_STACK
Booting to start_kernelarch/x86/kernel/head64.c, arch/arm64/kernel/head.S, init/main.cWhere the architecture hands over: x86_64_start_reservations() on x86_64 and __primary_switched on ARM64 both call start_kernel(). In init/main.c, rest_init() creates PID 1 (kernel_init) and PID 2 (kthreadd), and kernel_init() ends by trying /sbin/init (Appendix A).

Chapter 2 — Memory Addressing

One row per section of the chapter. Most rows pair x86_64 files with their ARM64 counterparts, because the chapter treats the two architectures side by side. These are the files the chapter reads from directly; it cites a great many more in passing.

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
Memory addressesarch/x86/include/asm/page_64_types.h, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h, Documentation/arch/x86/x86_64/mm.rst, arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h, Documentation/arch/arm64/memory.rstWhere each architecture fixes the virtual-address width and the split between the user and kernel halves of the address space. The two mm.rst/memory.rst files are the authoritative maps.
Segmentation on x86_64arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h, arch/x86/include/asm/desc_defs.h, arch/x86/include/asm/msr-index.h, arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c, arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S, arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c, Documentation/arch/arm64/memory.rst, arch/arm64/include/asm/mmu_context.h, arch/arm64/kernel/process.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/percpu.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/ptrace.h, arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S, arch/arm64/mm/context.cWhat survives of segmentation on x86_64: the GDT selectors and descriptor formats, the FS/GS base MSRs, SWAPGS on kernel entry, and the per-thread FS/GS bases switched in __switch_to(). The ARM64 files show the same jobs — per-CPU data, the thread pointer, and address-space identity — done with TPIDR_EL1, TPIDR_EL0, and ASIDs instead.
Paging in hardwarearch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h, Documentation/arch/x86/x86_64/mm.rst, arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-prot.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-types.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/sysreg.h, arch/arm64/mm/proc.SThe hardware page-table formats: x86_64’s four- and five-level layout and its _PAGE_* entry bits, ARM64’s granule-dependent levels and PTE_*/PROT_* attributes, and the TCR_EL1 setup in __cpu_setup that tells the MMU which format to walk.
The MMU and the SMMU/IOMMUarch/x86/include/asm/tlbflush.h, arch/arm64/mm/context.c, include/linux/iommu.h, drivers/iommu/iommu.c, drivers/iommu/iommu-sva.c, drivers/iommu/dma-iommu.c, drivers/iommu/io-pgtable-arm.c, include/linux/io-pgtable.h, drivers/iommu/intel/iommu.h, drivers/iommu/intel/iommu.c, drivers/iommu/intel/dmar.c, drivers/iommu/intel/svm.c, drivers/iommu/arm/arm-smmu/arm-smmu.h, drivers/iommu/arm/arm-smmu-v3/arm-smmu-v3.h, drivers/iommu/arm/arm-smmu-v3/arm-smmu-v3.c, drivers/iommu/arm/arm-smmu-v3/arm-smmu-v3-sva.c, include/linux/dma-mapping.hThe CPU side is per-CPU TLB state (struct tlb_state) and ASID allocation (active_asids, flush_context()). The device side is the generic IOMMU core — domains, groups, iommu_map(), PASID binding through iommu_sva_bind_device(), and the DMA-API bridge in dma-iommu.c — the ARM LPAE I/O page tables behind struct io_pgtable_ops, and the two drivers the chapter follows: Intel VT-d (DMAR parsing, root and context entries, SVA) and ARM SMMUv2/v3 (command queue, context descriptors). dma-mapping.h is the API drivers actually call.
Hardware cachesinclude/linux/cache.h, include/linux/prefetch.h, arch/x86/include/asm/cache.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/cache.h, arch/x86/mm/pat/memtype.c, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h, arch/arm64/mm/proc.S, arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/cacheflush.h, arch/arm64/mm/cache.S, kernel/sched/sched.h, include/linux/mmzone.h, include/linux/netdevice.hCache-line annotations (__cacheline_aligned, __read_mostly, CACHELINE_PADDING), prefetch hints, and each architecture’s L1_CACHE_SHIFT; memory types through x86 PAT (pgprot_writecombine()) and ARM64’s MAIR_EL1 indexes (MT_NORMAL, MT_DEVICE_nGnRnE); ARM64’s explicit cache maintenance (dcache_by_line_op, flush_icache_range()); and three hot structures laid out around cache lines: struct rq, struct zone, and struct net_device.
Translation lookaside buffersarch/x86/include/asm/tlbflush.h, arch/x86/include/asm/invpcid.h, arch/x86/include/asm/processor-flags.h, arch/x86/mm/tlb.c, arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/mmu.h, arch/arm64/mm/context.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h, arch/arm64/kernel/cpufeature.cx86 PCIDs — the TLB_NR_DYN_ASIDS dynamic slots, CR3_NOFLUSH, INVPCID, switch_mm_irqs_off(), the tlb_single_page_flush_ceiling heuristic, and the global ASID pool — and ARM64 ASIDs: TTBR_ASID_MASK, generation rollover in check_and_switch_context(), and TLBI wrappers with range encoding and TTL hints, gated by ARM64_HAS_TLB_RANGE. cpu/common.c reports the TLB sizes from CPUID.
Handling the hardware cache and TLBarch/x86/mm/tlb.c, arch/x86/include/asm/tlbflush.h, arch/x86/include/asm/invpcid.h, arch/x86/include/asm/tlb.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/mmu_context.h, include/asm-generic/tlb.h, mm/mmu_gather.c, arch/x86/mm/pat/set_memory.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/cacheflush.hThe flush interfaces built on that hardware: flush_tlb_mm_range() and __invpcid() on x86, flush_tlb_all() on ARM64, and the generic struct mmu_gather, which batches an unmap so that pages are freed only after the flush (tlb_flush_mmu_free()). set_memory.c is the example of a change that must flush caches and TLBs together.
Paging in Linuxinclude/asm-generic/pgtable-nop4d.h, include/asm-generic/pgtable-nopud.h, include/asm-generic/pgtable-nopmd.h, include/asm-generic/pgalloc.h, include/asm-generic/tlb.h, include/linux/pgtable.h, include/linux/pagewalk.h, include/linux/mm.h, include/linux/mm_types.h, mm/pagewalk.c, mm/mmu_gather.c, mm/mmap.c, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h, arch/x86/include/asm/pgalloc.h, arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c, arch/x86/mm/pgprot.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-types.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/pgalloc.hThe architecture-independent five-level model: folding of unused levels, the generic offset macros and the CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS check, struct ptdesc with pagetable_alloc() and pagetable_ctor(), the walk_page_range() walker, and batched page-table freeing through tlb_remove_table(). Then each architecture’s entry accessors and allocators, x86’s pgd_alloc() with its KPTI shadow, and protection_map[16] with vm_get_page_prot(), which turns VMA flags into entry bits.
Physical memory modelmm/sparse.c, mm/page_alloc.c, mm/memory_hotplug.c, mm/memremap.c, include/linux/mmzone.h, include/linux/mm_types.h, include/linux/mm.h, include/linux/memremap.h, include/linux/page-flags.h, include/linux/page-flags-layout.h, arch/x86/include/asm/sparsemem.h, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/sparsemem.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.hSPARSEMEM with vmemmap: struct mem_section, pfn_valid(), and SECTION_SIZE_BITS (27 on x86_64; 27 or 29 on ARM64, by page size). Also struct page, struct folio, and how zone, node, and section are packed into the page flags; zonelists and watermarks (build_zonelists(), setup_per_zone_wmarks()); memory hotplug, including MHP_MEMMAP_ON_MEMORY; and device pages through memremap_pages() and struct dev_pagemap.
Process page tablesinclude/linux/mm_types.h, kernel/fork.c, mm/mmap.c, mm/memory.c, fs/exec.c, arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c, arch/x86/mm/tlb.c, arch/x86/mm/init_64.c, arch/arm64/mm/context.c, arch/arm64/mm/pgd.cstruct mm_struct and its VMAs; how fork() duplicates an address space (dup_mm(), dup_mmap(), and copy_page_range(), which write-protects for copy-on-write) and execve() replaces it (exec_mmap()); per-architecture PGD allocation; and the switch that loads the new tables: switch_mm_irqs_off() on x86, check_and_switch_context() on ARM64. init_64.c shows how x86 keeps the kernel half shared by every PGD.
Kernel page tablesmm/init-mm.c, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64.h, arch/x86/kernel/head_64.S, arch/x86/boot/startup/map_kernel.c, arch/x86/mm/init_64.c, arch/x86/mm/init.c, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h, arch/x86/include/asm/page_64.h, arch/x86/include/asm/fixmap.h, arch/x86/mm/pat/set_memory.c, arch/x86/mm/dump_pagetables.c, arch/arm64/kernel/head.S, arch/arm64/mm/mmu.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/fixmap.h, kernel/kthread.cinit_mm and swapper_pg_dir; the boot-time tables (early_top_pgt and __startup_64() on x86, primary_entry on ARM64) and the full direct map built by init_memory_mapping() and paging_init(); page_offset_base, the fixmap, and the vmalloc bounds; permission changes through set_memory_ro() and its siblings; the debugfs page-table dump; and kthread_use_mm(), by which a kernel thread borrows a user address space.
Two-stage address translationarch/x86/kvm/vmx/vmx.c, arch/x86/kvm/mmu/spte.h, arch/x86/kvm/mmu/mmu.c, arch/x86/kvm/mmu/tdp_mmu.c, arch/x86/include/asm/kvm_host.h, arch/x86/kvm/vmx/nested.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/kvm_host.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/kvm_pgtable.h, arch/arm64/kvm/hyp/pgtable.c, arch/arm64/kvm/mmu.c, arch/arm64/kvm/vmid.c, arch/arm64/kvm/arm.c, arch/arm64/kvm/reset.c, arch/arm64/kvm/nested.cIntel EPT — enable_ept, the SPTE format, struct kvm_mmu, the TDP MMU’s kvm_tdp_mmu_map(), and nested EPT with INVEPT/INVVPID — and ARM64 stage 2: struct kvm_s2_mmu, kvm_pgtable_stage2_map(), kvm_handle_guest_abort(), VMID allocation in new_vmid(), the IPA size limit, and nested stage 2.
Kernel virtual memory layoutDocumentation/arch/x86/x86_64/mm.rst, Documentation/arch/arm64/memory.rst, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h, arch/x86/include/asm/page_64_types.h, arch/x86/mm/kaslr.c, arch/x86/include/asm/cpu_entry_area.h, arch/x86/mm/cpu_entry_area.c, arch/x86/include/asm/fixmap.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/fixmap.h, include/asm-generic/fixmap.h, mm/vmalloc.c, mm/sparse-vmemmap.c, arch/x86/mm/kasan_init_64.c, arch/arm64/mm/kasan_init.c, arch/x86/kernel/module.cThe two documented maps and the constants behind them: PAGE_OFFSET for four- and five-level paging, the vmalloc and module ranges, region randomization in kernel_randomize_memory(), struct cpu_entry_area, the fixmaps and __fix_to_virt(), the vmalloc allocator’s struct vmap_area tree, vmemmap_populate(), the KASAN shadow, and the module-relocation overflow checks.
Kernel page-table isolationarch/x86/mm/pti.c, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h, arch/x86/include/asm/pgalloc.h, arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c, arch/x86/entry/calling.h, arch/x86/include/asm/processor-flags.h, arch/x86/mm/tlb.c, arch/x86/include/asm/tlbflush.h, arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S, arch/arm64/mm/proc.S, arch/arm64/include/asm/mmu_context.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/fixmap.hOn x86, pti_init() and the user copy of the PGD allocated beside the kernel one, the SWITCH_TO_KERNEL_CR3/SWITCH_TO_USER_CR3 macros in calling.h, and the paired kernel and user PCIDs whose deferred flushes user_pcid_flush_mask records. On ARM64, the trampoline vectors (tramp_map_kernel), their fixmap slots, and the switch of kernel mappings to non-global in proc.S.
Address-space hardening (x86_64)arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c, arch/x86/include/asm/mmu_context.h, arch/x86/include/asm/uaccess_64.h, arch/x86/mm/tlb.c, arch/x86/include/uapi/asm/processor-flags.h, arch/x86/include/asm/smap.h, arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c, arch/x86/kernel/traps.c, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h, arch/x86/kernel/shstk.c, arch/x86/include/asm/msr-index.h, arch/x86/kernel/cet.c, arch/x86/include/asm/special_insns.h, arch/x86/include/asm/cpufeatures.h, arch/x86/configs/hardening.configLAM (enabled through arch_prctl(), folded into CR3 by build_cr3(), and stripped by __untagged_addr()), LASS (setup_lass(), lass_stac(), GP_LASS_VIOLATION), and CET shadow stacks (pte_shstk(), shstk_setup(), do_user_cp_fault()), with the feature bits and the hardening.config fragment that turns them on.

Chapter 3 — Processes

One row per section of the chapter. Most rows pair the generic code with its x86_64 and ARM64 counterparts where the two architectures differ — chiefly how current is found and how the context switch saves registers and FPU state. These are the files the chapter reads from directly; it cites a great many more in passing.

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
Processes, LWPs, and threadsinclude/uapi/linux/sched.h, kernel/fork.c, include/linux/sched/signal.hThe CLONE_* flags that decide what a new task shares, and signal_struct, the object a thread group shares
The process descriptorinclude/linux/sched.h, init/init_task.c, include/linux/sched/task.hstruct task_struct itself, the statically built init_task, and the reference-counting helpers that keep a descriptor alive
Process stateinclude/linux/sched.h, kernel/sched/core.c, kernel/freezer.cThe TASK_* bits and set_special_state(); try_to_wake_up(), which moves a task back to TASK_RUNNING; and __refrigerator(), which parks a task in TASK_FROZEN
Identifying a processinclude/linux/pid.h, kernel/pid.c, fs/pidfs.c, include/linux/pid_namespace.hstruct pid and its per-namespace upid numbers, PID allocation through the namespace IDR, pidfd_open(), and the pidfs inodes behind every pidfd
Process descriptor handlingkernel/fork.c, include/linux/thread_info.h, arch/x86/include/asm/current.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/current.h, arch/x86/include/asm/page_64_types.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.hAllocation of the descriptor and the vmalloc-backed kernel stack, and how each architecture finds current: a per-CPU variable on x86_64, the SP_EL0 register on ARM64
Doubly linked listsinclude/linux/types.h, include/linux/list.h, include/linux/container_of.h, include/linux/rculist.h, lib/list_debug.clist_head and hlist_head, the iterators built on container_of(), the RCU-safe variants used to walk the task list, and the corruption checks
Core kernel data structuresinclude/linux/rbtree.h, include/linux/rbtree_augmented.h, lib/rbtree.c, include/linux/idr.h, lib/idr.c, include/linux/kref.h, include/linux/llist.h, include/linux/kfifo.h, lib/kfifo.c, include/linux/hashtable.h, include/linux/notifier.h, kernel/notifier.crb_add() / rb_insert_color() and the cached and augmented trees; idr_alloc_cyclic(), idr_replace(), and the IDA bitmap chunks over the XArray; kref_put() on refcount_t; the llist_add_batch() cmpxchg loop; kfifo free-running indices and smp_wmb(); DEFINE_HASHTABLE(); notifier_call_chain() and the atomic, blocking, raw, and SRCU chains
Process list and run queuesinclude/linux/sched/signal.h, kernel/sched/sched.h, kernel/sched/fair.c, kernel/sched/rt.c, kernel/sched/deadline.cfor_each_process(); struct rq and its per-class queues; the EEVDF tree that __enqueue_entity() fills and pick_eevdf() searches; the RT priority array and the deadline tree
Wait queuesinclude/linux/wait.h, kernel/sched/wait.cwait_queue_head and the wait_event*() macros, and __wake_up_common(), which separates exclusive from non-exclusive waiters
Resource limitsinclude/uapi/asm-generic/resource.h, kernel/sys.c, include/linux/user_namespace.h, kernel/ucount.cThe RLIMIT_* numbers and defaults, do_prlimit(), and the ucounts that charge the four per-user limits to each user namespace level
Process switch on x86_64kernel/sched/core.c, arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S, arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c, arch/x86/include/asm/fpu/sched.h, arch/x86/mm/tlb.ccontext_switch(), the stack swap in __switch_to_asm, the segment and FSGSBASE work in __switch_to(), the eager FPU save, and the PCID-aware switch_mm_irqs_off()
Process switch on ARM64arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S, arch/arm64/kernel/process.c, arch/arm64/kernel/fpsimd.c, arch/arm64/mm/context.ccpu_switch_to, which swaps callee-saved registers and SP_EL0; __switch_to(); fpsimd_thread_switch(); and ASID allocation for the incoming address space
Creating processeskernel/fork.c, include/uapi/linux/sched.h, kernel/pid.cclone3() and struct clone_args, kernel_clone() and copy_process(), and PID allocation with an optional set_tid
Kernel threadskernel/kthread.c, include/linux/kthread.h, init/main.c, kernel/sched/idle.ckthreadd and kthread_create_on_node(), the creation of PID 1 and PID 2 in rest_init(), and the per-CPU idle loop

Chapter 6 — Timing Measurements

One row per section of the chapter. The timer subsystem is unusually layered — hardware counter, clocksource, clock event device, tick, timer — and the files divide along exactly those layers, so the split below is the subsystem’s own rather than an editorial one.

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
Two kinds of timinginclude/linux/clocksource.h, include/linux/clockchips.h, include/linux/jiffies.h, kernel/time/jiffies.c, include/uapi/linux/time.h, kernel/sched/clock.c, include/linux/sched/clock.hThe split the whole chapter rests on: a device that counts versus a device that fires. kernel/time/jiffies.c is the rating-1 floor that guarantees a tick source always exists.
x86 hardware countersarch/x86/kernel/tsc.c, arch/x86/include/asm/tsc.h, arch/x86/kernel/tsc_sync.c, arch/x86/kernel/hpet.c, arch/x86/kernel/apic/apic.c, arch/x86/KconfigTSC calibration, the constant and nonstop feature checks, cross-CPU synchronisation testing, and the HPET and local APIC timers.
Legacy and hypervisor x86 countersdrivers/clocksource/acpi_pm.c, include/linux/acpi_pmtmr.h, drivers/clocksource/i8253.c, drivers/clocksource/hyperv_timer.c, arch/x86/coco/sev/core.cThe fallbacks below the TSC, the Hyper-V reference counter, and the SEV-SNP secure TSC that a confidential guest must use instead.
ARM64 hardware countersdrivers/clocksource/arm_arch_timer.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/arch_timer.h, drivers/clocksource/arm_arch_timer_mmio.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/pvclock-abi.hThe architected timer that gives ARM64 one counter and comparator in the architecture itself, plus its errata workarounds.

The clocksource frameworkkernel/time/clocksource.c, include/linux/clocksource.h, include/linux/clocksource_ids.hRating-based selection, the mult/shift conversion, and the watchdog that demotes a clocksource caught drifting.

The private header holds the tick layer’s internal interface.

Tick consumerskernel/sched/fair.c, kernel/sched/sched.h, kernel/rcu/tree.c, drivers/cpufreq/cpufreq.c, fs/proc/stat.cWhy stopping the tick is intrusive: the scheduler, RCU, cpufreq sampling and /proc/stat all assumed it was running.
High-resolution timerskernel/time/hrtimer.c, include/linux/hrtimer.h, include/linux/hrtimer_defs.h, include/linux/hrtimer_types.h, include/linux/interrupt.h, kernel/softirq.cThe per-CPU clock bases, red-black tree ordering, the softirq-deferred _SOFT bases, and reprogramming on every change.

Chapter 7 — Process Scheduling

Almost everything here is in kernel/sched/, and two files in it — kernel/sched/core.c and kernel/sched/sched.h — appear in most rows because they hold the class-independent core and the definitions every class shares. The rows below name what each section adds on top of those two.

Chapter 8 — Memory Management

Chapter 8 spans four allocators and nine cross-cutting subsystems, and the rows follow the chapter’s four parts in order. Roughly a third of these files are the same three — mm/page_alloc.c, mm/slub.c and mm/vmalloc.c — which is itself the point: everything in the chapter eventually reaches one of them.

The mmap() path and unmapped-area searchmm/mmap.c, mm/vma.c, mm/util.c, include/linux/mman.h, include/linux/fs.h, arch/x86/kernel/sys_x86_64.c, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64.hArgument validation in do_mmap(), the phases of __mmap_region(), the mmap_prepare hook that replaces f_op->mmap, MDWE, and the gap search including the architecture overrides x86_64 supplies.
Hardware-assisted region typesarch/x86/kernel/shstk.c, arch/arm64/mm/gcs.c, mm/mprotect.c, mm/secretmem.cShadow stacks reach the same do_mmap() request from Intel CET and ARM GCS; protection keys and secret memory likewise turn architecture features into ordinary VMA flags.
Architecture fault entryarch/x86/mm/fault.c, arch/arm64/mm/fault.c, arch/x86/include/asm/trap_pf.h, mm/mmap_lock.cDecode hardware exceptions, classify the error code, absorb stack expansion in lock_mm_and_find_vma(), and enter the generic MM fault path.
Generic fault handlingmm/memory.c, include/linux/mm.h, include/linux/mm_types.h, mm/swapfile.cDefine struct vm_fault, the FAULT_FLAG_* and VM_FAULT_* vocabularies, handle_mm_fault(), CoW, fault-around, and core demand paging.
Per-VMA lockinginclude/linux/mm_types.h, include/linux/mmap_lock.h, mm/mmap_lock.c, include/linux/vm_event_item.h, include/linux/sched.hRefcount-based fast-path synchronization for page faults, and the vma_lock_* statistics that report how often it succeeds.
Transparent huge faultsmm/huge_memory.cTHP allocation, huge PMD creation, and huge write-protect handling.
Kernel-mode faultsarch/x86/mm/fault.c, arch/x86/include/asm/extable.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/extable.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/asm-extable.h, kernel/extable.cVmalloc sync, exception table fixups for copy_*_user(), and oops handling.
userfaultfdfs/userfaultfd.c, mm/userfaultfd.c, include/uapi/linux/userfaultfd.h, include/linux/userfaultfd_k.h, include/linux/swapops.hUser-space fault delegation, the ioctl protocol, the mfill resolution family, and PTE markers.
Fork and mm lifetimekernel/fork.c, kernel/exit.c, mm/mmap.c, kernel/kthread.c, kernel/sched/core.c, Documentation/mm/active_mm.rstAddress-space duplication, final release, and teardown.
Exec replacementfs/exec.c, mm/vma_exec.cTemporary mm and initial stack VMA, the point of no return, and the switch to a new mm_struct.
Heap managementmm/mmap.c, mm/vma.c, mm/util.c, fs/binfmt_elf.c, arch/x86/kernel/process.cbrk(), do_brk_flags(), aligned unmap on shrink, and where start_brk is placed and randomized.
Region transformsmm/mremap.c, mm/mprotect.c, mm/madvise.c, mm/mlock.c, mm/mseal.cResizing, permission changes, policy hints, and memory locking.
Residency queriesmm/mincore.c, mm/filemap.cmincore()’s page-table walk and page-cache lookup, the can_do_mincore() write-permission check that hides other users’ page-cache state, and cachestat(), its file-descriptor-based successor.
Pinning user pages (GUP)mm/gup.c, include/linux/mm.h, mm/internal.h, include/linux/mm_types.h, Documentation/core-api/pin_user_pages.rstThe get and pin families, try_grab_folio() and the pin bias, the slow and lockless fast walks, check_vma_flags(), long-term-pin migration in __gup_longterm_locked(), and gup_must_unshare(). The FOLL_* comment in mm_types.h and the documentation file explain which callers must use which flag.
Pin consumerslib/iov_iter.c, block/bio.c, io_uring/memmap.c, io_uring/rsrc.c, drivers/infiniband/core/umem.c, drivers/vfio/vfio_iommu_type1.c, drivers/iommu/iommufd/pages.c, drivers/dma-buf/udmabuf.c, mm/memfd.cDirect I/O, io_uring registered buffers, RDMA memory regions, VFIO and iommufd DMA mappings, and udmabuf — the main callers of pin_user_pages*() and memfd_pin_folios(), and where RLIMIT_MEMLOCK is charged against locked_vm or pinned_vm.

Restart and signal interactioninclude/linux/errno.h, include/linux/restart_block.h, include/linux/thread_info.h, include/linux/sched/signal.h, kernel/signal.c, kernel/sched/wait.c, kernel/entry/common.c, kernel/time/hrtimer.c, kernel/futex/waitwake.c, kernel/futex/pi.c, fs/select.c, fs/open.c, kernel/fork.c, arch/x86/kernel/signal.c, arch/x86/include/asm/thread_info.h, arch/arm64/kernel/signal.c, arch/arm64/kernel/signal32.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/ptrace.hThe four internal -ERESTART* codes, their numeric values and the signal_pending() precondition the header states, the generic wait machinery in prepare_to_wait_event() that makes -ERESTARTSYS the default for every wait_event_interruptible(), the refusal in close() to be restarted at all and the pre-flight -ERESTARTNOINTR in copy_process(), the three-armed restart_block union with set_restart_fn() and the do_no_restart_syscall() disarm, the saved-sigmask protocol built from set_user_sigmask(), sigmask_to_save() and restore_saved_sigmask_unless(), and the two architecture strategies: the late x86_64 switches with arch_set_restart_data() and get_nr_restart_syscall(), versus the optimistic ARM64 rewind that forget_syscall() makes idempotent and the regs->pc == restart_addr guard reverts.

Kernel-side callersfs/open.c, fs/read_write.c, fs/init.c, fs/exec.c, fs/namei.c, fs/select.c, fs/kernel_read_file.c, kernel/fork.c, kernel/sys_ni.c, kernel/umh.c, mm/mmap.c, init/main.c, init/do_mounts.c, init/initramfs.c, arch/x86/kernel/sys_ia32.c, arch/arm64/kernel/sys32.c, include/linux/syscalls.h, include/linux/init_syscalls.h, include/linux/kernel_read_file.h, include/linux/cleanup.h, include/linux/file.h, include/linux/linkage.h, include/asm-generic/compat.hWhy the pt_regs signature that SYSCALL_DEFINE generates makes sys_*() uncallable from C and therefore makes the ksys_* and do_sys_* layering necessary, the FTRUNCATE_LFS style of hoisting ABI differences into an explicit parameter, the compat_arg_u64_dual() and compat_arg_u64_glue() macros and the __ARCH_WANT_COMPAT_* gating, the x86 ia32 and ARM64 aarch32 architecture wrappers, the ITER_KVEC basis of kernel_read()/kernel_write() and the file-purpose identifiers of kernel_read_file(), the CLASS() and __free() scope-based cleanup idiom now pervasive in these files, the seventeen init_* boot-time helpers and kernel_execve(), call_usermodehelper(), and the COND_SYSCALL/sys_ni_syscall mechanism for system calls that were configured out.

Chapter 11 — Signals

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
Core signal data structuresinclude/linux/signal_types.h, include/linux/signal.h, include/linux/sched/signal.h, include/uapi/asm-generic/signal.h, include/uapi/asm-generic/signal-defs.h, include/uapi/asm-generic/siginfo.h, include/uapi/linux/signal.h, arch/x86/include/uapi/asm/signal.hWhere struct sigpending, struct sigqueue, struct k_sigaction and struct sighand_struct are declared, the _NSIG/_NSIG_BPW/_NSIG_WORDS definitions that make a 64-bit mask one word on x86_64 and ARM64 and two on 32-bit architectures, the SA_* flag values, and the siginfo union with the si_code namespaces that tell a handler where a signal came from.
Signal set manipulationinclude/linux/signal.h, kernel/signal.cThe sigaddset()/sigismember()/sigorsets() family and the _NSIG_WORDS == 1 specializations beside the general loops, plus next_signal(), which is where the ordering that gives low-numbered and real-time signals their relative priority is actually decided.
Generation: the core send pathkernel/signal.c, kernel/exit.c, kernel/fork.c, include/linux/sched/signal.hsend_signal_locked(), __send_signal_locked() and complete_signal(): allocation of a sigqueue entry under RLIMIT_SIGPENDING, the legacy collapse of duplicate standard signals, the choice between a thread’s private queue and the thread group’s shared_pending, the wants_signal() test that picks a target thread, and the group-stop and group-exit paths that let one signal terminate every thread.
Forced and synchronous signalskernel/signal.c, arch/x86/mm/fault.c, arch/arm64/mm/fault.c, arch/x86/kernel/traps.c, arch/arm64/kernel/traps.cforce_sig(), force_sig_fault(), force_sig_info() and the force_sig_mceerr()/force_sig_bnderr() specializations, and the rule that a fault-generated SIGSEGV or SIGBUS resets an ignored or blocked disposition rather than being dropped — the mechanism that keeps a thread from looping forever on an unfixable fault.
Delivery: the generic halfkernel/signal.c, kernel/entry/common.c, include/linux/irq-entry-common.h, arch/arm64/kernel/entry-common.c, kernel/rseq.c, include/linux/rseq.hget_signal(), the loop in dequeue_signal(), and the default-action decisions for terminate, stop, ignore and core. arch_do_signal_or_restart() is the single hook by which the return-to-user pipeline of Chapter 10 reaches this code, which is why signal delivery happens on the way out of the kernel and nowhere else. kernel/rseq.c and include/linux/rseq.h belong here too: rseq_signal_deliver() is the first statement of frame setup on both architectures, and it aborts any in-flight restartable sequence before the saved instruction pointer is redirected at the handler.
Delivery: the x86_64 framearch/x86/kernel/signal.c, arch/x86/kernel/signal_64.c, arch/x86/kernel/signal_32.c, arch/x86/include/asm/sigframe.h, arch/x86/include/asm/sighandling.h, arch/x86/kernel/fpu/signal.c, arch/x86/include/asm/fpu/signal.h, arch/x86/include/uapi/asm/sigcontext.hHow struct rt_sigframe is laid out on the user stack, the 128-byte red zone and alignment rules, where the saved pt_regs image goes, and how the FPU and XSAVE extended state is written out and validated on return — the reason the frame is variable-sized rather than a fixed structure.
Delivery: the ARM64 framearch/arm64/kernel/signal.c, arch/arm64/kernel/signal32.c, arch/arm64/include/uapi/asm/sigcontext.h, arch/arm64/kernel/fpsimd.cThe extensible record format that distinguishes ARM64 from x86_64: a chain of magic-tagged blocks (FPSIMD_MAGIC, SVE_MAGIC, ZA_MAGIC, ZT_MAGIC, ESR_MAGIC, GCS_MAGIC) terminated by EXTRA_MAGIC, which is how a signal frame can carry scalable-vector and SME state whose size is not known at compile time.
The return trampolinesarch/arm64/kernel/vdso/sigreturn.S, arch/x86/entry/vdso/vdso32/sigreturn.S, arch/x86/kernel/signal_64.c, arch/arm64/kernel/signal.cWhere the instruction that issues rt_sigreturn lives now that user stacks are non-executable, and the restore_sigcontext() validation on the way back — the point at which the kernel must assume the frame may have been tampered with.
Signal-related system callskernel/signal.c, include/linux/syscalls.h, kernel/sys.c, kernel/pid.c, include/linux/pid.hkill, tgkill and tkill and the permission checks in check_kill_permission(); rt_sigaction, rt_sigprocmask, rt_sigpending, rt_sigsuspend, rt_sigtimedwait and rt_sigqueueinfo; sigaltstack; and pidfd_send_signal(), whose whole purpose is to make the target of a signal unambiguous in the presence of PID reuse.
Real-time signals and POSIX timerskernel/signal.c, kernel/time/posix-timers.c, kernel/time/itimer.c, include/linux/posix-timers.hWhy real-time signals queue instead of collapsing, the RLIMIT_SIGPENDING accounting charged to the receiving task’s user, and the SIGQUEUE_PREALLOC node embedded in every POSIX timer at creation so that timer expiry can never fail for lack of memory.
signalfdfs/signalfd.c, include/uapi/linux/signalfd.h, include/linux/signalfd.hHow a file descriptor is made to consume from the very same private and shared pending queues that get_signal() reads, the signotset() inversion that turns a “signals I want” mask into the “signals to skip” mask dequeue_signal() expects, the flattening of the kernel siginfo union into the fixed-layout struct signalfd_siginfo, the wake_up_pollfree() teardown that stops a poll waiter outliving the sighand_struct, and why the mechanism only works if the signals are blocked first.
Coredumpfs/coredump.c, include/linux/coredump.h, fs/binfmt_elf.c, kernel/exit.c, kernel/sys.c, include/uapi/linux/coredump.h, include/linux/mm_types.hThe path from a fatal signal with a core default action through coredump_wait(), which must stop every other thread in the group and then wait for each to become inactive so its extended register state reaches memory, to the four destinations selected by core_pattern — COREDUMP_FILE, COREDUMP_PIPE, COREDUMP_SOCK and the negotiated COREDUMP_SOCK_REQ — plus the credential override that dumps a tainted process as root into a controlled destination, the MMF_DUMP* filter behind /proc/<pid>/coredump_filter that decides which regions are written, and the 0x80 bit in the group exit code that WCOREDUMP() reports.
ptrace interactionkernel/ptrace.c, kernel/signal.c, include/uapi/linux/ptrace.hptrace_signal() and the signal-delivery stop, which lets a tracer see, suppress or substitute a signal before the tracee acts on it, and the group-stop protocol that makes SIGSTOP visible to a debugger without letting it be ignored.

Chapter 12 — The Virtual Filesystem

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
The common file modelinclude/linux/fs.h, include/linux/fs/super_types.h, include/linux/dcache.h, include/linux/mount.h, include/linux/path.hWhere struct super_block, struct inode, struct file, struct dentry and struct path are declared, together with the four operation tables (super_operations, inode_operations, file_operations, dentry_operations) that every filesystem fills in. Reading these headers first is the fastest way to see which of the four objects owns a given piece of state, because the rest of fs/ is largely a set of dispatchers into these tables. Note that struct super_block and super_operations now live in include/linux/fs/super_types.h rather than in include/linux/fs.h.
The superblock and mount-time tree constructionfs/super.c, fs/fs_context.c, include/linux/fs_context.hvfs_get_tree() and the get_tree_bdev()/get_tree_nodev()/get_tree_single()/get_tree_keyed() family, which is how a filesystem declares whether its superblock is backed by a block device, is unique per mount, is shared by every mount, or is keyed on some other identity. fs/fs_context.c holds the parameter-parsing state machine that replaced the old option string.
The inodefs/inode.c, fs/attr.c, fs/namei.cInode allocation, the inode hash and LRU, and the timestamp helpers inode_set_ctime(), inode_get_mtime() and touch_atime() that exist because the timestamps are no longer plain fields. inode_permission() in fs/namei.c is the single gate every lookup and open passes through, and fs/attr.c holds setattr_prepare(), the counterpart for changing metadata.
The file object and its reference countfs/file_table.c, include/linux/file_ref.h, include/linux/fs.halloc_empty_file(), get_file(), fput(), and the deferred-release path __fput_deferred()/____fput() that exists because the final fput() can happen in contexts where a filesystem may not sleep. file_ref_t is the reason the count no longer reads as a plain atomic_long_t: it biases the counter so that the last-reference transition is a single test rather than a compare-and-swap loop.
The dentry cachefs/dcache.c, include/linux/dcache.h, include/linux/lockref.h, lib/lockref.cd_lookup(), __d_lookup() and the lockless __d_lookup_rcu(), plus d_alloc_parallel(), which is how two threads that miss on the same name are prevented from both calling into the filesystem. The lockref files are essential rather than incidental: fast_dput() and lockref_get_not_dead() are what let a reference be taken and dropped without acquiring d_lock, and they are the mechanism that makes RCU-walk finish at all.
Negative dentries and reclaimfs/dcache.c, include/linux/list_lru.hd_add() and d_instantiate(), the distinction between a dentry that names nothing and one that has no dentry, and the list_lru-based shrinker that decides how many negative entries a system is willing to keep. This is where a cache that speeds up failed lookups becomes a memory-consumption question.
Per-process file descriptorsfs/file.c, include/linux/fdtable.h, include/linux/file.hfiles_struct, the RCU-published fdtable, and the descriptor-allocation path get_unused_fd_flags() → fd_install() with put_unused_fd() as its rollback. include/linux/file.h holds struct fd and the fdget()/fdput() pair whose whole purpose is to avoid touching the reference count when the descriptor table cannot change underneath the caller.
Filesystem registrationfs/filesystems.c, include/linux/fs.hregister_filesystem(), get_fs_type(), and the file_system_type list behind /proc/filesystems. Short and worth reading in full, because it shows that a filesystem’s only obligation to the VFS at registration time is an init_fs_context method.
Mount option schemasfs/fs_parser.c, include/linux/fs_parser.hThe fs_parameter_spec tables and the fsparam_* macros that build them, plus fs_validate_description(), which rejects a duplicate option name when the filesystem registers rather than when a mount first trips over it. Worth reading for the value types that have no textual form at all — fsparam_fd, fsparam_blob, fsparam_path — since they are the clearest evidence that the mount API change was not cosmetic.
Special filesystemsfs/libfs.c, mm/shmem.c, fs/proc/root.c, fs/sysfs/mount.c, drivers/base/devtmpfs.cfs/libfs.c supplies the generic implementations that a filesystem with no backing store can adopt wholesale; the others are worked examples of it. They also demonstrate kern_mount(), the internal mount that exists so kernel code can hold files on a filesystem that user space may never see.
seq_file and procfsfs/seq_file.c, include/linux/seq_file.h, fs/proc/generic.c, fs/proc/inode.c, fs/proc/internal.h, fs/proc/base.cseq_read_iter() and its grow-and-restart buffer, single_open() and the DEFINE_SHOW_ATTRIBUTE() helpers, proc_create_single_data() and the proc_dir_entry rb-tree, the in_use drain in proc_entry_rundown(), and the per-PID directory built on demand from tgid_base_stuff
Sysctlfs/proc/proc_sysctl.c, kernel/sysctl.c, include/linux/sysctl.hregister_sysctl() with const ctl_table arrays, proc_sys_call_handler() copying into a kernel buffer and running the cgroup BPF hook, proc_dointvec_minmax() with the SYSCTL_ZERO-style bounds, and do_sysctl_args() for sysctl.*= on the command line
debugfs, tracefs, and kernfsfs/debugfs/inode.c, fs/debugfs/file.c, fs/tracefs/inode.c, fs/tracefs/event_inode.c, fs/kernfs/dir.cdebugfs_create_file() and the full-proxy fops that let a removed file fail cleanly, the debugfs= boot gate, tracefs instances and the just-in-time eventfs tree, and kernfs as the separate backing store behind sysfs and cgroupfs
Mounts and the mount treefs/namespace.c, fs/mount.h, fs/pnode.c, include/linux/mount.hThe split between struct mount, which is private to fs/, and struct vfsmount, which filesystems may see — real_mount() in fs/mount.h converts between them. path_mount() and do_new_mount() are the classic path; fs/pnode.c holds the propagation logic (shared, slave, private, unbindable) that makes one mount event replicate across peer groups.
The new mount APIfs/fsopen.c, fs/namespace.c, include/linux/fs_context.hfsopen(), fsconfig(), fsmount(), move_mount(), open_tree() and mount_setattr(). The point of reading these together is that they decompose the single old mount() syscall into separate configure, instantiate, and attach steps, each of which can fail with a specific error and can be performed by a different privilege holder.
Mount namespacesfs/namespace.c, kernel/nsproxy.c, fs/nsfs.ccopy_mnt_ns() and the setns() path, plus the nsfs descriptors behind /proc/<pid>/ns/mnt that let a namespace be referred to by an open file rather than by a PID.
Idmapped mountsfs/mnt_idmapping.c, include/linux/mnt_idmapping.hstruct mnt_idmap and the i_uid_into_vfsuid()/vfsuid_eq_kuid() conversions. This is a comparatively recent mechanism and the reason so many VFS functions now take a struct mnt_idmap * as their first argument: ownership is a property of the path used to reach a file, not of the inode alone.
Pathname lookupfs/namei.c, include/linux/namei.hfilename_lookup(), path_init(), link_path_walk(), walk_component(), lookup_fast() and step_into(). The single most important thing to trace here is try_to_unlazy(), the function that converts a speculative RCU-walk into a reference-holding ref-walk, because every RCU-walk optimization in the file is contingent on that conversion being able to succeed.
Symbolic links and openat2fs/namei.c, include/uapi/linux/openat2.hpick_link() and reserve_stack(), which are where the nesting and total-symlink limits are enforced, and the RESOLVE_* flags that let openat2() express constraints — no symlinks, no crossing a mount, stay beneath this directory — that no combination of older syscalls could express safely.
Opening a filefs/open.c, fs/namei.cdo_sys_openat2(), build_open_flags(), path_openat(), do_open(), finish_open() and vfs_open(). Worth following end to end once, because it is the clearest demonstration of how descriptor allocation, pathname resolution, permission checking, and file instantiation are kept as separately failing steps.
Reading and writingfs/read_write.c, lib/iov_iter.c, include/linux/uio.hvfs_read()/vfs_write(), the rw_verify_area() checks, and the new_sync_read() path that calls filp->f_op->read_iter() by which a plain read() is turned into an iterator operation. iov_iter is the abstraction that lets one filesystem method serve read, readv, preadv2, direct I/O, and splice without distinguishing between them.
Zero-copy transferfs/read_write.c, fs/splice.ccopy_file_range(), sendfile() and splice() — three interfaces that all try to avoid a round trip through a user buffer, and that differ mainly in whether the filesystem is allowed to perform the copy itself.
Closing a filefs/file.c, fs/open.c, fs/file_table.cclose_fd(), file_close_fd_locked() and filp_close(), and why removing a descriptor from the table is deliberately separated from releasing the struct file it referred to — other descriptors, memory mappings, or in-flight I/O may still hold references.
Extended attributesfs/xattr.c, include/uapi/linux/xattr.h, include/linux/xattr.h, fs/posix_acl.cxattr_permission() and its per-namespace policy, path_setxattrat() behind both the classic and the 6.13 *xattrat calls, struct xattr_handler dispatch, simple_xattrs for in-memory filesystems, and vfs_set_acl() taking ACLs away from the xattr handlers
fsnotify, inotify, and fanotifyfs/notify/fsnotify.c, fs/notify/mark.c, fs/notify/notification.c, include/linux/fsnotify.h, include/linux/fsnotify_backend.h, fs/notify/inotify/inotify_user.c, fs/notify/fanotify/fanotify_user.c, fs/notify/fanotify/fanotify.c, fs/notify/dnotify/dnotify.cthe fsnotify_*() hooks and the fsnotify_sb_has_watchers() fast path, groups, marks, and connectors on inodes, mounts, superblocks, and mount namespaces, the inotify limits, fanotify event merging, and permission events waiting on fanotify_response
File lockingfs/locks.c, include/linux/filelock.hlocks_get_lock_context() and the lazily allocated file_lock_context that keeps the three lock kinds in separate lists, posix_lock_file() with the cycle detection in posix_locks_deadlock(), flock_lock_inode(), leases, and open file description locks. The reason all four live in one file is that they share an inode’s lock context while differing in exactly one respect: what owns the lock — a fact made concrete by posix_same_owner(), which is a single pointer comparison on flc_owner. Two entry points are easy to miss because they live elsewhere: locks_remove_posix() is called from filp_flush() in fs/open.c on every close, and break_lease() is called from do_dentry_open() and do_truncate(), which is the only way an ordinary open() ever reaches the lease code. try_break_deleg()/break_deleg_wait() in include/linux/filelock.h are worth reading for the drop-locks-and-retry pattern that unlink and rename use.

Chapter 13 — I/O Architecture and Device Drivers

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
I/O ports and I/O memory as resourceskernel/resource.c, include/linux/ioport.hstruct resource and the request_region()/request_mem_region() pair, plus __request_region() and insert_resource() underneath them. Read this before any driver: it shows that /proc/ioports and /proc/iomem are simply two roots of one tree, and that claiming a range is an advisory registration in a sorted tree rather than anything the hardware enforces. devm_request_mem_region() and devm_ioremap_resource() are the managed forms nearly every modern driver actually calls.
Port and MMIO accessorsarch/x86/include/asm/io.h, arch/arm64/include/asm/io.h, include/asm-generic/io.h, lib/iomap.cinb()/outb() next to readl()/writel() in both architectures at once, which is the fastest way to see that ARM64 has no port-I/O instructions at all and synthesizes inb() as a load from a fixed window. The two headers also differ in exactly the way that matters for correctness: the barriers. Compare x86—s accessors with ARM64—s __iormb()/__iowmb() to see why readl_relaxed() exists and what it gives up. lib/iomap.c holds ioread32()/iowrite32(), the port-or-MMIO-agnostic form.
Polling a register and managed resourcesinclude/linux/iopoll.h, lib/devres.c, drivers/base/devres.c, drivers/pci/devres.creadl_poll_timeout() and readl_poll_timeout_atomic(), the macros that replaced hand-written spin loops around a status register. drivers/base/devres.c is the general mechanism behind every devm_ prefix in the chapter: a per-device list of release callbacks unwound in reverse on unbind, which is why a modern probe function can return an error at any point without leaking. Reading devres_alloc() and devres_release_all() once makes every managed API in the tree self-explanatory.
kobjects, ksets, and sysfslib/kobject.c, include/linux/kobject.h, fs/sysfs/, fs/kernfs/kobject_init_and_add(), kobject_get()/kobject_put(), and kobject_uevent(). The essential point is visible only by reading kobj_type and sysfs_ops together: a kobject is a refcounted node whose type supplies the show/store dispatch, so sysfs attributes are per-type rather than per-object. fs/kernfs/ is the generic in-memory filesystem that sysfs, cgroupfs, and others are all built on; fs/sysfs/ itself is thin by comparison.
device, device_driver, and bus_typedrivers/base/core.c, drivers/base/bus.c, drivers/base/driver.c, include/linux/device.hdevice_register()/device_add(), driver_register(), and bus_register(). drivers/base/core.c is large but the parts that matter are the parent/child links that build the sysfs device tree and the device links (device_link_add()) that express a supplier/consumer dependency the parent relationship cannot. Read bus_type in include/linux/device/bus.h alongside these: match and probe are the only two hooks that make the driver model work, and everything else is bookkeeping.
Probing, matching, and deferred probedrivers/base/dd.cdriver_probe_device(), really_probe(), __device_attach() and __driver_attach() — the two directions from which a bind can be attempted, since either a new device or a new driver can trigger the search. The file’s most instructive content is the deferred-probe machinery: driver_deferred_probe_trigger(), the pending list, and driver_deferred_probe_check_state(). It exists because probe order on a non-discoverable bus is essentially arbitrary, so -EPROBE_DEFER converts an ordering problem into a retry loop that terminates once the last initcall has run.
Device classesdrivers/base/class.c, include/linux/device/class.hclass_register(), device_create() and device_create_with_groups(). A class is the answer to a question the bus hierarchy cannot answer — "what are all the input devices?" — so /sys/class/ is a second, functional view over the same devices that /sys/devices/ holds physically. devnode and dev_uevent in the class are what let a driver control the name and permissions of the node udev eventually creates.
The device treedrivers/of/base.c, drivers/of/fdt.c, drivers/of/address.c, drivers/of/platform.c, include/linux/of.hof_property_read_u32(), of_parse_phandle(), of_match_node(), and of_address_to_resource(), which is where reg and the ranges chain above it become a struct resource a driver can map. unflatten_device_tree() in drivers/of/fdt.c turns the flattened blob the bootloader supplied into the live device_node graph, and of_platform_populate() is what walks that graph creating platform devices. drivers/of/overlay.c and scripts/dtc/ cover runtime overlays and the compiler.
ACPI enumerationdrivers/acpi/scan.c, drivers/acpi/property.c, drivers/acpi/arm64/acpi_bus_scan() and acpi_device_add(), the ACPI counterpart of of_platform_populate(). Read drivers/acpi/property.c next to drivers/of/property.c: the fwnode abstraction and the device_property_read_u32() family exist precisely so that a driver can be written once and enumerated either way, which is why the same driver binds on an ARM64 device-tree platform and an x86_64 ACPI one. drivers/acpi/arm64/ is evidence that ACPI is not an x86-only mechanism.
Platform, auxiliary, and faux devicesdrivers/base/platform.c, include/linux/platform_device.h, drivers/base/auxiliary.c, drivers/base/faux.cplatform_driver_register(), platform_get_resource(), platform_get_irq() and devm_platform_ioremap_resource() — the whole interface a non-discoverable driver needs. The other two files are worth knowing because they are often mistaken for the platform bus: the auxiliary bus splits one physical device into several functional drivers that must bind independently (a network card exposing RDMA and Ethernet separately), while the faux bus exists for pseudo-devices that need a struct device but have no bus at all, and is the modern replacement for abusing a platform device to get one.
Device numbers and device special filesinclude/linux/kdev_t.h, fs/char_dev.c, fs/inode.c, fs/namei.cMAJOR(), MINOR() and MKDEV(), and the 32-bit dev_t encoding (12-bit major, 20-bit minor) that replaced the 16-bit one. init_special_inode() in fs/inode.c is the single function that connects the VFS to this chapter: it is what points a device inode’s i_fop at def_chr_fops (or def_blk_fops), so that opening a node under /dev reaches a driver at all. chrdev_open() in fs/char_dev.c then performs the substitution of the real file_operations.
Registering a character devicefs/char_dev.c, include/linux/cdev.h, drivers/char/misc.calloc_chrdev_region(), register_chrdev_region(), cdev_init(), cdev_add() and the combined cdev_device_add(). Trace chrdev_open() in full once: the kobj_lookup() into the major/minor map, the swap of filp->f_op, and the call to the driver’s own open are the entire mechanism, and they explain why cdev_add() must be the last step of initialization. drivers/char/misc.c is the shortcut for a driver that needs exactly one minor: misc_register() hands out a minor from major 10 and creates the node for you.
Requesting and handling interruptskernel/irq/manage.c, include/linux/interrupt.h, kernel/irq/handle.crequest_irq(), request_threaded_irq() and devm_request_threaded_irq(), plus the IRQF_SHARED/IRQF_ONESHOT flags and what each obliges the handler to do. __handle_irq_event_percpu() in kernel/irq/handle.c is the loop that walks a shared line’s handler list and is the reason a handler on a shared IRQ must return IRQ_NONE when the device was not the source. Read irq_thread() in kernel/irq/manage.c for the threaded model that lets the bulk of handling run in a schedulable context.
IRQ domains and irqchipskernel/irq/irqdomain.c, kernel/irq/chip.c, include/linux/irqdomain.h, drivers/irqchip/irq_domain_create_linear() (the older irq_domain_add_linear() survives as a wrapper), irq_create_mapping() and irq_find_mapping(), which are what make a Linux IRQ number a purely software token rather than a hardware line number. struct irq_chip in kernel/irq/chip.c is the mask/unmask/ack interface every controller implements; drivers/irqchip/irq-gic-v3.c is the ARM64 reference implementation and the clearest example of a hierarchical domain, where one interrupt is described by a stack of domains from the device up to the root controller.
MSI and MSI-Xkernel/irq/msi.c, drivers/pci/msi/, include/linux/msi.hpci_alloc_irq_vectors() and pci_irq_vector(), the two calls that are all most PCI drivers need, and the msi_domain machinery beneath them. The conceptual payoff of reading these is that an MSI is not a wire but a memory write the device performs at an address the interrupt controller decodes, which is why MSIs cannot be shared, are inherently ordered after the DMA that preceded them, and can be steered per-vector to a chosen CPU. pci_alloc_irq_vectors_affinity() is what a multi-queue driver uses to align vectors with CPUs.
Deferred work: softirqs, tasklets, and workqueueskernel/softirq.c, kernel/workqueue.c, include/linux/workqueue.h, lib/irq_poll.cThe bottom-half mechanisms an interrupt handler defers to, and the reason there is more than one. __do_softirq() shows the fixed vector table and the ksoftirqd fallback that bounds how long softirq processing may monopolize a CPU. kernel/workqueue.c is where work may sleep; read the concurrency-managed worker pools and alloc_workqueue()’s flags rather than the whole file. lib/irq_poll.c is the NAPI-style polling library for devices whose completion rate would otherwise cause an interrupt storm.
The DMA mapping APIkernel/dma/mapping.c, include/linux/dma-mapping.h, kernel/dma/direct.cdma_map_single(), dma_unmap_single(), dma_map_sg(), dma_alloc_coherent() and the dma_sync_single_for_cpu()/_for_device() pair. Read dma_set_mask_and_coherent() first, because everything else is contingent on the addressing limit a device declares. The essential idea to extract is that a dma_addr_t is the address the device uses and may be nothing like a physical address — it may be an IOMMU-translated IOVA or a bounce-buffer address — which is why a driver may never construct one itself.
Bounce buffers and contiguous allocationkernel/dma/swiotlb.c, kernel/dma/contiguous.c, kernel/dma/pool.c, mm/dmapool.cswiotlb_map() and the bounce buffer that silently rescues a transfer whose memory the device cannot reach, at the cost of a copy in each direction — the reason an unexpectedly slow driver is sometimes a DMA-mask bug. kernel/dma/contiguous.c holds the CMA reservation that satisfies large coherent allocations on systems with no IOMMU, and mm/dmapool.c (dma_pool_create(), dma_pool_alloc()) is the sub-page allocator for the many small, aligned descriptors a controller needs.
Scatter/gather and the IOMMUinclude/linux/scatterlist.h, lib/scatterlist.c, drivers/iommu/dma-iommu.c, drivers/iommu/iommu.cstruct scatterlist, sg_init_table(), sg_set_page() and the for_each_sg()/for_each_sg_dma_page() iterators — note carefully that after dma_map_sg() the returned count may be smaller than the count passed in, because coalescing happened, and that reading sg_dma_address() from the original count is a common bug. drivers/iommu/dma-iommu.c is where that coalescing comes from: it is the IOMMU implementation of the same dma_map_ops, and reading it shows how a scattered buffer is presented to the device as one contiguous window.
The dmaengine subsysteminclude/linux/dmaengine.h, drivers/dma/dmaengine.c, drivers/dma/dmatest.cdma_request_chan(), dmaengine_prep_slave_sg(), dmaengine_submit() and dma_async_issue_pending(). This is the API for a third-party DMA controller — a separate engine that moves data on behalf of a peripheral that cannot master the bus itself, which is the common arrangement on embedded SoCs and the opposite of the bus-mastering PCIe model. Keeping the two apart is the single most useful distinction in the chapter’s DMA material. drivers/dma/dmatest.c is a small, readable consumer.
Clocks and regulatorsdrivers/clk/clk.c, include/linux/clk.h, include/linux/clk-provider.h, drivers/regulator/core.c, include/linux/regulator/consumer.hclk_prepare(), clk_enable() and clk_core_set_rate_nolock(), with the prepare_lock mutex and enable_lock spinlock that explain why the two steps exist. struct clk versus struct clk_core shows the per-consumer handle over a shared node, and clk_disable_unused() is the late-boot sweep behind many “worked until late init” bugs. In drivers/regulator/core.c, read _regulator_get() for the dummy-regulator fallback and regulator_check_consumers() for how several consumers’ voltage ranges are intersected inside the board constraints.
Pins, GPIOs, resets, and power domainsdrivers/base/pinctrl.c, drivers/pinctrl/core.c, drivers/gpio/gpiolib.c, drivers/reset/core.c, drivers/pmdomain/core.c, drivers/of/property.cpinctrl_bind_pins(), which really_probe() calls so that pin states are set before a driver runs. gpiod_find_and_request() and gpiod_set_value() in gpiolib.c show the logical/raw split and the can_sleep warning. __reset_control_get() and the deassert_count logic define exclusive versus shared resets. genpd_dev_pm_attach() and genpd_runtime_suspend() show how a power domain follows runtime PM, and the supplier table at the end of drivers/of/property.c is what lets fw_devlink order all of these providers before their consumers.
Firmware loadingdrivers/base/firmware_loader/main.c, drivers/base/firmware_loader/fallback.c, include/linux/firmware.h_request_firmware() and fw_get_filesystem_firmware(): the built-in, cache, direct-file, compressed, and platform lookups in order, the fw_path[] search list, and the read with kernel credentials in the initial namespace. fallback.c holds the optional sysfs fallback and the fw_run_sysfs_fallback() test that decides whether it runs; fw_pm_notify() in main.c is the firmware cache that makes reloading on resume safe.
The tty layer and serial coredrivers/tty/tty_io.c, drivers/tty/n_tty.c, drivers/tty/tty_buffer.c, drivers/tty/pty.c, drivers/tty/serial/serial_core.c, drivers/tty/serdev/tty_fops and tty_open() show the tty core owning every node’s file_operations. flush_to_ldisc() in tty_buffer.c is the workqueue hand-off from the flip buffer to the line discipline, and n_tty_receive_buf_common() and isig() in n_tty.c are canonical mode and job-control signals. pty_write() is a few lines that explain pseudo-terminals. uart_write() and uart_add_one_port() show what the serial core does for every UART driver.
The input subsystemdrivers/input/input.c, drivers/input/evdev.c, include/linux/input.h, include/uapi/linux/input-event-codes.h, drivers/input/keyboard/gpio_keys.cinput_event(), input_handle_event() and input_pass_values() show how events are batched until SYN_REPORT; input_attach_handler() is the device–handler matching. In evdev.c, __pass_event() is the per-client ring buffer with its SYN_DROPPED overflow rule, and evdev_connect() creates the eventN node. gpio_keys.c is a complete, production-quality input driver built on GPIO descriptors.
devtmpfs and udevdrivers/base/devtmpfs.c, lib/kobject_uevent.cdevtmpfs_create_node(), called from device_add(), which is why /dev has usable nodes before any userspace daemon has started. lib/kobject_uevent.c holds kobject_uevent_env() and the netlink broadcast that udev listens on — reading it clarifies the division of labor: the kernel creates a minimal node and announces the event, and userspace is responsible only for permissions, symlinks, and persistent names.

Chapter 14 — Block Device Drivers

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
The entry point and the two submission pathsblock/blk-core.c, include/linux/blkdev.h, include/linux/bio.hsubmit_bio(), submit_bio_noacct() and __submit_bio() — the fork where a bio either enters blk_mq_submit_bio() or is handed to a stacking driver’s own submit_bio method. Read __submit_bio() first: the single BD_HAS_SUBMIT_BIO test in it is the whole distinction between a request-based driver and a bio-based one, and it explains why the two kinds of driver in this chapter share no code below this point. blk_queue_enter() and the q_usage_counter in the same file are the reference that makes disk removal and queue freezing wait for in-flight I/O.
Queue limits and block-device geometryinclude/linux/blkdev.h, block/blk-settings.cstruct queue_limits and the blk_validate_limits()/queue_limits_commit_update() pair that replaced the old per-limit blk_queue_*() setters, so a driver now proposes an entire limits structure at once and the core validates it as a unit. The accessors bdev_logical_block_size(), bdev_physical_block_size() and queue_max_segments() are what filesystems and the merge code actually call; reading them together is the fastest way to see that the kernel’s 512-byte sector_t is a software convention (SECTOR_SHIFT) and not a hardware claim.
The bio and the bvec iteratorinclude/linux/blk_types.h, include/linux/bvec.h, include/linux/bio.hstruct bio, struct bio_vec and struct bvec_iter, plus the three iteration families bio_for_each_segment(), bio_for_each_bvec() and bio_for_each_segment_all(). The point of reading the headers before any block/ file is that they make the central invariant visible: the bio_vec array is immutable once the bio is built and all progress lives in the iterator, which is what lets a split child and its parent share one array. bi_opf and the REQ_OP_*/REQ_* values in include/linux/blk_types.h are worth reading in full, since operation and modifier flags are packed into one field.
Allocating, splitting and chaining biosblock/bio.c, block/blk-merge.cbio_alloc_bioset() and the bio_set mempool that lets block I/O proceed during the memory pressure that triggered it, the per-CPU allocation cache behind BIOSET_PERCPU_CACHE/REQ_ALLOC_CACHE, and bio_endio(). bio_split(), bio_chain() and bio_chain_endio() are the mechanism for completing one logical transfer only after every fragment finishes; __bio_split_to_limits() in block/blk-merge.c is where the queue limits are actually enforced. Read bio_chain_endio() to see that __bi_remaining counts unfinished children and is a different thing from the __bi_cnt reference count.
gendisk, block_device and partitionsblock/genhd.c, block/partitions/core.c, include/linux/blkdev.h, include/linux/part_stat.h__blk_mq_alloc_disk(), add_disk() (now __must_check), del_gendisk() and put_disk(), together with bdev_disk_changed() and add_partition(), which are what populate a disk’s part_tbl. blk_partition_remap() in block/blk-core.c is the one function that makes the partition abstraction work — it adds bd_start_sect so the driver only ever sees whole-disk sectors. include/linux/part_stat.h holds the per-partition counters behind /proc/diskstats.
The blk-mq coreblock/blk-mq.c, include/linux/blk-mq.h, block/blk-mq-cpumap.cblk_mq_submit_bio() is the function to trace first: bio splitting, plug and scheduler merge attempts, request allocation, and the decision to plug, schedule, or issue directly all happen inside it. struct blk_mq_ctx (software staging queue) and struct blk_mq_hw_ctx (hardware dispatch queue) are the two-level structure, blk_mq_run_hw_queue() drives dispatch, and blk_mq_alloc_tag_set() is where the requests are preallocated one per tag with cmd_size bytes of driver scratch appended inline. block/blk-mq-cpumap.c holds the default CPU→hardware-queue mapping that map_queues overrides.
Tags and the sbitmapblock/blk-mq-tag.c, include/linux/sbitmap.h, lib/sbitmap.cblk_mq_get_tag(), blk_mq_put_tag() and blk_mq_tag_to_rq(), which is how a tag echoed back by hardware becomes a struct request again. The sbitmap files are essential rather than incidental: the scattered per-CPU word hints in sbitmap_get() are what keep many CPUs from colliding on the same cache line while claiming tags, and sbitmap_queue’s wait queues are the flow control that takes over when the pool is exhausted — the role the single queue lock used to play.
I/O schedulersblock/elevator.c, block/elevator.h, block/blk-mq-sched.c, block/mq-deadline.c, block/kyber-iosched.c, block/bfq-iosched.cstruct elevator_type and elevator_mq_ops define the interface; elevator_change() is the live-switch path behind /sys/block/<dev>/queue/scheduler, and the default-selection logic in block/elevator.c is where mq-deadline for a single-hardware-queue device and none for a multi-queue one is decided. block/blk-mq-sched.c holds the second tag pool (sched_tags), which is the structural reason a scheduler can hold more requests than the device’s queue depth. Of the three schedulers, block/mq-deadline.c is by far the shortest and the best one to read in full.
Merging and pluggingblock/blk-merge.c, block/blk-mq.cblk_attempt_plug_merge(), bio_attempt_back_merge()/bio_attempt_front_merge() and blk_rq_merge_ok(), plus blk_start_plug()/blk_finish_plug() and blk_mq_flush_plug_list(). Worth reading as a pair with the limits code, because every merge decision is a check against max_sectors, max_segments and max_segment_size — the layer continually splits transfers down to what the hardware accepts while merging them back up to the largest efficient unit. blk_rq_map_sg() is the counterpart that turns the finished request into the scatter/gather list a driver hands to DMA.
I/O prioritiesblock/ioprio.c, include/uapi/linux/ioprio.h, include/linux/ioprio.h, block/blk-ioprio.cThe ioprio_set() and ioprio_get() system calls, and ioprio_check_cap() with its capability rules. The uapi header shows the class, hint, and level fields, and __get_task_ioprio() shows how an unset priority is derived from the CPU scheduler's policy and nice value. bio_set_ioprio() in block/blk-core.c is where every bio without a priority receives the submitter's, and block/blk-ioprio.c implements the io.prio.class cgroup policies applied immediately afterward.
Throttling and cgroup I/O controlblock/blk-rq-qos.c, block/blk-wbt.c, block/blk-throttle.c, block/blk-iocost.c, block/blk-iolatency.cThe layer the chapter treats alongside the elevator but which is entirely separate from it. rq_qos_throttle() and the rq_qos chain in block/blk-rq-qos.c can delay a bio before it ever becomes a request; block/blk-wbt.c is the smallest member and the easiest to read, holding back buffered writeback when it detects that read latency is suffering. block/blk-throttle.c implements the hard caps of the cgroup file io.max, while block/blk-iocost.c and block/blk-iolatency.c implement the proportional and latency-target policies behind io.cost.qos and io.latency. These are worth reading precisely because the fastest devices run with no I/O scheduler at all: on such a device, fairness between cgroups is provided here and nowhere else.
Dispatch and completionblock/blk-mq.c, block/blk-flush.cblk_mq_start_request() (which must be the first call in queue_rq, and which records the deadline that the queue's single timeout worker later scans for), blk_mq_complete_request() with blk_mq_complete_need_ipi(), and blk_mq_end_request()/blk_update_request(). The completion-steering code is the most instructive part: it shows blk-mq spending an IPI to move completion back to the submitting CPU, which is the same per-CPU locality argument that motivated the whole design. blk_mq_poll() is the interrupt-free alternative, and block/blk-flush.c shows how REQ_PREFLUSH and REQ_FUA are decomposed into a small state machine of extra requests.
Tracing and I/O accountinginclude/trace/events/block.h, kernel/trace/blktrace.c, include/linux/part_stat.hThe instrumentation that makes the request lifecycle observable rather than theoretical. The tracepoints in include/trace/events/block.h land on exactly the stages the chapter describes — block_bio_queue, block_bio_backmerge, block_rq_insert, block_rq_issue, block_rq_complete — so reading the tracepoint definitions is a fast way to check one's mental model of the path against the code. kernel/trace/blktrace.c is what blktrace and blkparse sit on. The per-request timestamps start_time_ns and io_start_time_ns, accounted through include/linux/part_stat.h, are what separate queueing delay from device service time in /proc/diskstats and iostat.
Data integrity (T10 PI)include/linux/blk-integrity.h, include/linux/bio-integrity.h, include/linux/t10-pi.h, block/bio-integrity-auto.c, block/bio-integrity.c, block/blk-integrity.c, block/t10-pi.cbio-integrity-auto.c is the whole automatic path (split out in 6.15): bio_integrity_prep() after the split, and the kintegrityd workqueue that verifies reads. block/t10-pi.c holds the guard and reference-tag code for every format, including blk_integrity_prepare() and blk_integrity_complete(), which remap reference tags. block/blk-integrity.c has the sysfs files and blk_integrity_profile_name(). For the driver side, read nvme_init_integrity() in drivers/nvme/host/core.c.
Inline encryptioninclude/linux/blk-crypto.h, include/linux/blk-crypto-profile.h, block/blk-crypto.c, block/blk-crypto-profile.c, block/blk-crypto-fallback.cblk_crypto_init_key() and bio_crypt_set_ctx() are the filesystem's interface. blk_crypto_submit_bio() is the 7.0 entry point that chooses between the hardware and the fallback. blk_crypto_get_keyslot() manages keyslots in block/blk-crypto-profile.c and refuses to register a profile on a queue that supports integrity. fs/crypto/inline_crypt.c is the reference consumer, and drivers/ufs/core/ufshcd-crypto.c with drivers/soc/qcom/ice.c is the hardware side most ARM64 systems use.
Mapping a request for DMAinclude/linux/blk-mq-dma.h, block/blk-mq-dma.c, include/linux/blk-mq.hThe last step before the hardware, which the chapter's driver skeleton leaves to a comment and the integrity section explains. blk_rq_map_sg() is the classic form, filling a scatterlist whose length is bounded by the max_segments the driver declared. blk_rq_dma_map_iter_start() and blk_rq_dma_map_iter_next() are the newer alternative (6.17) that walks and maps in a single pass without an intermediate array, with blk_rq_dma_map_coalesce() reporting when an IOMMU folded the whole request into one range, blk_rq_dma_unmap() undoing it, and blk_rq_integrity_dma_map_iter_start() doing the same for metadata; drivers/nvme/host/pci.c is currently the driver to read for it. Worth reading next to struct queue_limits, since every segment limit exists to bound exactly this step.
Reference driver implementationsdrivers/block/null_blk/main.c, drivers/block/virtio_blk.c, drivers/block/loop.c, drivers/nvme/host/pci.c, drivers/scsi/scsi_lib.cdrivers/block/null_blk/main.c is the canonical minimal blk-mq driver and the right place to start; drivers/block/virtio_blk.c is the smallest complete real one. drivers/nvme/host/pci.c is where the multi-queue design pays off — one hardware queue per CPU, and the poll_queues parameter that removes interrupts entirely. drivers/scsi/scsi_lib.c is worth knowing about even if never read in full, because it implements queue_rq once on behalf of every SCSI, SAS and SATA disk, which is why hundreds of host-adapter drivers never touch blk-mq directly. Two more are worth knowing: drivers/nvme/host/core.c holds the command set, namespace and multipath logic that nvme-pci, nvme-tcp, nvme-rdma and nvme-fc all share, so the transport files are thinner than they look; and drivers/block/ublk_drv.c forwards every request to a userspace daemon over io_uring, which is the modern answer to writing a virtual block device without writing kernel code. A Rust implementation of null_blk lives in drivers/block/rnull/rnull.rs for readers who want to see the same interface expressed through the kernel’s Rust abstractions.
Stacking (bio-based) driversdrivers/md/dm.c, drivers/md/md.cThe counter-examples to everything above: these allocate their disk with blk_alloc_disk() and supply a submit_bio method, so no struct request, tag, or scheduler is involved. dm_submit_bio() is the clearest illustration of the transform-and-resubmit pattern — retarget bi_bdev, adjust bi_iter.bi_sector, split or clone as the target requires, and call submit_bio_noacct() — and it is what makes a single logical write cross submit_bio() more than once.
Opening a block device fileblock/fops.c, block/bdev.c, block/ioctl.cdef_blk_fops, blkdev_open(), blkdev_read_iter()/blkdev_write_iter() and the blkdev_iomap_begin() buffered path alongside blkdev_direct_IO(). block/bdev.c holds blkdev_get_no_open(), bdev_file_open_by_dev(), bd_prepare_to_claim() and the blockdev_superblock pseudo-filesystem that gives every disk and partition one canonical block_device and backing inode — the change from 2.6, where the block_device hung off the device-file inode. Read bd_prepare_to_claim() together with BLK_OPEN_RESTRICT_WRITES to see why a mounted filesystem can now block outside writers without blocking readers. Finish with blkdev_ioctl() in block/ioctl.c for the query and action commands a device file answers, blkdev_fallocate() and blkdev_uring_cmd() for the newer discard and write-zeroes entry points, and bdev_release() for the close path that syncs, yields the claim, and calls the driver's release on the last opener.
Zoned devicesblock/blk-zoned.c, include/linux/blkdev.hThe reason REQ_OP_ZONE_APPEND appears in the operation list. The zone write plugging in this file is what lets the block layer preserve strict per-zone write ordering on a host-managed SMR disk or ZNS SSD even though the rest of blk-mq is deliberately unordered — a useful illustration of how an ordering constraint is added at the edge rather than by weakening the core.

Chapter 15 — The Page Cache

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
The page cache propermm/filemap.c, include/linux/pagemap.hThe single largest file in this chapter’s trail. __filemap_get_folio() is the one function to read first — every lookup, every allocation-on-miss, and every FGP_* flag passes through it. filemap_get_entry() shows the lockless RCU descent and the speculative folio_try_get() retry that makes a cache hit cost no lock at all.
