address_space and its operationsinclude/linux/fs.h, fs/inode.cstruct address_space and struct address_space_operations are both declared in fs.h; read them together, because the second is the only way the first reaches a filesystem. inode_init_always() shows an inode’s i_data being wired up with empty_aops and GFP_HIGHUSER_MOVABLE before the filesystem installs its own table.
Which mapping a file really usesblock/bdev.c, fs/char_dev.cRead blkdev_open() for the one line that matters — bdev_file->f_mapping = bdev->bd_mapping — which is how every opener of a disk lands on the same folios no matter which device node it came through. cd_forget() in char_dev.c is the counterpart, restoring i_mapping to an inode’s own i_data when a character device that had redirected it goes away.
Foliosinclude/linux/mm_types.h, include/linux/page-flags.hThe struct folio definition and, immediately after it, the FOLIO_MATCH() static assertions that force its fields to line up with struct page — the clearest proof that page_folio() is a cast and not a conversion. page-flags.h generates the paired folio_test_* / PageFoo() accessors from one macro list.
Large folios and folio orderinclude/linux/pagemap.h, mm/readahead.cmapping_set_large_folios(), mapping_set_folio_order_range(), and mapping_max_folio_order() define what orders a file may use; readahead.c is where the order is actually chosen, growing with the length of a sequential run and collapsing when access turns random.
Splitting a large folioinclude/linux/huge_mm.h, mm/huge_memory.cThe other half of the large-folio story. split_folio() shatters a folio into order-0 pieces; folio_split() is the non-uniform variant that shrinks the order only around the page that needs it. min_order_for_split() is why a mapping’s minimum folio order is a promise rather than a hint, and deferred_split_folio() is why the common case pays nothing.
The XArrayinclude/linux/xarray.h, lib/xarray.c, include/linux/radix-tree.hThe successor to the radix tree. Read the header first for xa_state, the xa_mk_value()/xa_is_value() pair, and XA_MAX_MARKS (three — exactly the number of page-cache tags); then xas_find_marked() in the C file for the hierarchical mark search that lets writeback skip whole clean subtrees. Open radix-tree.h afterwards for the two-line proof that the two are one structure: #define radix_tree_root xarray and #define radix_tree_node xa_node, under the comment “Keep unconverted code working”.
Readaheadmm/readahead.cThe file opens with a long comment that is the best available explanation of the algorithm. page_cache_sync_ra() handles the miss, page_cache_async_ra() fires off the PG_readahead marker planted in the previous window, and the two together are why a streaming reader never stalls.
The buffered read and write pathsmm/filemap.cfilemap_read() with filemap_get_pages() beneath it, and generic_perform_write() with its write_begin/write_end loop. Note the short-copy handling in the write loop and the balance_dirty_pages_ratelimited() call at the top of each iteration — the throttle reaches into the write path itself.
Faulting mapped filesmm/filemap.c, mm/memory.cfilemap_fault() is the same lookup as a read, ending in a page-table entry instead of a copy; filemap_map_pages() is fault-around, installing entries for already-cached neighbors. memory.c holds do_shared_fault() and the page_mkwrite call that turns an mmap store into a dirty folio.
Buffer headsfs/buffer.c, include/linux/buffer_head.hWhat survives of the old buffer cache. bdev_getblk() replaced __getblk(), ll_rw_block() is gone entirely, and submit_bh() is the two-line bridge from a buffer head into the block layer of Chapter 14. The per-CPU bh_lru explains why repeated metadata lookups are cheap. Read block_write_full_folio() to see the old writepage body still doing its job, now called from a filesystem’s writepages rather than by the kernel, and mark_buffer_dirty_inode() with sync_mapping_buffers() for the b_assoc_map list that makes fsync() cover an inode’s metadata blocks. Then look at fs/Makefile: the whole file is gated on CONFIG_BUFFER_HEAD.
The iomap frameworkfs/iomap/buffered-io.c, include/linux/iomap.h, fs/iomap/iter.cThe replacement for buffer heads on file data. struct iomap and its IOMAP_* types are in the header; iomap_folio_state in buffered-io.c is the two-bits-per-block array that lets one large folio track hundreds of blocks in a few words. fs/iomap/iter.c holds the iomap_iter() loop every other file in the directory is built from; read it alongside iomap_valid() and iomap->validity_cookie in the header to see how a cached extent map is revalidated and marked IOMAP_F_STALE. Contrast iomap_begin() with get_block_t in include/linux/fs.h — one call per extent against one call per block.
Marking folios dirtymm/page-writeback.c, fs/fs-writeback.cfilemap_dirty_folio() sets the folio flag and the PAGECACHE_TAG_DIRTY mark, then __mark_inode_dirty() in fs-writeback.c puts the inode on a backing device’s list. The I_DIRTY_* mask it takes is precisely the distinction fdatasync later exploits.
The flusher threadsfs/fs-writeback.c, include/linux/writeback.hwb_workfn() is the successor to pdflush. Follow it down through wb_do_writeback() → wb_writeback() → queue_io() → writeback_sb_inodes() and watch inodes move between b_dirty, b_io, and b_more_io — the three lists are the whole scheduling policy, with b_dirty_time off to the side for inodes whose only dirty state is a timestamp. Then read writeback_iter() in mm/page-writeback.c: it replaced write_cache_pages() outright, turning a callback into a for loop, and its sync-mode branch is where an integrity pass earns the right to run past nr_to_write and past errors. For the cgroup side, wbc_detach_inode() and the WB_FRN_* constants show how inode ownership is decided by a moving average of who actually writes.
Dirty limits and throttlingmm/page-writeback.cbalance_dirty_pages() is the pacing algorithm: a per-task dirty budget avoids checking the global total on every page, and wb_calc_thresh() gives each backing device a proportional share so a slow USB stick cannot stall a writer to an SSD. The sysctl defaults (20, 10, 3000, 500) are at the top of the file.
Backing devicesinclude/linux/backing-dev-defs.h, mm/backing-dev.cstruct backing_dev_info and struct bdi_writeback are declared in backing-dev-defs.h; wb_init() and cgwb_create() in the C file build the per-device (and, with cgroup writeback, per-cgroup) contexts that replaced the shared pdflush pool. Read the b_dirty/b_io/b_more_io lists in the struct alongside queue_io() above.
sync, fsync, and fdatasyncfs/sync.c, lib/errseq.cksys_sync() makes the two-pass async-then-wait structure obvious in a dozen lines, and sync_filesystem() next to it shows the same dance within one superblock. Note that SYSCALL_DEFINE0(sync) discards the result entirely while syncfs has its own s_wb_err sequence. Pair it with errseq.c: errseq_check_and_advance() is the small algorithm that guarantees every open file description sees each writeback error occurring after it was opened exactly once, closing the old lost-error hole — and filemap_sample_wb_err() at open time in fs/open.c is why “after it was opened” is the operative clause. Finish with generic_write_sync() in include/linux/fs.h, four lines that turn O_DSYNC into a range fdatasync after every write.
Truncation and invalidationmm/truncate.cThe other end of a folio’s life. truncate_inode_pages_range() shows the non-blocking first pass and the waiting second one, truncate_inode_partial_folio() the handling of the ends of a range — zero, then try to split a large folio with try_folio_split_or_unmap() so the truncated part is really freed, falling back to zero-don’t-drop only when the split fails — and invalidate_inode_pages2_range() the strict variant that may return -EBUSY.
Reclaim and shadow entriesmm/vmscan.c, mm/workingset.c__remove_mapping() is where a folio is finally detached, and the comment above it — a worked race between get_user_pages() and dirtying — explains why the refcount and dirty tests must be ordered as they are. workingset.c holds workingset_eviction() and workingset_refault(), the pair that turns an empty XArray slot into refault detection.
Dropping the cache on requestmm/fadvise.c, fs/drop_caches.cTwo short files worth reading in full. POSIX_FADV_DONTNEED deliberately spares the partial folios at each end of a range, and its lru_add_drain() / retry / lru_add_drain_all() dance is the clearest demonstration anywhere that a per-CPU LRU batch holds a reference; drop_caches is a loop over every inode calling invalidate_mapping_pages(), which is exactly why it drops only clean folios and why sync must precede it.
Asking what is cachedmm/filemap.c, include/uapi/linux/mman.h, mm/mincore.cThe cachestat() system call and filemap_cachestat() sit at the end of filemap.c. The comment inside the loop explains why the walk derives everything from the XArray (order, dirty and writeback marks, shadow values) instead of dereferencing a folio it has not pinned. The shmem branch shows how a swap entry is followed to its swap-cache shadow, and workingset_test_recent() with its flush argument shows the same refault test reused outside a fault. Read mincore_page() alongside it for the older, per-page answer.
Where the numbers come fromfs/proc/meminfo.c, mm/vmstat.cThe shortest way to check any claim in this chapter against a running system. Note in meminfo.c that Cached is computed — NR_FILE_PAGES less the swap cache less Buffers — rather than counted, which is why Cached and Buffers partition the file cache instead of overlapping.
Where the page cache endsmm/swap_state.c, mm/swap_table.hWorth reading to see a boundary move. The comment calling swapper_space “a fiction” sits directly above the global swap_space, which now exists only to keep folio->mapping paths working; the real swap index is the per-cluster flat struct swap_table, no longer an XArray.
Direct I/O, the bypassfs/iomap/direct-io.cThe path that is not the page cache, included because the contrast is instructive: iomap_dio_rw() builds bios straight from user pages, and the invalidation it performs around the transfer is the reason invalidate_inode_pages2_range() has to be the strict variant.

Chapter 16 — Accessing Files

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
The read and write entry pointsfs/read_write.cWhere a system call becomes a method call. vfs_read()/vfs_write() and the new_sync_read()/new_sync_write() shims that wrap a single user buffer in an iov_iter show that read and write are now compatibility wrappers around read_iter and write_iter, not peers of them. do_iter_read() and rw_verify_area() hold the checks every path shares, and kiocb_set_rw_flags() is the one place the RWF_* flags become IOCB_* bits — including the FOP_DONTCACHE gate that makes RWF_DONTCACHE fail cleanly on filesystems that cannot honour it.
kiocb and file_operationsinclude/linux/fs.hRead struct kiocb and struct file_operations in one sitting. The IOCB_* list next to the RWF_* definitions in include/uapi/linux/fs.h is the whole per-request flag vocabulary; ki_complete is the single field that decides whether the entire stack below behaves synchronously or returns -EIOCBQUEUED. In the operations table, note that read_iter/write_iter sit alongside splice_read, splice_write, copy_file_range, and remap_file_range — the data methods a filesystem may implement are more than two.
iov_iterinclude/linux/uio.h, lib/iov_iter.cThe abstraction the whole chapter rests on. The header defines the backing types and the iov_iter_ubuf(), iov_iter_kvec(), iov_iter_bvec(), and iov_iter_xarray() constructors; the C file holds copy_to_iter(), copy_page_to_iter(), and iov_iter_advance(). Read iterate_and_advance() to see how one macro generates a per-type loop, which is why adding a new buffer flavour does not touch any filesystem.
The buffered read and write pathsmm/filemap.cChapter 15 covered these as cache operations; read them again here as the bodies of read_iter and write_iter. generic_file_read_iter() is the fork in the road, dispatching on IOCB_DIRECT before filemap_read() is ever reached. filemap_get_pages() is where IOCB_NOWAIT, IOCB_NOIO, and IOCB_DONTCACHE each change behaviour in a single line, and __filemap_get_folio()’s __folio_set_dropbehind() call is all there is to uncached buffered I/O.
Write-through and O_SYNCinclude/linux/fs.h, fs/sync.cgeneric_write_sync() is four lines and settles the question of what O_SYNC costs: it calls vfs_fsync_range() on the bytes just written, after they have gone into the page cache. That ordering is the difference between write-through and write-around, and it is the reason O_SYNC is not a way to avoid the cache.
Read-aheadmm/readahead.c, include/linux/pagemap.hThe long comment at the top of the C file is the algorithm’s own documentation. page_cache_sync_ra() and page_cache_async_ra() are the miss and hit paths; get_init_ra_size() and get_next_ra_size() are the ramp; page_cache_ra_order() is where a sequential run is recognised and rewarded with a larger folio order. struct file_ra_state and struct readahead_control in pagemap.h replaced the 2.6 pair of windows with one state plus a per-call control block, and the dropbehind field on the control is how RWF_DONTCACHE reaches read-ahead.
VMAs and the maple treeinclude/linux/mm_types.h, mm/vma.c, lib/maple_tree.cstruct vm_area_struct no longer has vm_next or an rbtree node; the replacement is mm->mm_mt, walked with VMA_ITERATOR and for_each_vma(). mm/vma.c holds the split, merge, and unmap logic that used to live in mm/mmap.c; read vma_merge_new_range() and vms_complete_munmap_vmas() there. The per-VMA lock is why a page fault usually does not touch mmap_lock at all: vm_lock_seq lives in the VMA in mm_types.h, and lock_vma_under_rcu() in mm/mmap_lock.c is the lookup that takes it.
Creating a mappingmm/mmap.c, mm/util.cdo_mmap() down through mmap_region() is the whole of mmap(): flags become vm_flags, a VMA is allocated and inserted, and the file’s mmap (or the newer mmap_prepare) method installs vm_ops. Nothing is read. That is the point — the mapping is a promise, and section 5.3 of the chapter is about how it is kept.
Faulting a mapped filemm/memory.c, mm/filemap.chandle_mm_fault() and do_fault() in memory.c, ending in generic_file_vm_ops’s filemap_fault() and filemap_map_pages(). Read them next to filemap_read(): the lookup is identical and only the last step differs, a page-table entry instead of a copy. do_shared_fault() and the page_mkwrite call are where a store to a mapping becomes a dirty folio the flushers will find.
Direct I/Ofs/iomap/direct-io.c, fs/direct-io.ciomap_dio_rw() is the modern path: it invalidates the overlapping cache range, pins the user pages, builds bios directly on them, and completes either inline or from a workqueue through iomap_dio_complete(). fs/direct-io.c with __blockdev_direct_IO() is the 2.6-era implementation, still present for the filesystems that have not converted — worth opening once purely to see how much per-block bookkeeping iomap removed. In iomap/direct-io.c the two functions that repay close reading are iomap_dio_iter(), whose switch shows the cases in which direct I/O is not a DMA at all (a hole is zeroed, inline data is copied, a delalloc extent is a collision with a buffered writer and returns -EIO), and iomap_dio_complete(), whose comment explains why the cache invalidation must follow ->end_io() rather than precede it. The flag definitions in include/linux/iomap.h are the other half of the story: IOMAP_DIO_OVERWRITE_ONLY, IOMAP_DIO_PARTIAL, and IOMAP_DIO_BOUNCE each carry a comment that is shorter and clearer than any summary of it.
Alignment and atomicity limitsfs/stat.c, include/uapi/linux/stat.hSTATX_DIOALIGN and the stx_dio_mem_align/stx_dio_offset_align fields, filled in by each filesystem’s getattr. This is the answer to a question that was folklore for twenty years: a program no longer has to guess what alignment O_DIRECT demands, it can ask — and the two fields being separate is itself the correction, since the buffer and the file offset are constrained by different things. STATX_WRITE_ATOMIC and the stx_atomic_write_unit_min/_max fields alongside them do the same job for RWF_ATOMIC; compare how fs/ext4/inode.c and fs/xfs/xfs_iops.c fill all of these in, because the values differ in ways that matter.
splice and the pipefs/splice.c, include/linux/splice.h, mm/filemap.cRead filemap_splice_read() in filemap.c directly against filemap_read() above it — same filemap_get_pages() loop, same i_size recheck, same folio_mark_accessed(), with splice_folio_into_pipe() where the copy used to be. Then read iter_file_splice_write() in splice.c, which builds a bio_vec array over the pipe’s buffers and calls write_iter: the write half of splice is not a special path at all. do_splice_read() is where O_DIRECT and DAX fall back to copy_splice_read(), the case in which splice is not zero-copy. Do not stop before splice_to_socket(), though: sockets install it as their own splice_write, so the classic file-to-socket transfer never reaches iter_file_splice_write() at all. It builds a sixteen-entry bio_vec array and calls sock_sendmsg() with MSG_SPLICE_PAGES, the flag that tells the transport to attach pages to an sk_buff by reference. Its automatic use of MSG_MORE is what makes the otherwise puzzling splice_eof method necessary — follow it to tcp_splice_eof(), which is four lines and exists only to flush the segment that MSG_MORE held back.
sendfile and copy_file_rangefs/read_write.c, fs/splice.cdo_sendfile() is a dozen meaningful lines: if the output is a pipe it calls splice_file_to_pipe(), otherwise do_splice_direct() with an internal pipe. vfs_copy_file_range() is the more interesting one — its preference order is the output file’s copy_file_range (server-side copy), then the input file’s remap_file_range on the same superblock (reflink), then a fallback splice. The comment explaining why the fallback exists is worth reading in full: it is a deliberate promise to userspace that the call always works. Read generic_copy_file_checks() immediately afterwards to see the limits of that promise — a copy between different superblocks is refused with -EXDEV unless both files reach the same copy_file_range implementation, which is the exception that lets NFS and SMB copy between two of their own mounts.
Legacy asynchronous I/Ofs/aio.cio_submit_one() and aio_read()/aio_write() show the whole mechanism: set ki_complete, call read_iter, and treat -EIOCBQUEUED as success. aio_complete() posts the event ring entry. Read it before io_uring, because io_uring does the same thing with a different transport. Two details in aio_prep_rw() are worth pausing on: the IOCB_FLAG_RESFD handling, which routes completions to an eventfd and is the only way to fold AIO into an epoll loop, and the line that clears IOCB_HIPRI because nobody is left in a position to poll for the I/O.
io_uringio_uring/io_uring.c, io_uring/rw.c, include/uapi/linux/io_uring.hThe header is the interface: io_uring_sqe, io_uring_cqe, the opcode list, and the IORING_SETUP_* flags including SQPOLL and SQE128. rw.c is the part that matters for this chapter — io_read() and io_write() build a kiocb and an iov_iter and call the same read_iter/write_iter a plain read() would. Registered buffers are the one genuinely new capability: io_sqe_buffers_register() in io_uring/rsrc.c pins a set of user pages once, and every later READ_FIXED/WRITE_FIXED request reuses them as an ITER_BVEC iterator instead of pinning again. The other function to read in rw.c is io_rw_should_retry(), whose comment is the clearest description anywhere of how a buffered read is made asynchronous without a thread: it arms a wait_page_queue on the folio, swaps IOCB_NOWAIT for IOCB_WAITQ, and lets __folio_lock_async() in mm/filemap.c return -EIOCBQUEUED. io-wq is the fallback, not the mechanism. For the modern shape of the interface, read the IORING_SETUP_* and IORING_CQE_F_* comments in the uapi header alongside io_uring/kbuf.c — multishot requests and provided buffer rings only make sense together.

Chapter 17 — Page Frame Reclaiming

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
The LRU lists and the lruvecinclude/linux/mmzone.h, mm/swap.cRead enum lru_list and struct lruvec together: the five lists, anon_cost/file_cost, and nonresident_age are the whole of the classic ager’s state. Then read struct cpu_fbatches in mm/swap.c and count the batches — six, not one — and notice that lru_move_tail sits behind a second, interrupt-disabling lock because it alone runs from a block-layer completion. That split is the clearest statement anywhere that a folio’s flags are the truth and the lists merely catch up.
Aging and the second chancemm/swap.c, mm/vmscan.cfolio_mark_accessed() is shorter than its reputation: four transitions and two early exits. Note that it returns immediately on PG_dropbehind, which is a more decisive answer to cache pollution than the second-chance rule itself. In vmscan.c, inactive_is_low() computes its target as int_sqrt(10 * gb) — work the value out for a few sizes and the shrinking inactive fraction stops looking arbitrary. prepare_scan_control() is where you discover that the active lists are often not scanned at all.
Balancing anonymous and file reclaimmm/vmscan.cget_scan_count() and calculate_pressure_balance(). The thing to check is that MAX_SWAPPINESS is 200, not 100, so the two sides balance at 100 and the default of 60 is already a strong bias toward file reclaim. The four SCAN_* modes are tested in a fixed order; read the file_is_tiny comment, which names the runaway feedback loop the check exists to break and explains why it deliberately consults raw sizes instead of the cost machinery.
Multi-Gen LRUmm/vmscan.c, include/linux/mmzone.hThe lru_gen block in vmscan.c is a second reclaimer living in the same file. inc_max_seq() is aging and evict_folios() is eviction; between them, iterate_mm_list() makes the cost proportional to address spaces rather than to memory. Read get_pmd_pfn() and the Bloom-filter helpers to see how a cold region is skipped without being examined, and lru_gen_look_around() to see eviction feed the next aging pass. In the header, LRU_GEN_MASK and LRU_REFS_MASK show that generations and tiers cost a folio no extra bytes.
Reverse mappingmm/rmap.c, include/linux/rmap.hRead rmap_walk() once for structure, then try_to_unmap_one() line by line — the dirty-bit transfer to folio_mark_dirty() is three lines and is the reason a page dirtied only through a mapping is not silently lost. folio_lock_anon_vma_read() shows why every anon_vma carries a root pointer: one lock per family, taken after a re-check, because nothing pinned the folio when the pointer was read. page_vma_mapped_walk() is the reason every rmap callback is written as a loop.
Deferred TLB flushingmm/rmap.c, mm/vmscan.cshould_defer_flush() and flush_tlb_batched_pending() are worth reading as a pair, because the second is where the cost of the first is paid. The kernel does not track which address spaces have flushes outstanding; it records only that batching happened and makes mprotect() and munmap() flush before they proceed. A good example of moving a cost off a hot path by putting it somewhere colder rather than removing it.
The eviction pipelinemm/vmscan.cshrink_folio_list() is the heart of the chapter and repays a slow read. The ordering in __remove_mapping() is the subtle part: the reference count is frozen before the final checks, so a racing get_user_pages() either arrives first or fails outright. Above it, the three-way writeback decision explains why reclaim usually sets PG_reclaim and moves on instead of writing; and too_many_isolated() shows a reclaimer choosing to sleep rather than compete with the ones already working.
Demotion and memory tieringmm/memory-tiers.c, mm/vmscan.cThe file that did not exist in 2.6. establish_demotion_targets() builds the tier graph, and next_demotion_node() is what demote_folio_list() consults. Read them to see that demotion uses migrate_pages() and therefore try_to_migrate(), not try_to_unmap() — a demoted folio is relocated, not evicted, which is why an anonymous page can be reclaimed on a machine with no swap at all.
Refault detectionmm/workingset.c, mm/swap_state.cThe shortest file that will change how you think about reclaim. workingset_eviction() packs a shadow entry, workingset_test_recent() unpacks it and compares the refault distance against the competing set across types — not simply against the active list of the same type, which is the detail most summaries get wrong. Follow the anonymous side into mm/swap_state.c: swp_tb_is_shadow() shows that swap now stores shadows in its own table, so both halves of reclaim feed one estimator.
Watermarksmm/page_alloc.c, include/linux/mmzone.h__setup_per_zone_wmarks() settles a persistent misconception in about thirty lines: min_free_kbytes sets WMARK_MIN alone, while the min→low and low→high steps come from watermark_scale_factor. zone_watermark_ok() is the other half, and it checks more than a free total — the requested order and lowmem_reserve both participate, which is why an allocation can fail on a zone that looks comfortable.
kswapd and reclaim throttlingmm/vmscan.ckswapd() and balance_pgdat() for the background loop; note the short HZ/10 sleep before the real one and the conditional handoff to kcompactd. Then read throttle_direct_reclaim() and reclaim_throttle(), which are the modern answer to a thundering herd: a thread whose node is too depleted sleeps on pfmemalloc_wait rather than reclaiming. The three exemptions — kernel threads, a pending fatal signal, and a one-second cap for !__GFP_FS — each prevent a specific deadlock and are worth naming.
Cgroup-scoped reclaimmm/memcontrol.c, include/linux/memcontrol.htry_charge_memcg() is where a limit becomes reclaim. The memory.high penalty is not a cap but a calculated sleep applied on return to userspace; read calculate_high_delay() and put real numbers in, because the curve is steeper than it sounds. mem_cgroup_get_oom_group() is short and surprising: memory.oom.group kills the highest flagged ancestor between the victim and the OOMing cgroup, not the cgroup that hit its limit.
Pressure stall informationkernel/sched/psi.c, include/linux/psi_types.hRead psi_memstall_enter() and then grep for its callers in mm/filemap.c and mm/page_io.c. Every one of them is gated on PG_workingset, which is precisely why PSI reports thrashing rather than I/O in general: a wait only counts if the folio was already judged part of the working set. psi_trigger_create() is what makes the file pollable, and therefore what makes a userspace killer possible before the kernel’s own.
The shrinker interfacemm/shrinker.c, include/linux/shrinker.h, mm/list_lru.cdo_shrink_slab() is forty lines that decide how hard every cache in the kernel is squeezed. Two things to check: deferred work is also shifted by priority (total_scan = (nr >> priority) + delta), not merely added; and seeks == 0 is a special case that asks for half the cache outright. In the header, struct shrinker_info carries a per-cgroup bitmap, which is how a machine with thousands of containers avoids calling thousands of empty shrinkers.
The dentry and inode cachesfs/super.c, fs/dcache.c, fs/inode.csuper_cache_scan() shows a shrinker refusing to run — on !__GFP_FS, and when super_trylock_shared() fails — and then splitting its quota three ways. The row to chase is inode_lru_isolate() in fs/inode.c, which calls invalidate_mapping_pages() on the inode’s own mapping and counts PGINODESTEAL: the object shrinkers free page frames directly, not merely slab. vfs_cache_pressure in fs/dcache.c scales the reported count rather than the scan, which is not what its name suggests.
The OOM killer and reapermm/oom_kill.c, include/linux/oom.hRead out_of_memory() from the top and count the ways it returns without killing anything — a notifier freed memory, the caller is already dying, or the allocation cleared __GFP_FS. oom_badness() shows that OOM_SCORE_ADJ_MIN is an explicit early return rather than the end of a scale. __oom_kill_process() kills every task sharing the victim’s mm, and queue_oom_reaper() arms a two-second timer rather than waking the reaper at once — the grace period is the point. __oom_reap_task_mm() walks the address space backwards, to avoid contending with the victim tearing it down from the other end.
Swap areas and slot allocationmm/swapfile.c, include/linux/swap.hstruct swap_info_struct is best read for its cluster lists: a free list, a full list, and per-allocation-order lists of partially used clusters, which is what makes large-folio swap-out a lookup instead of a search. In the constants, SWAP_MAP_MAX is 0x3e, so a slot’s reference count saturates at 62 and anything beyond needs SWP_CONTINUED. try_to_unuse() at the end of the file is the hardest function here: it drains three populations and can legitimately fail with -ENOMEM.
Swap entriesinclude/linux/swapops.h, include/linux/swap.hShort, and it explains a number that looks arbitrary. MAX_SWAPFILES_SHIFT is 5, so thirty-two type values exist — and the MAX_SWAPFILES expression subtracts the ones reserved for migration, device-private memory, hardware poison, and PTE markers, leaving twenty-four swap areas on a fully configured kernel. The page-table entry is contested real estate, and this is where the rent is visible.
The swap cachemm/swap_state.c, mm/swap_table.hThe swap-cache index is no longer an XArray in a fake address_space but a flat per-cluster struct swap_table, so swap_cache_get_folio() is an offset computation with no shared lock. The comment above swap_space calls it a fiction retained only so that code reaching for folio->mapping finds something valid.
Swap I/O and read-aheadmm/page_io.c, mm/swap_state.c, mm/memory.cswap_writeout() and swap_read_folio() for the transfers; do_swap_page() in mm/memory.c for the fault. The correction to make here is in swap_use_vma_readahead(): the policy is chosen by device — VMA read-ahead unless a rotating swap device is active — and not by measured success. What the hit rate governs is the window, in __swapin_nr_pages(), which both policies share. Follow the SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO branch to see the fault path skip the swap cache and the read-ahead together.
Compressed swapmm/zswap.c, drivers/block/zram/zram_drv.cThe two sit in different places for a reason, and the module parameters at the top of zswap.c say why: a pool capped at twenty percent of memory, and an accept threshold of ninety percent that gives the cap hysteresis instead of a cliff. zswap_shrinker_scan() and the zswap_list_lru show that zswap ages its compressed entries on a per-cgroup LRU and writes the coldest through to the real device under ordinary reclaim pressure — the property zram, having nothing behind it, cannot have.
The migration enginemm/migrate.c, include/linux/migrate.h, include/linux/migrate_mode.hStart with migrate_pages() and migrate_pages_batch() and keep the retry constants at hand: ten passes, the first three asynchronous, and a batch the size of one PMD so that one TLB flush covers the lot. __migrate_folio() is the detail to notice: it copies with folio_mc_copy() before folio_ref_freeze(), betting that no one will take a reference meanwhile, and discards the copy with -EAGAIN if someone did. enum migrate_reason in migrate_mode.h is a map of every caller in the kernel.
Migration entriesinclude/linux/leafops.h, mm/rmap.c, include/linux/swap.henum softleaf_type is the newest way to read a non-present PTE: one type per meaning, so swap, migration, device-private, hwpoison, and markers stop pretending to be swap areas. try_to_migrate_one() chooses among three migration types; work out why a read-only entry for an exclusive anonymous page has to be distinguished from a merely read-only one. In swap.h, SWP_MIGRATION_NUM and SWP_HWPOISON_NUM show what each meaning costs in swap-type space.
Moving pages off the LRUmm/migrate.c, mm/balloon.c, mm/zsmalloc.cset_movable_ops() is a closed switch with two cases, the result of the 6.17 rework that took the ops pointer out of page->mapping. Read the balloon and zsmalloc implementations of struct movable_operations side by side: one moves an empty page whose only content is its identity, the other rewrites every handle that points into the page it moves.
Memory hot-removemm/memory_hotplug.c, mm/page_isolation.c, drivers/base/memory.coffline_pages() is a loop that ends only in success, a signal, or a page that can never move, and do_migrate_range() is what it repeats. page_is_unmovable() in page_isolation.c is the real answer to “why can’t I offline this block”: in ZONE_MOVABLE it trusts anything not reserved, and elsewhere it rejects any in-use page that is neither on the LRU nor movable_ops. The auto_movable_* helpers implement the online policy as plain arithmetic on zone page counts.
Memory failuremm/memory-failure.c, mm/hwpoison-inject.cmemory_failure() reads top to bottom as the steps in the chapter. The table to study is error_states[], whose order matters because the first match wins and saved page flags are consulted only on a second pass. In hwpoison_user_mappings(), see how a clean file page drops TTU_HWPOISON so that no one is signaled at all; in kill_proc(), see the single test that decides between BUS_MCEERR_AR and BUS_MCEERR_AO.
Delivering memory errorsarch/x86/kernel/cpu/mce/core.c, arch/arm64/mm/fault.c, drivers/acpi/apei/ghes.c, drivers/ras/cec.cCompare kill_me_maybe() with GHES’s memory_failure_cb(): two architectures reaching the same decision, both from task work and never from the exception itself. In do_machine_check(), the branch that queues kill_me_never() is the reason a kernel copy from user memory can survive poison. do_sea() on ARM64 is short; most of the work is in firmware. cec.c is the soft-offline trigger for corrected errors.

Chapter 18 — The Ext4 Filesystem

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
Feature flags and the one-driver rulefs/ext4/ext4.h, fs/ext4/super.cRead the three EXT4_FEATURE_* families together and the question “what is the difference between ext2, ext3, and ext4?” answers itself: nothing but flags. ext4_check_feature_set_compat() and the ext3 filesystem_type registration in super.c show one driver serving three names.
Block groups and flexible block groupsfs/ext4/balloc.c, fs/ext4/ext4.hext4_get_group_desc() is three lines of arithmetic that fix the whole volume geometry in your head. Then look at EXT4_BG_BLOCK_UNINIT → a group whose bitmap is never written at all.
Lazy inode-table initializationfs/ext4/super.cext4_lazyinit_thread() and ext4_run_li_request() explain why mkfs.ext4 returns instantly on a 10 TB disk. Note the throttle: it times the first group and then waits s_li_wait_mult (10) times as long between groups, rather than using a fixed rate.
The superblock and the mountfs/ext4/super.c__ext4_fill_super() is long but it is the whole bootstrap in order — read the superblock at byte 1024, validate the magic and features, derive the geometry, load the group descriptors, then start the journal. Everything else in the driver assumes this ran.
The inode and its negotiated tailfs/ext4/inode.c, fs/ext4/ext4.hext4_iget() shows why i_extra_isize exists: fields past byte 128 are read only if the inode is large enough to hold them, so one driver reads 128-byte ext2 inodes and 256-byte ext4 inodes with the same code.
Extended attributesfs/ext4/xattr.c, fs/ext4/xattr.hStart with xattr.h — it is short and the entry format is clearer there than in prose. Then look at ext4_xattr_block_set() for the part that surprises people: an attribute block is shared by reference count across up to 1024 inodes, found through an mb_cache, and cloned on write.
Directory entries, HTree, and casefoldingfs/ext4/namei.c, fs/ext4/hash.c, fs/libfs.cThe HTree index hides inside the directory as entries with inode 0, so ext4_dx_find_entry() and the plain linear scan coexist. For casefolding, note that the hash is taken over the folded name — that one choice is what lets the index survive case-insensitive lookup.
Extentsfs/ext4/extents.c, fs/ext4/ext4_extents.hRead the 12-byte ext4_extent field split first; the 48-bit physical start and the 15-bit length with its high bit stolen for “unwritten” explain both the 1 EiB volume limit and the 32,768-block extent limit. ext4_ext_map_blocks() is where splitting an unwritten extent into three actually happens.
The extent status treefs/ext4/extents_status.cThe one ext4 structure with no on-disk counterpart. Worth reading because it is not merely a cache: delayed extents live here, which is what makes delayed allocation representable at all.
Mapping blocksfs/ext4/inode.c, fs/ext4/indirect.cext4_map_blocks() is the hinge of the read and write paths. Follow the dispatch on EXT4_INODE_EXTENTS to see the ext2 indirect-block code still living alongside the extent code in the same driver.
The multi-block allocatorfs/ext4/mballoc.cThe largest single algorithm in ext4. Read ext4_mb_normalize_request() before the search loop — the allocator rounds a request up to a bucket based on file size, so what it looks for is rarely what was asked for. Then follow the staged CR_* criteria from cheap to desperate.
Delayed allocationfs/ext4/inode.cext4_da_write_begin() reserves without allocating; ext4_writepages() allocates. The interesting part is the reservation accounting that keeps ENOSPC from surfacing at writeback, where no caller is waiting to hear it.
Large folios and the journalfs/ext4/super.c, fs/ext4/inode.cA short but revealing pair: ext4_set_max_mapping_order() caps the folio order to bound journal credits, and clamps it to the minimum entirely under data=journal. It is a clean example of one subsystem limiting another.
Journaling: handles and transactionsfs/ext4/ext4_jbd2.c, fs/jbd2/transaction.c, fs/jbd2/journal.c, include/linux/jbd2.hRead the credit arithmetic in ext4_jbd2.h before the journal itself. Every ext4 operation must declare in advance how many metadata blocks it may dirty, and that requirement shapes the structure of nearly every function in the driver.
Commit and checkpointfs/jbd2/commit.c, fs/jbd2/checkpoint.cjbd2_journal_commit_transaction() is the write-ahead rule made literal, phase by phase. Note that the commit block is written with REQ_PREFLUSH | REQ_FUA — the atomicity of the whole scheme rests on that one flag pair.
Recovery and revokefs/jbd2/recovery.c, fs/jbd2/revoke.cThree passes, and the middle one exists for a single subtle reason: a block that was metadata when it was journaled may be file data by the time replay runs. Revoke records are the fix, and they are the least obvious part of journaling.
Fast commitfs/ext4/fast_commit.cRead the EXT4_FC_TAG_* vocabulary and then the ineligibility checks. The list of things that cannot be fast-committed teaches more about the mechanism than the list of things that can.
Inline datafs/ext4/inline.cSmall files live in i_block and spill into the system.data extended attribute, so this file only makes sense after xattr.c. It is also the clearest demonstration that i_block is 60 bytes of reinterpretable space, not a pointer array.
Encryption and verityfs/ext4/crypto.c, fs/ext4/verity.c, fs/crypto/, fs/verity/Both ext4 files are thin shims over generic frameworks, which is the point. The detail worth catching is in verity.c: the Merkle tree is stored past i_size, at the next 64 KiB boundary — not in an extended attribute, as the encryption policy is.
Free-space accounting and the reserved poolfs/ext4/balloc.c, fs/ext4/super.cext4_has_free_clusters() is the whole space policy in one function: three separate reserves subtracted in a fixed order, and three classes of caller allowed past different ones. Read ext4_set_resv_clusters() alongside it — the pool exists because freeing space on an extent filesystem can require allocating a block, so a full volume could otherwise wedge. Then ext4_should_retry_alloc() for why ENOSPC is usually transient.
Inode allocation and the Orlov policyfs/ext4/ialloc.cTwo policies in one file. find_group_other() keeps a file beside its parent; find_group_orlov() deliberately scatters directories, but only those whose parent is the root or carries EXT4_INODE_TOPDIR. Note that the starting group comes from a hash of the directory's name, which makes the scattering reproducible rather than random.
Metadata checksumsfs/ext4/super.c, fs/ext4/inode.cLook at how ext4_group_desc_csum() and ext4_inode_csum() both substitute a zeroed dummy_csum for the field being computed — the standard trick for checksumming a structure that contains its own checksum. The inode's split i_checksum_lo/i_checksum_hi shows what happens when a field must straddle the old and new halves of a structure.
Multi-mount protectionfs/ext4/mmp.cA short, self-contained file worth reading whole. With no lock manager available, kmmpd() and ext4_multi_mount_protect() implement mutual exclusion between hosts as a timed heartbeat written into one disk block — a good example of solving a distributed problem with only the shared medium.
Migrating between extents and indirect blocksfs/ext4/migrate.cext4_ext_migrate() and ext4_ind_migrate() convert an inode between the two mapping formats in both directions without moving any file data, which is the clearest demonstration that the block map is metadata layered over the same blocks.

Chapter 19 — Process Communication

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
The pipe object and its buffer ringfs/pipe.cStart at do_pipe2() and alloc_pipe_info(). The buffers are a resizable power-of-two ring of struct pipe_buffer entries, each pointing at a page rather than owning a copy — which is the single design decision that later made splice possible. pipe_write() shows why the atomicity guarantee stops at PIPE_BUF.
FIFO open semanticsfs/pipe.cfifo_open() is the whole of what a FIFO adds to a pipe. It is worth reading for the rendezvous rule alone: a blocking open for reading waits for a writer and vice versa, so the S_IFIFO inode is only a marker and the two ends meet in the same pipe_inode_info that an anonymous pipe uses.
Zero-copy: splice, tee, vmsplicefs/splice.csplice_to_pipe() and iter_file_splice_write() move page references between a pipe and a file without copying bytes. do_tee() duplicates references between two pipes without consuming them, which shows most clearly that a pipe buffer is a reference and not data. Note where the kernel must still fall back to a copy.
Notification pipeskernel/watch_queue.cRead post_one_notification() to see the kernel writing into a pipe ring directly. The buffers carry PIPE_BUF_FLAG_WHOLE, so a short read returns ENOBUFS instead of truncating a record, and note_loss makes overflow visible to the reader rather than silent. User-space writes to such a pipe are refused with EXDEV.
The System V IPC frameworkipc/util.c, ipc/ipc_sysctl.cipc_obtain_object_idr() and ipcget() are the common machinery behind semget, msgget, and shmget. The lookup is an IDR plus an rhashtable under RCU — read the identifier-to-object path and the reason the API’s ancient key/identifier split survives unchanged on top of a completely rewritten implementation. Then compare SEM_STAT with SEM_STAT_ANY in ipc/sem.c for a small, clear case of a permission check being replaced by an audit record plus an LSM hook.
IPC namespacesipc/namespace.c, include/linux/ipc_namespace.hcopy_ipcs() builds a namespace with empty object tables and its own limits. This is what makes two containers able to use the same ftok key without colliding, and it is the clearest illustration of why System V’s global names needed a containment mechanism bolted on.
System V semaphores and undoipc/sem.cdo_semtimedop() is long but repays reading twice: once for the all-or-nothing semantics of an operation array, once for the fine-grained locking that replaced the old per-array lock. Then read struct sem_undo and exit_sem() — the undo list is the kernel cleaning up after a process that died holding a semaphore, and has no equivalent in any newer mechanism.
System V message queuesipc/msg.c, ipc/msgutil.cdo_msgsnd() and do_msgrcv() implement type-based selective receive, the one thing a POSIX queue cannot do. The “pipelined send” optimization — publishing the message into an already-waiting receiver’s structure with a release barrier so it never enters the queue — recurs in almost every blocking IPC path in the kernel. ipc/msgutil.c shows why a message is a chain of single pages rather than one allocation.
System V shared memoryipc/shm.c, mm/shmem.cnewseg() creates a shmem file and do_shmat() maps it. The punchline is that there is no special segment type at all: a System V segment is an ordinary tmpfs file, demand-faulted like any other, which is why it shows up in /proc/<pid>/maps and obeys the same reclaim rules.
POSIX message queuesipc/mqueue.cdo_mq_timedsend() and struct posix_msg_tree_node hold the priority red-black tree with its cached rightmost node. Read do_mq_notify() alongside it: the async notification System V never had, delivered by signal or by a netlink message whose cookie skb is allocated and attached at registration time so delivery can never fail, and one-shot by design. mqueue_get_inode() shows the RLIMIT_MSGQUEUE charge including per-message headers and tree nodes.
POSIX shared memory and semaphoresmm/shmem.c, ipc/mqueue.cThere is no file to read for either one, and that is the lesson. The syscall table has no shm_open, sem_open, sem_wait, or sem_post — the six mq_* calls are the whole POSIX IPC surface. shm_open is library-side open on tmpfs at /dev/shm, and an uncontended POSIX semaphore never enters the kernel at all — it is a futex word in shared memory. The absence of kernel code is the design.
The futex corekernel/futex/core.c, kernel/futex/waitwake.cget_futex_key() is the heart of it: how a user-space address becomes an identity every participating process agrees on, keyed by (mm, address) for a private futex and by page and offset for a shared one. Then futex_wait_setup() and futex_wake() in waitwake.c for the wait/wake pair and the race the comparison closes. futex_hash() and futex_hash_allocate_default() show the per-process hash table that replaced the one global array, sized from the thread count and tunable with prctl(PR_FUTEX_HASH).
Requeue, PI, and robust futexeskernel/futex/requeue.c, kernel/futex/pi.c, kernel/futex/core.cfutex_requeue() exists so that pthread_cond_broadcast does not stampede every waiter onto one mutex. futex_lock_pi() hands the kernel’s rt-mutex priority inheritance to a user-space lock. exit_robust_list() and futex_cleanup() are the kernel repairing a lock whose owner died holding it.
futex2 and futex_waitvkernel/futex/syscalls.csys_futex_waitv() lets one thread wait on several futexes and wake on any, which the original interface could not express; the FUTEX2_* flags carry per-futex attributes the operation word had no room for. Note that the size flags are declared but not implemented — futex_flags_valid() in kernel/futex/futex.h still accepts only 32-bit futexes. Read this next to the original sys_futex() multiplexer, and next to sys_futex_wake(), sys_futex_wait() and sys_futex_requeue(), to see an interface being replaced rather than extended.
eventfdfs/eventfd.cThe smallest complete example of the fd-as-IPC pattern: a 64-bit counter, eventfd_read() and eventfd_write(), and eventfd_signal() for the in-kernel fast path that io_uring and KVM use. Note that the in-kernel call adds exactly one and cannot block, so it lets the count saturate at ULLONG_MAX and reports the loss as EPOLLERR; the current->in_eventfd guard shows what nested wakeups would otherwise do. Read it first if the file-descriptor theme of the chapter is not yet obvious.
signalfd and timerfdfs/signalfd.c, fs/timerfd.csignalfd_dequeue() dequeues from the “reader’s” own pending set, not the creator’s — the mask is fixed at creation but the signals come from whoever calls read(), a subtlety worth confirming in the code. timerfd_setup() hooks an ordinary hrtimer to a descriptor, which is all it takes to bring timers into an epoll loop.
memfd and sealingmm/memfd.cmemfd_create() makes a nameless tmpfs file; memfd_add_seals() applies the F_SEAL_* seals. check_sysctl_memfd_noexec() is the other half of the story: F_SEAL_EXEC and MFD_NOEXEC_SEAL exist to stop a memfd being used for fileless execution, and the policy is per PID namespace and may only be tightened. The important reading is why a seal must be irreversible and why F_SEAL_WRITE needs a check that no writable mapping already exists — without that, a receiver could not trust the seal it just verified.
pidfdkernel/pid.c, kernel/signal.c, kernel/fork.c, fs/pidfs.cpidfd_open(), pidfd_send_signal(), and the CLONE_PIDFD path in copy_process(). Then fs/pidfs.c, which moved pidfds off the anonymous inode onto a filesystem of their own so that two pidfds can finally be compared, exported as file handles, and asked for an exit status with PIDFD_GET_INFO. The theme of the chapter ends here: even a process becomes a file descriptor, and the pid-reuse race that has bitten Unix supervisors for forty years closes.
epollfs/eventpoll.c, include/linux/eventpoll.hRead ep_insert() and ep_ptable_queue_proc() together to see how one call to the target's poll method installs the permanent wait-queue entry whose wake function, ep_poll_callback(), pushes readiness onto rdllist. Then follow ep_poll() into ep_send_events(): the re-poll before copy-out, the branch that re-queues level-triggered items and skips EPOLLET ones, and the ovflist hand-off that lets callbacks run during a scan. ep_loop_check() and EP_MAX_NESTS bound nested instances, and eventpoll_release(), called only from __fput(), is why a watch outlives a close() on a duplicated descriptor.
Unix-domain socketsnet/unix/af_unix.cunix_stream_sendmsg() and unix_dgram_sendmsg() for the three socket types, and unix_bind() and unix_autobind() for the addressing. Confirm two things that surprise people: an abstract name is scoped by the net namespace, and an unbound socket is given a random five-hex-digit abstract name automatically.
Descriptor and credential passingnet/core/scm.c, net/unix/af_unix.c, fs/file.cscm_detach_fds() and the receive_fd() it now calls through fs/file.c are where a struct file reference crosses a process boundary, and where security_file_receive() is the only policy check in the path — note that the descriptor number never travels, and that MSG_CMSG_CLOEXEC exists because there is no other instant at which a receiver could close the exec window. Note also that a control buffer too small to hold every descriptor yields a partial delivery plus MSG_CTRUNC, not a refusal. SO_PASSPIDFD and SCM_PIDFD replace the forgeable-looking pid with a pidfd.
AF_UNIX garbage collectionnet/unix/garbage.cThe only garbage collector in the kernel, and it exists purely because SCM_RIGHTS lets one socket hold a reference to another. unix_add_edges() and unix_del_edges() maintain the graph; __unix_walk_scc() runs Tarjan’s algorithm over it. The best detail is the UNIX_GRAPH_NOT_CYCLIC state, which lets the collector skip everything when no cycle can exist — which is nearly always.

Chapter 20 — Program Execution

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
The exec pipelinefs/exec.cdo_execveat_common() and bprm_execve() are the spine of the chapter. Read it once for the order of operations and a second time for the reason behind that order: everything fallible happens before begin_new_exec() and everything after it must succeed. alloc_bprm() builds a detached mm_struct with a one-page temporary stack, so the caller’s program stays intact and an abort is just a free. exec_mmap() is where the switch actually happens. Two things at the edges are easy to skip: the RLIMIT_NPROC recheck at the very top, which collects a debt deliberately deferred from an unchecked set*uid(), and the empty-argv fixup at the very bottom, which substitutes an empty string so a program that starts parsing at argv[1] cannot walk into its own environment.
Unsafe execution statesfs/exec.c, include/linux/security.hcheck_unsafe_exec() is fifteen lines that decide whether privilege may be gained. It counts the tasks sharing the caller’s fs_struct and raises LSM_UNSAFE_SHARE if any of them is outside the thread group, because such a task could move the current directory under a setuid exec. When it is safe it does the opposite and sets fs->in_exec, blocking clone(CLONE_FS) until de_thread() completes — which is the lock that the in_exec = 0 line in begin_new_exec() releases. do_open_execat() nearby is where noexec mounts and ETXTBSY come from.
What an exec seversfs/exec.c, kernel/signal.c, kernel/time/posix-timers.cRead begin_new_exec() a third time, this time only for what it destroys. The most-misremembered line in it is flush_signal_handlers(me, 0): the zero means a disposition of SIG_IGN is preserved, not reset, which is why a shell can start a background job with SIGINT already ignored. exit_itimers() shows that timers, by contrast, are destroyed outright rather than disarmed. And de_thread() is where a non-leader thread takes over the group leader’s tid, pgid, sid, and even its birthdate — the pid survives the exec, but not necessarily the task that held it.
Argument and environment limitsfs/exec.c, include/uapi/linux/binfmts.hbprm_stack_limits() answers the question every programmer eventually asks: where does E2BIG come from? The cap is a quarter of the stack rlimit or three quarters of _STK_LIM, whichever is smaller, floored at ARG_MAX so a small stack cannot break ordinary command lines — and the argv and envp pointer arrays are charged against it here, early, because by the time they are written the failure could no longer be reported.
The binary-format frameworkfs/exec.c, include/linux/binfmts.hsearch_binary_handler() walks the formats list and offers the file to each load_binary in turn. Note how little is left of struct linux_binfmt: a list hook, a module pointer, load_binary, and a core dumper. The load_shlib entry of 2.6-era kernels, and the uselib system call behind it, are gone — the kernel no longer has any notion of loading a library. Two more things to look for: the request_module("binfmt-%04x", ...) retry that 2.6.11 used to demand-load a format from bytes in the file itself is gone, so the walk is a single pass; and list order is chosen at registration by __register_binfmt()’s insert argument, where exactly one format in the tree — binfmt_misc — asks to go in front of binfmt_elf. The loop that retries after a rewrite lives one level up, in exec_binprm(), bounded to five interpreter rewrites before ELOOP.
Scripts and binfmt_miscfs/binfmt_script.c, fs/binfmt_misc.cload_script() is forty lines and repays every one of them: it does not load anything, it stores the interpreter in bprm->interpreter and returns, leaving the retry to exec_binprm(). Read the long comment in the middle: an over-long #! line does not silently truncate the interpreter path, it fails the exec with ENOEXEC, because running a prefix of a pathname would be a security hazard. fs/binfmt_misc.c generalizes the same trick to administrator-supplied rules, which is how a foreign-architecture binary runs under an emulator without anyone typing the emulator’s name. Its four flags are worth reading in load_misc_binary(): C is not a variant of O but sets execfd_creds, so credentials are computed from the emulated binary rather than the emulator.
Loading an ELF binaryfs/binfmt_elf.cload_elf_binary() is the most consequential load_binary in the kernel. Watch where it calls begin_new_exec() — everything before that line is re-validation, everything after is mapping. elf_map() places each PT_LOAD segment, padzero() handles the BSS tail, and the PT_INTERP path is what makes the dynamic linker, not the program, the first code to run. Read the load-bias branch and its comment carefully: only an ET_DYN with an interpreter is placed at ELF_ET_DYN_BASE plus arch_mmap_rnd(); a static PIE gets a bias of zero and lands at the mmap base, so that ./ld.so someprog cannot put a loader and its program in the same range. total_mapping_size() and MAP_FIXED_NOREPLACE are what keep segments from landing on each other.
The initial stack and the auxiliary vectorfs/binfmt_elf.c, include/uapi/linux/auxvec.hcreate_elf_tables() lays out argc, the pointer arrays, and the auxiliary vector. The vector is the kernel’s private channel to the new program: AT_RANDOM carries sixteen fresh random bytes for the stack canary and AT_SECURE is the one bit that tells the dynamic linker to distrust the environment. Read it alongside arch_parse_elf_property() in the same file. Note that the vector is written twice — once onto the stack and once into mm->saved_auxv, which is what /proc/<pid>/auxv reads back long after the stack copy is gone — and that the first entries come from the architecture’s ARCH_DLINFO, where AT_SYSINFO_EHDR and AT_MINSIGSTKSZ originate.
Address-space layout and randomizationmm/util.c, arch/x86/mm/mmap.c, mm/mmap.c, mm/memory.carch_pick_mmap_layout() in mm/util.c is the generic top-down layout; x86 overrides it in arch/x86/mm/mmap.c. The entropy comes from arch_mmap_rnd(), bounded by the mmap_rnd_bits sysctl, and randomize_va_space in mm/memory.c selects how much of the layout moves. The interesting thing is how many independent draws there are — stack, mmap base, PIE load bias, brk — and that a single leaked pointer collapses only one of them. Read mmap_base() closely: the gap it subtracts from the stack top begins as RLIMIT_STACK, so ulimit -s silently relocates every shared library. Compare STACK_RND_MASK in the two architectures’ asm/elf.h for how unequal “ASLR is on” really is.
The stack guard gapmm/mmap.c, mm/vma.c, include/linux/mm.hstack_guard_gap, 256 pages by default and settable only on the kernel command line, is the whole of the fix for the 2017 “Stack Clash” attacks. One variable, two comparisons — expand_downwards() in mm/vma.c checks it growing down, and the brk path in mm/mmap.c checks it growing up — and a class of exploit that walked a growing stack into a neighbouring mapping stops working. Note that the gap is skipped against an inaccessible neighbour, and that VM_STARTGAP_FLAGS extends it to shadow stacks.
Stack growth and the mmap lockmm/mmap_lock.c, mm/vma.c, mm/memory.clock_mm_and_find_vma() is where a page fault discovers that the address it is servicing lies below a VM_GROWSDOWN VMA and that it therefore has to modify the address space while holding only a read lock. Watch it try to upgrade atomically, and fall back to dropping the lock, retaking it for writing, and redoing the lookup because the VMA may be gone. It downgrades again before returning. expand_stack() reached from __access_remote_vm() is the other way in: ptrace and /proc/<pid>/mem can grow a stack too.
The interpreter and the vDSOfs/binfmt_elf.c, arch/x86/entry/vdso/vma.c, arch/arm64/kernel/vdso.cRead load_elf_interp() for the whole of the kernel’s opinion about dynamic linking: a handful of sanity checks, one mmap per PT_LOAD, and AT_BASE so the linker can find itself. Note that exe_file_allow_write_access() releases the interpreter immediately after loading, so unlike the executable it is not protected by ETXTBSY while it runs — and a shared library never was. The vDSO side is a real shared object built into the kernel with the soname linux-vdso.so.1, which is why ldd lists a library with no path.
Sealing and memory-deny-write-executemm/mseal.c, include/linux/mman.h, kernel/sys.cTwo answers to the same question: what stops an attacker from undoing a hardening measure with mprotect? mseal() makes a range’s protections permanent, which is what turns full RELRO from a speed bump into a barrier, and VM_SEALED_SYSMAP applies it to the mappings the kernel makes itself. map_deny_write_exec() is the other half — read both clauses, because refusing to let a non-executable mapping become executable is the one that actually closes the loophole.
The credential objectkernel/cred.c, include/linux/cred.hprepare_creds() and commit_creds() are the whole discipline in two functions: never modify a live credential, copy it, change the copy, install it atomically. commit_creds() is what exec calls at its point of no return. The body does far more than swap a pointer: it sets the dumpable state from fs.suid_dumpable, clears pdeath_signal, and issues the write barrier that __ptrace_may_access() depends on. Note the two pointers a task holds, real_cred and cred, and that identities are kuid_t values meaningful only relative to a user namespace.
Namespace-relative capability checkskernel/capability.c, security/commoncap.ccapable() is only ns_capable(&init_user_ns, cap); the real work is in cap_capable(), which walks from the target namespace toward the root and grants everything to the namespace’s owner. That one rule is why unprivileged containers work. capable_wrt_inode_uidgid() and file_ns_capable() guard the seams, and cap_convert_nscap() shows why the revision-3 file-capability attribute needs a rootid.
Capabilities and the exec transitionsecurity/commoncap.c, include/uapi/linux/capability.hcap_bprm_creds_from_file() is the arithmetic that decides what a program comes out of an exec holding, and it is worth working through by hand once. The same file sets bprm->secureexec, which becomes AT_SECURE in the auxiliary vector, and honours the no_new_privs latch that makes unprivileged sandboxing possible at all. CAP_LAST_CAP is 40 (CAP_CHECKPOINT_RESTORE) in the 7.0 series, and code that clears the bounding set must read it rather than assume. Read handle_privileged_root() in the same file for what uid 0 actually gets: the caller’s bounding set combined with its inheritable set, not the full capability set, and the effective bit only when the effective uid is root.
Trusted execution and the new securebitsfs/exec.c, security/commoncap.c, include/uapi/linux/securebits.hThe AT_EXECVE_CHECK flag and the SECBIT_EXEC_RESTRICT_FILE and SECBIT_EXEC_DENY_INTERACTIVE bits are one feature read from two ends. bprm->is_check makes the kernel run every exec check and then return without executing, so an interpreter can ask whether a file it is about to “source” would have been allowed to run; the securebits are what make the answer binding. They are also the first securebits an unprivileged process may set on itself.
Credential system callskernel/sys.c, kernel/groups.c, kernel/capability.c, security/commoncap.c__sys_setresuid() shows the whole pattern in seventy lines: make_kuid, the "is any id new?" test against ns_capable_setid(CAP_SETUID), then prepare_creds, security_task_fix_setuid and commit_creds; compare __sys_setfsuid(), which returns the old value instead of an error, and flag_nproc_exceeded(), which defers the RLIMIT_NPROC failure to execve. In kernel/capability.c, cap_validate_magic() is the version handshake and capset refuses any pid but the caller's; cap_capset(), cap_emulate_setxuid() and the PR_SET_KEEPCAPS/PR_CAP_AMBIENT cases of cap_task_prctl() in security/commoncap.c hold the capability rules.
The LSM framework and stackingsecurity/security.c, include/linux/lsm_hook_defs.hinclude/linux/lsm_hook_defs.h is a single list of every hook in the kernel, and reading it is the fastest way to see how thoroughly security is threaded through the rest of the book’s subjects. In security/security.c, look at how hooks are dispatched through static calls and how lsm_blob_sizes lets several modules share one per-object blob — the mechanism that turned “the security module” into “the security module stack”. Beware the exec hooks: they do not run in the order they are declared. Follow the calls in fs/exec.c instead — bprm_creds_for_exec, then bprm_check_security from inside search_binary_handler(), then bprm_creds_from_file.
Querying the active LSM stacksecurity/lsm_syscalls.c, include/uapi/linux/lsm.hThree small system calls added in Linux 6.8 — lsm_list_modules(), lsm_get_self_attr() and lsm_set_self_attr() — replace the per-module files under /proc/<pid>/attr, which could not say which module an answer came from once several were active. The LSM_ID_* numbers in the UAPI header are the current roster: alongside SELinux and AppArmor sit LoadPin, SafeSetID, Lockdown, IPE and the BPF LSM.
Seccompkernel/seccomp.c__seccomp_filter() runs on every system call, so read it with an eye on cost as well as policy. Filters are additive and never removable, which is what makes a sandbox trustworthy, and SECCOMP_RET_USER_NOTIF turns a denied call into a message to a supervising process — the hook every modern container runtime uses to emulate the syscalls it will not simply allow. Note the signed comparison in seccomp_run_filters(): the action constants are numbered so that the lowest return value is the least permissive, which is the whole of the “filters can only tighten” guarantee.
Landlocksecurity/landlock/Start with landlock_restrict_self() in security/landlock/syscalls.c, then security/landlock/fs.c. The striking thing is that none of it requires privilege: no_new_privs plus landlock_restrict_self() is the whole trust argument. Track the ABI version constant in security/landlock/syscalls.c to see the feature list grow — LANDLOCK_ACCESS_FS_REFER for renames, LANDLOCK_ACCESS_FS_IOCTL_DEV for device ioctls, and the scopes that fence off abstract sockets and signals.
ptrace and Yamakernel/ptrace.c, security/yama/yama_lsm.cptrace_may_access() is the gate every tracing operation passes through, and it is short. Then read yama_ptrace_access_check() in security/yama/yama_lsm.c, which is the entire implementation of kernel.yama.ptrace_scope — a reminder that an LSM can be a hundred useful lines. task_is_descendant() and ptracer_exception_found() are the two halves of scope 1 — any descendant, plus whoever the target named with PR_SET_PTRACER. A setuid binary run under a tracer normally loses its privileges, but check the ptracer_capable() test in security/commoncap.c for the exception.

Chapter 21 — Power Management

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
Firmware interfacesdrivers/firmware/psci/psci.c, drivers/acpi/sleep.c, include/uapi/linux/psci.hRead these as two catalogues of what the kernel may ask firmware to do. psci.c probes the PSCI version and its optional functions at boot, then installs whatever the firmware supports: psci_cpu_suspend_enter() for idle, the SYSTEM_SUSPEND suspend ops, psci_sys_poweroff(), and the SYSTEM_RESET/SYSTEM_RESET2 restart handler. The function IDs in the uapi header are the whole protocol. sleep.c is the ACPI equivalent for x86: acpi_sleep_init() evaluates the _S0–_S5 objects to find which sleep states exist, and registers the suspend, hibernation, and power-off handlers to match. Before tracing any of the paths below, check which of the two files is running on the machine in front of you.
Measuring power and residencyarch/x86/events/rapl.c, arch/x86/events/intel/cstate.c, drivers/platform/x86/intel/pmc/core.c, drivers/acpi/acpi_lpit.c, tools/power/x86/turbostat/turbostat.cThese are where the numbers of §1.4 come from. rapl_pmu_init() in rapl.c registers the power perf PMU whose energy events read the RAPL counters, and cstate_init() in cstate.c registers the cstate_core and cstate_pkg PMUs over the hardware residency MSRs. For S0ix, pmc_core_dbgfs_register() creates slp_s0_residency_usec from the PMC’s own counter, and acpi_init_lpit() reads the ACPI LPIT table that backs the low_power_idle_*_residency_us attributes. turbostat.c is the user-space side: it reads the same MSRs directly and is the quickest way to see whether a CPU or package state was actually reached, as opposed to requested.
Sleep states and /sys/powerkernel/power/main.c, kernel/power/suspend.cStart with pm_states_init() and suspend_set_ops(), which together decide which words appear in /sys/power/state. valid_state() asks the platform’s ->valid(), and s2idle is always offered. Then read decode_state(), state_store(), and mem_sleep_store() to see how the word “mem” is resolved through mem_sleep_current. The mapping from labels to suspend_state_t lives in the pm_labels and mem_sleep_labels arrays; mem_sleep_default and the mem_sleep_default= boot parameter set what “mem” means before user space does.
Suspend-to-idlekernel/power/suspend.c, kernel/sched/idle.c, kernel/time/tick-common.c, drivers/acpi/x86/s2idle.cs2idle_enter() is short enough to read in full: it sets s2idle_state, wakes every idle CPU, and sleeps on a wait queue until s2idle_wake() runs. On the other CPUs, idle_should_enter_s2idle() in the idle loop routes them to call_cpuidle_s2idle(). The last CPU to arrive stops timekeeping in tick_freeze(). Then read s2idle_loop() for the spurious-wakeup loop, which goes back to sleep without resuming devices. On x86 laptops, acpi_s2idle_ops_lps0 and the LPS0 _DSM calls in s2idle.c are what make s2idle as frugal as S3.
Platform suspend hooksinclude/linux/suspend.h, drivers/acpi/sleep.c, arch/x86/kernel/acpi/sleep.c, arch/x86/kernel/acpi/wakeup_64.S, arch/arm64/kernel/suspend.cRead struct platform_suspend_ops next to suspend_enter() to see where each hook fires. Then compare the two implementations. acpi_suspend_ops uses acpi_pm_prepare() to install the waking vector, and acpi_suspend_enter() descends through x86_acpi_suspend_lowlevel() to do_suspend_lowlevel, which writes the PM1 control registers. PSCI’s version is a few lines: psci_system_suspend_enter() calls cpu_suspend() with a finisher that issues SYSTEM_SUSPEND, pointing at cpu_resume. __cpu_suspend_enter() returning twice, like setjmp(), is the idea to take away.
Power-off and rebootkernel/reboot.c, drivers/base/core.c, kernel/pid_namespace.cSYSCALL_DEFINE4(reboot) shows the magic-number check, the CAP_SYS_BOOT test against the PID namespace’s user namespace, and the diversion into reboot_pid_ns() for a container. kernel_power_off() and kernel_restart() are short: notifiers, device_shutdown(), migrate_to_reboot_cpu(), syscore_shutdown(), then the machine hook. device_shutdown() walks devices_kset backwards, one device at a time. Then read register_sys_off_handler() and do_kernel_power_off() for the prioritized handler chain that replaced the bare pm_power_off pointer in 5.19. Finish with __hw_protection_trigger() and orderly_poweroff(): when the kernel decides on its own to shut down, it first asks user space and keeps a deadline as a fallback.
Architecture reset pathsarch/x86/kernel/reboot.c, arch/arm64/kernel/process.c, drivers/firmware/efi/reboot.cThe two architectures take opposite approaches. On x86, native_machine_emergency_restart() is a loop over reboot_type that tries ACPI, the keyboard controller, EFI, the BIOS, port 0xCF9, and finally a triple fault, falling through to the next method whenever one fails to reset the machine; the DMI quirk table above it shows why the loop is needed. On ARM64, machine_restart() stops the other CPUs, prefers efi_reboot() when runtime services exist, and otherwise runs the restart-handler chain. PSCI’s psci_sys_reset() is on that chain at priority 129, just above the default. If every handler returns, the CPU halts with “Reboot failed”.
Entering system sleepkernel/power/suspend.c, kernel/power/main.cFollow pm_suspend() into enter_state(), suspend_prepare(), suspend_devices_and_enter(), and suspend_enter(), and mark each place that returns early through suspend_test() or pm_wakeup_pending(). The order is the chapter in miniature: system_transition_mutex and the filesystem sync, the PM notifiers and the freezer, the device phases, the platform’s ->prepare() hooks, taking the non-boot CPUs offline, syscore_suspend() with interrupts off, and ->enter(). Resume is the same code read upward from ->enter(). Notice how each error label undoes exactly the steps that have already been done.
Device suspend phasesdrivers/base/power/main.c, include/linux/pm.hRead the five list heads at the top first. A device moves from dpm_list through dpm_prepared_list, dpm_suspended_list, dpm_late_early_list, and dpm_noirq_list as it passes each phase, and resume moves it back. Then read one callback selector, such as the device_suspend_noirq() body: the PM domain is consulted first, then the device type, the class, the bus, and only then the driver. The async machinery is the subtle part. dpm_async_fn() runs a device in a worker, and dpm_wait_for_subordinate() on suspend and dpm_wait_for_superior() on resume preserve parent–child and device-link order. struct dev_pm_ops in pm.h lists all the callbacks a driver may implement.
The freezerkernel/freezer.c, kernel/power/process.c, include/linux/freezer.htry_to_freeze_tasks() is the loop that gives up after freeze_timeout_msecs (20 s) and prints the tasks “refusing to freeze”. freeze_processes() calls it for user space and, after that, disables the OOM killer; freeze_kernel_threads() then handles the kthreads that called set_freezable(). In freezer.c, freeze_task() sends a fake signal or a wakeup, and __refrigerator() parks the task in TASK_FROZEN. __set_task_frozen() shows which sleeping tasks can be frozen in place without waking them at all. The freezer_active static key keeps freezing() free on every other path.
Wakeup sources and autosleepdrivers/base/power/wakeup.c, kernel/power/autosleep.c, kernel/power/wakelock.cThe comment above combined_event_count and split_counters() explain the design: completed events in the upper half, events in progress in the lower half, so starting or finishing an event is one atomic add. Then read pm_get_wakeup_count(), pm_save_wakeup_count(), and pm_wakeup_pending() together. They are the user-space handshake that closes the race between deciding to suspend and a wakeup arriving. pm_system_irq_wakeup() is how an armed interrupt aborts a suspend that is already under way. try_to_suspend() in autosleep.c is the same loop run by the kernel itself, and wakelock.c is the Android user-space interface.
Suspend diagnosticskernel/power/main.c, drivers/base/power/main.c, drivers/base/power/trace.c, include/trace/events/power.h, tools/power/pm-graph/sleepgraph.pyIn kernel/power/main.c read the pm_tests array and pm_test_store(), then find each suspend_test() call in suspend.c to see where every test level stops. The same file keeps suspend_stats, fed by dpm_save_failed_dev() and dpm_save_failed_step(), and exports it to sysfs and debugfs. dpm_watchdog_set() arms the timer that panics when one device callback hangs. trace.c is the PM_TRACE_RTC trick: it hashes the last device into the RTC so the next boot can name it. power.h defines the suspend_resume and device_pm_callback_* tracepoints from which sleepgraph.py builds its timelines.
Hibernation control flowkernel/power/hibernate.cRead hibernate(), hibernation_snapshot(), and create_image() in order, with one variable in mind: in_suspend, which is in __nosavedata so that its value survives the restore. After swsusp_arch_suspend() returns, create_image() runs twice in effect, once in the kernel that saved the image (with in_suspend set) and once in the kernel that was restored (with it clear). power_down() shows the choice between platform S4, kernel_power_off(), kernel_restart(), and suspend-to-both. software_resume(), load_image_and_restore(), hibernation_restore(), and resume_target_kernel() are the return path. Then follow the failure paths: hibernation_platform_enter() returning -EAGAIN after a wakeup event, swsusp_unmark() putting the swap signature back, and the snapshot_test flag with which test_resume mode restores the image without powering off.
The snapshotkernel/power/snapshot.cStart at struct memory_bitmap and struct rtree_node: a per-zone radix tree of page-sized bitmaps that holds one bit per page frame. Then read hibernate_preallocate_memory(), which reclaims memory until the image fits and allocates the copies in advance, and swsusp_save(), which runs with interrupts off and does nothing but copy. saveable_page() is the definition of “memory that matters”, and copy_data_pages() drives it. On resume, snapshot_write_next() and get_safe_page() solve the harder problem of loading pages whose final frames the boot kernel is still using.
Image I/Okernel/power/swap.c, kernel/power/user.cswsusp_write() writes the header, the metadata pages, and the data, then stamps HIBERNATE_SIG over the swap signature so that swapon will refuse the partition. save_compressed_image() is a small producer–consumer pipeline: compression threads, a CRC32 thread (crc32_threadfn()), and the writer, all fed from the snapshot. struct swsusp_header and its SF_* flags record how the image was made. swsusp_check() and swsusp_read() are the boot-time side; note that swsusp_check() restores the original swap signature as soon as it finds an image, so an image is never used twice. user.c exposes the same snapshot to user-space tools through /dev/snapshot; snapshot_ioctl() is effectively that interface’s whole API.
Architecture image restorearch/x86/power/hibernate.c, arch/x86/power/hibernate_64.c, arch/x86/power/hibernate_asm_64.S, arch/arm64/kernel/hibernate.cswsusp_arch_suspend() saves the registers and calls swsusp_save(). swsusp_arch_resume() must overwrite the running kernel with the image without overwriting the code doing the copying. x86 copies core_restore_code to a safe page with relocate_restore_code() and runs it on temporary page tables. ARM64 builds a copy of the linear map with trans_pgd_create_copy() and relocates swsusp_arch_suspend_exit(). Compare the image headers: struct restore_data_record checks an e820 checksum, and struct arch_hibernate_hdr refuses an image from a different kernel build and records the MPIDR of the CPU that hibernated. hibernate_resume_nonboot_cpu_disable() (arch/x86/power/cpu.c and the ARM64 file) shows how each architecture parks the other CPUs before the jump, and ARM64’s swsusp_mte_save_tags() saves the memory tags that a page copy would lose.
Runtime PMdrivers/base/power/runtime.c, include/linux/pm_runtime.hrpm_check_suspend_allowed() is the best first read: eight lines that list every reason a device must stay on (an error, disabled runtime PM, a usage count, active children, a pending resume, or a zero PM QoS latency). Then read rpm_suspend() and rpm_resume() for the state machine. Look at how RPM_ASYNC hands work to pm_wq through pm_runtime_work(), how the autosuspend timer defers it, and how rpm_get_suppliers() resumes device-link suppliers first. __rpm_callback() shows why pm_runtime_irq_safe() pins the parent on. The many wrappers in pm_runtime.h are thin layers over three functions: __pm_runtime_idle(), __pm_runtime_suspend(), and __pm_runtime_resume().
Runtime PM in bus types and wake IRQsdrivers/pci/pci-driver.c, drivers/usb/core/driver.c, drivers/base/power/wakeirq.c, drivers/base/dd.cRead local_pci_probe() for the usage reference every PCI driver inherits, then pci_pm_runtime_suspend() and pci_finish_runtime_suspend() to see the bus save config space and choose D3hot or D3cold after the driver’s callback. In the USB core, follow usb_autopm_get_interface() to pm_runtime_resume_and_get() and read autosuspend_check() for the remote-wakeup rule. handle_threaded_wake_irq() and dev_pm_enable_wake_irq_check() show how a dedicated wake line resumes a runtime-suspended device. In driver_probe_device(), note the supplier and parent references held around probe.
Runtime PM and system sleepdrivers/base/power/main.c, drivers/base/power/runtime.cFind the pm_runtime_get_noresume() call in device_prepare(), the pm_runtime_disable() call in device_suspend_late(), and the matching re-enable in device_resume_early(); together these three keep runtime PM from acting during a transition.
