Then read pm_runtime_force_suspend() and pm_runtime_force_resume() to see how a driver can reuse its runtime callbacks for system sleep, and how needs_force_resume decides whether a device that was idle before the suspend has to be woken afterward.
ACPI device powerdrivers/base/power/common.c, drivers/acpi/device_pm.c, drivers/pci/pci-acpi.cdev_pm_domain_attach() tries ACPI first, then genpd. In device_pm.c, acpi_dev_pm_attach() installs acpi_general_pm_domain. Its callbacks, such as acpi_subsys_runtime_suspend(), call the driver and then acpi_dev_suspend(), which arms wakeup and chooses the lowest D-state the firmware allows. acpi_device_set_power() is where _PSx methods and power resources are actually evaluated. PCI keeps its own D-state logic and calls into ACPI only through acpi_pci_set_power_state().
Generic PM domainsdrivers/pmdomain/core.c, include/linux/pm_domain.hpm_genpd_init() creates a domain and of_genpd_add_provider_onecell() publishes it to the device tree. genpd_power_off() is worth reading line by line, because its early returns list every reason a domain stays on: a prepared transition, the always-on flag, active subdomains, or a device that is not runtime-suspended. Then the governor gets a veto. genpd_power_on() powers the parents first. genpd_runtime_suspend() and genpd_runtime_resume() show the domain wrapping the driver’s callbacks. genpd_suspend_noirq() and genpd_sync_power_off() are the system-sleep path, which runs without locks because only one CPU is left.
Multiple domains, boot hand-over, and debugfsdrivers/base/power/common.c, drivers/pmdomain/core.c, drivers/base/dd.cdev_pm_domain_attach_list() in common.c creates one virtual device per domain through genpd_dev_pm_attach_by_id() and links each to the consumer; the PD_FLAG_* bits are decoded there. In core.c, pm_genpd_init() and genpd_set_stay_on() show which domains wait for sync_state(), of_genpd_add_provider_simple() and genpd_provider_sync_state() show how that callback is wired up, and genpd_power_off_unused() is the late_initcall_sync sweep. genpd_debug_add() and idle_states_show() build /sys/kernel/debug/pm_genpd/. In dd.c, device_unbind_cleanup() shows the 6.17 detach order.
Domain governors and performance statesdrivers/pmdomain/governor.c, drivers/cpuidle/cpuidle-psci-domain.c, drivers/pmdomain/qcom/rpmhpd.cIn governor.c, default_power_down_ok() walks the domain’s idle states against the QoS limits of its devices, and cpu_power_down_ok() adds the next hrtimer expiry of every CPU in a cluster. cpuidle-psci-domain.c shows how a CPU cluster becomes a genpd, and how psci_pd_power_off() records the state the last CPU will pass to firmware. For performance states, read dev_pm_genpd_set_performance_state() and _genpd_reeval_performance_state() in core.c (maximum over devices and subdomains, then propagation to parents). rpmhpd.c shows the provider side, turning a level into a request to Qualcomm’s RPMh.
The cpufreq coredrivers/cpufreq/cpufreq.c, include/linux/cpufreq.h, kernel/power/qos.ccpufreq_online() builds a policy for the first CPU of a group and attaches the others. cpufreq_set_policy() applies the limits: policy->min and ->max are the aggregate of the frequency QoS requests that freq_qos_add_request() registers. Compare __cpufreq_driver_target(), which may sleep and wraps the change in cpufreq_freq_transition_begin() notifiers, with cpufreq_driver_fast_switch(), which is called with the runqueue lock held and so cannot sleep. struct cpufreq_driver in the header shows the three styles of driver: ->target_index(), ->setpolicy(), and ->adjust_perf().
Policy lifecycle, frequency tables, and driver flagsdrivers/cpufreq/cpufreq.c, drivers/cpufreq/freq_table.c, drivers/cpufreq/cpufreq_stats.c, include/linux/cpufreq.hcpufreq_register_driver() checks the callbacks, enables frequency invariance for drivers that set frequencies, and hooks the cpufreq:online hotplug state; cpufreq_policy_online() and cpufreq_offline() keep a policy alive across hotplug. cpufreq_table_validate_and_sort() and the cpufreq_table_find_index_*() helpers implement the relations, cpufreq_boost_trigger_state() and store_local_boost() the boost rules, and cpufreq_stats_record_transition() the stats/ files.
schedutilkernel/sched/cpufreq_schedutil.c, include/linux/sched/cpufreq.hsugov_update_single_freq() and sugov_update_shared() run from the scheduler’s cpufreq_update_util() hook with the runqueue lock held. The 25% headroom is map_util_perf() in the header, applied in sugov_effective_cpu_perf(). get_next_freq() then scales the result by get_capacity_ref_freq() and caches it, so repeated equal requests cost nothing. sugov_should_update_freq() applies the rate limit, while limits_changed and need_freq_update force a re-evaluation after a QoS change. On the slow path, sugov_deferred_update() and sugov_work() wake the sugov:N kthread, which runs as SCHED_DEADLINE.
Scaling drivers and OPPdrivers/cpufreq/intel_pstate.c, drivers/cpufreq/amd-pstate.c, drivers/cpufreq/cppc_cpufreq.c, drivers/cpufreq/scmi-cpufreq.c, drivers/cpufreq/cpufreq-dt.c, drivers/opp/core.cContrast what each driver does with a request. intel_pstate_update_util_hwp() leaves the decision to the processor; it acts only when hwp_dynamic_boost is set, raising the HWP minimum after repeated I/O-wait wakeups. amd_pstate_adjust_perf() and intel_cpufreq_adjust_perf() take schedutil’s utilization directly as performance levels. cppc_cpufreq_set_target() writes the ACPI CPPC desired-performance register, and scmi_cpufreq_fast_switch() sends a message to the SCMI firmware. On a plain device-tree SoC, cpufreq-dt.c’s set_target() calls dev_pm_opp_set_rate(). Read _set_opp() in drivers/opp/core.c for its ordering rule: when scaling up, required OPPs, bandwidth, and voltage are raised before the clock; when scaling down, after it.
Frequency invariancearch/x86/kernel/cpu/aperfmperf.c, arch/arm64/kernel/topology.c, drivers/base/arch_topology.cOn x86, arch_scale_freq_tick() and scale_freq_tick() derive the per-CPU arch_freq_scale from the APERF/MPERF deltas on each tick. The *_set_max_freq_ratio() helpers estimate the turbo ratio that defines “100%”, and disable_freq_invariance_workfn() is the fallback when the counters misbehave. On ARM64, amu_scale_freq_tick() does the same with AMU counters. topology_set_freq_scale() in arch_topology.c is the software path that cpufreq drivers use, and it does nothing once a counter source has been registered with topology_set_scale_freq_source(). arch_freq_get_on_cpu() backs cpuinfo_avg_freq.
The idle loopkernel/sched/idle.c, drivers/cpuidle/cpuidle.c, include/linux/cpuidle.hFollow do_idle() into cpuidle_idle_call(), and notice the three exits before a governor is consulted: no cpuidle driver (default_idle_call()), a forced idle-injection state, and s2idle. Then follow cpuidle_select(), the decision to stop or keep the tick, and cpuidle_enter_state(). That function measures the real residency, stores it in last_residency_ns, and counts the above and below misses that appear in sysfs as evidence of bad predictions; cpuidle_reflect() feeds the result back to the governor.
cpuidle registration, sysfs, and the polling statedrivers/cpuidle/driver.c, drivers/cpuidle/sysfs.c, drivers/cpuidle/poll_state.c, drivers/cpuidle/cpuidle-haltpoll.c, drivers/cpuidle/governors/haltpoll.c__cpuidle_driver_init() converts each state's microsecond figures to nanoseconds, sets bctimer for CPUIDLE_FLAG_TIMER_STOP states, and warns when exit latency exceeds target residency. In sysfs.c, store_state_disable() sets the user-disable bit and show_state_default_status() reports CPUIDLE_FLAG_OFF. poll_idle() checks the clock only every 200 iterations and gives up after cpuidle_poll_time(), setting poll_time_limit for the governor. adjust_poll_limit() in the haltpoll governor grows and shrinks a guest's poll window.
Idle governorsdrivers/cpuidle/governors/menu.c, drivers/cpuidle/governors/teo.c, drivers/cpuidle/governor.cmenu_select() starts from tick_nohz_get_sleep_length() and corrects it with get_typical_interval(), which looks for a stable pattern in the last eight idle durations. teo’s long header comment is the best description of its algorithm anywhere. teo_update() sorts each wakeup into a “hit” (the timer fired as predicted) or an “intercept” (something else woke the CPU first), and teo_select() uses those decaying counts to choose a shallower state when intercepts dominate. Both call cpuidle_governor_latency_req() to respect PM QoS.
Idle driversdrivers/idle/intel_idle.c, drivers/acpi/processor_idle.c, drivers/cpuidle/cpuidle-psci.c, drivers/cpuidle/dt_idle_states.c, kernel/cpu_pm.cCompare intel_idle() with its per-model tables such as spr_cstates (MWAIT hints and hand-measured latencies), acpi_idle_enter() (whatever _CST says), and psci_enter_idle_state() (PSCI CPU_SUSPEND with a parameter from the device tree, parsed by dt_init_idle_driver()). __psci_enter_domain_idle_state() is where a CPU in OS-initiated mode drops its cluster reference. cpu_pm_enter() runs the notifiers that save the GIC and PMU state a power-down state would lose.
PM QoSkernel/power/qos.c, include/linux/pm_qos.h, drivers/base/power/qos.cpm_qos_update_target() is the single aggregation engine: a priority list whose minimum or maximum is the effective constraint. cpu_latency_qos_add_request() uses it for the global CPU latency limit, and cpu_latency_qos_apply() calls wake_up_all_idle_cpus() when the effective limit changes, so sleeping CPUs re-select their idle states. cpu_latency_qos_open() shows that the /dev/cpu_dma_latency request lasts exactly as long as the file stays open. freq_qos_add_request() and freq_qos_apply() drive cpufreq’s min and max. drivers/base/power/qos.c holds the per-device resume-latency constraints that the runtime PM and genpd governors consult.
The Energy Modelkernel/power/energy_model.c, include/linux/energy_model.h, drivers/opp/of.c, kernel/power/em_netlink.cem_dev_register_perf_domain() calls em_create_perf_table(), which asks the driver’s active_power() for each frequency, and em_compute_costs(), which precomputes cost and flags inefficient states. em_cpu_energy() in the header is the formula energy-aware scheduling evaluates for every candidate CPU. em_dev_update_perf_domain() swaps the table under RCU at run time. dev_pm_opp_of_register_em() in of.c is the device-tree estimate from dynamic-power-coefficient. em_netlink.c (6.19) exports the model to user space.
Thermal coredrivers/thermal/thermal_core.c, drivers/thermal/thermal_trip.c, drivers/thermal/thermal_of.cthermal_zone_device_register_with_trips() creates a zone. __thermal_zone_device_update() is the heart of the core: it reads the temperature, sorts the trips into crossed-up and crossed-down lists, notifies the governor once per crossing, and reprograms the hardware trip window through thermal_zone_set_trips(). handle_critical_trips(), thermal_zone_device_critical(), and thermal_zone_device_halt() lead into __hw_protection_trigger() in kernel/reboot.c. thermal_of_zone_register() shows how a device-tree thermal-zones node becomes a zone with its trips and cooling maps.
Thermal interfaces to user spacedrivers/thermal/thermal_sysfs.c, drivers/thermal/thermal_netlink.c, drivers/thermal/thermal_thresholds.c, drivers/thermal/thermal_debugfs.c, drivers/thermal/intel/intel_hfi.cthermal_zone_create_device_groups() builds the thermal_zoneN attributes, including the trip_point_N_* files that become writable only for trips flagged THERMAL_TRIP_FLAG_RW_TEMP. thermal_genl_sampling_temp() and the thermal_notify_*() helpers feed the sampling and event multicast groups, and thermal_genl_cmd_threshold_add() checks CAP_SYS_ADMIN before thermal_thresholds_add(); thermal_thresholds_handle() folds the thresholds into the window passed to ->set_trips(). thermal_debug_tz_trip_up() and thermal_debug_tz_trip_down() record mitigation episodes. In intel_hfi.c, hfi_update_work_fn() and update_capabilities() turn the hardware feedback table into CPU_CAPABILITY_CHANGE events.
Governors and cooling devicesdrivers/thermal/gov_step_wise.c, drivers/thermal/gov_power_allocator.c, drivers/thermal/cpufreq_cooling.c, drivers/thermal/cpuidle_cooling.cstep_wise_manage() moves each cooling device one state at a time. power_allocator_manage() runs the PID controller, and allocate_power() splits the budget among actors in proportion to their weighted requests. In cpufreq_cooling.c, cpufreq_set_cur_state() turns a cooling state into a frequency QoS cap, and the ->state2power()/->power2state() pair reads the Energy Model. cpuidle_cooling.c cools by forcing idle through drivers/powercap/idle_inject.c and play_idle_precise(), which is why the idle loop has a forced-idle exit.
Powercapdrivers/powercap/powercap_sys.c, drivers/powercap/intel_rapl_common.c, drivers/powercap/intel_rapl_msr.c, drivers/powercap/dtpm.c, drivers/powercap/arm_scmi_powercap.cpowercap_register_control_type() and powercap_register_zone() build the /sys/class/powercap tree. In the RAPL driver, rapl_add_package() discovers the domains of a package, and the MSR and TPMI back ends supply register access; the processor enforces the limit itself. dtpm.c is the software alternative: dtpm_register() and dtpm_create_hierarchy() build a tree whose leaves use the Energy Model to turn a power limit into a frequency cap: a frequency QoS request for a CPU policy, a device PM QoS request for a devfreq device. arm_scmi_powercap.c passes limits to firmware. rapl_package_add_pmu() lets the RAPL driver itself register the power perf PMU, for TPMI parts (6.10) and, through rapl_package_add_pmu_locked(), for the MSR back end on newer client parts (6.19).

Chapter 22 — Networking

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
Socket system calls and sockfsnet/socket.c, include/linux/net.hStart with __sys_socket(), which calls __sock_create() to find the family in net_families[] (filled by sock_register()) and then sock_alloc_file() to wrap the new struct socket in a file whose f_op is socket_file_ops. sock_alloc() takes the socket from the sockfs inode cache (sock_alloc_inode()), so a socket and its inode are a single allocation. Then read __sys_sendto() and ___sys_sendmsg(): each resolves the descriptor with sock_from_file(), copies the address and iovec in, and ends in __sock_sendmsg(), which runs the LSM hook and calls sock->ops->sendmsg. sock_recvmsg() is the mirror image, and __sys_accept4() shows a new socket and file being created for each accepted connection.
struct sock, memory accounting and lockinginclude/net/sock.h, net/core/sock.c, net/core/stream.cRead struct sock with its struct sock_common head, then struct proto, whose object size and slab let sk_alloc() → sk_prot_alloc() allocate a whole TCP or UDP socket as one object. The locking model is lock_sock_nested() and release_sock(): while a task owns the socket (sock_owned_by_user()), softirq receive appends packets with sk_add_backlog(), and __release_sock() replays them through sk_backlog_rcv before the lock is dropped. lock_sock_fast() avoids setting the owner when nobody contends. For memory, follow sk_wmem_schedule() and sk_rmem_schedule() into __sk_mem_raise_allocated(), which charges the protocol’s memory_allocated and the socket’s memcg, and read sk_stream_wait_memory(), where a sender sleeps when its buffer is full.
INET sockets and the socket hash tablesnet/ipv4/af_inet.c, net/ipv4/inet_hashtables.c, net/ipv4/inet_connection_sock.cinet_create() searches inetsw[] (filled from inetsw_array) for the type and protocol, sets sock->ops to inet_stream_ops or inet_dgram_ops, and calls the protocol’s init. inet_init() is the boot-time registration of IPv4: tcp_prot and udp_prot, the inet_protos[] handlers and the IP packet type. inet_bind(), inet_stream_connect() and inet_accept() are the generic halves of the connection calls. In the hash tables, __inet_lookup_established() finds a connected socket for an incoming segment and __inet_lookup_listener() is the fallback; inet_csk_get_port() assigns the local port on bind(), and __inet_hash_connect() picks an ephemeral port on connect().
Drop accounting and socket introspectionnet/core/net-procfs.c, net/ipv4/proc.c, include/trace/events/skb.h, net/core/drop_monitor.c, net/core/sock_diag.c, net/ipv4/inet_diag.c, net/ipv4/tcp_diag.cStart with softnet_seq_show() in net-procfs.c, which prints one line of /proc/net/softnet_stat per CPU from struct softnet_data, including the six zero columns kept for compatibility; the same file prints /proc/net/dev with dev_seq_show(). snmp_seq_show() and netstat_seq_show() in net/ipv4/proc.c turn the per-namespace MIB arrays into /proc/net/snmp and /proc/net/netstat, each counter named by a struct snmp_mib table. skb.h under include/trace/events defines the skb:kfree_skb tracepoint with its location, reason and receiving-socket fields. drop_monitor.c is the in-kernel consumer behind dropwatch: it attaches to that tracepoint and to devlink:devlink_trap_report for packets dropped in hardware, and reports through the NET_DM generic netlink family either as per-location summaries or as whole packets. For sockets, __sock_diag_cmd() dispatches a NETLINK_SOCK_DIAG request to the handler a family registered with sock_diag_register(). inet_diag.c parses the request, runs the filter bytecode ss compiles (inet_diag_bc_run()) and fills each reply with inet_sk_diag_fill(), and tcp_diag.c adds tcp_info, congestion-control and ULP state and implements SOCK_DESTROY with tcp_diag_destroy().
The socket bufferinclude/linux/skbuff.h, net/core/skbuff.c, include/net/dropreason-core.hRead struct sk_buff from the top: the list and rbtree union, dev, the cb[] control block that each layer overlays with its own structure, then the header offsets and the head/data/tail/end quartet. The inline skb_reserve(), skb_put(), skb_push() and skb_pull() show that headers are added and stripped by moving data, not by copying. In skbuff.c, __alloc_skb() takes the skb from a slab cache and the data from kmalloc_reserve(), while napi_build_skb() wraps a buffer the driver has already filled. skb_clone() shares the data; pskb_expand_head() and skb_cow_head() make it private when a layer must write a shared header. Packets end in consume_skb() or sk_skb_reason_drop(), whose enum skb_drop_reason drop monitors can trace.
Paged data and checksum stateinclude/linux/skbuff.h, net/core/skbuff.c, net/core/datagram.c, net/core/dev.cstruct skb_shared_info sits at end and holds frags[] (up to MAX_SKB_FRAGS, 17 by default), frag_list, the GSO fields and the dataref count. Drivers attach received pages with skb_add_rx_frag(), and __pskb_pull_tail() copies bytes from the fragments into the linear area when a parser needs a contiguous header. The “DOC: skb checksums” comment near the top of skbuff.h is the specification of ip_summed in both directions. On transmit, skb_checksum_help() finishes a CHECKSUM_PARTIAL packet in software. On receive, __skb_checksum_complete() verifies a checksum the hardware did not, and skb_copy_and_csum_datagram_msg() folds the verification into the copy to user space.
Page fragment cachemm/page_frag_cache.c, include/linux/page_frag_cache.h__page_frag_alloc_align() carves small buffers out of a high-order page that __page_frag_cache_refill() obtains. Instead of touching the page reference count once per fragment, it adds a large bias to the count when it takes the page and then decrements a private pagecnt_bias; the two are reconciled only when the page is used up. page_frag_free() drops one fragment’s reference, and __page_frag_cache_drain() releases a cache. It is the allocator behind napi_alloc_skb() and __netdev_alloc_skb(), which keep one cache per CPU (netdev_alloc_cache and the NAPI cache in skbuff.c) for drivers that do not use page_pool.
Checksum routines on x86-64 and ARM64arch/x86/lib/csum-partial_64.c, arch/x86/lib/csum-copy_64.S, arch/x86/include/asm/checksum_64.h, arch/arm64/lib/csum.c, arch/arm64/include/asm/checksum.h, lib/checksum.c, include/net/checksum.hThis is where the two architectures differ most in the stack. x86’s csum_partial() is an adc chain over 64-bit words, and csum-copy_64.S has the assembly csum_partial_copy_generic() used when checksumming while copying from user space. ARM64 instead uses the generic csum_partial() in lib/checksum.c and overrides only do_csum(), plain C that loads 128 bits at a time and folds carries by rotation. ARM64’s csum.c also holds csum_ipv6_magic(). Each architecture keeps ip_fast_csum() and csum_fold() in its checksum.h. include/net/checksum.h has the architecture-neutral helpers such as csum_add(), csum_sub() and csum_block_add(), used throughout the stack to combine partial sums and adjust a checksum without recomputing it.
Network devicesinclude/linux/netdevice.h, net/core/dev.c, net/core/dev_api.c, include/net/netdev_lock.h, net/core/link_watch.c, net/ethernet/eth.cSkim struct net_device, whose hot fields are grouped by access pattern (net_device_read_tx, net_device_read_txrx, net_device_read_rx), and struct net_device_ops, the methods a driver implements. alloc_netdev_mqs() allocates the device with its private area and its TX and RX queue arrays. register_netdevice() validates the features, assigns an ifindex, creates the sysfs entries and sends NETDEV_REGISTER to notifiers. __dev_open() calls ndo_open, and dev_activate() attaches the qdiscs. netif_close() and unregister_netdevice_many() undo all of this, and netdev_run_todo() waits for the last reference before the device is freed. netif_carrier_on() is how a driver reports that the link is up. It queues the device for linkwatch_event(), which computes operstate in rfc2863_policy() and activates or deactivates the qdiscs. dev_get_stats() merges driver, per-CPU and core counters. netdev_need_ops_lock() decides whether a device’s operations run under its instance lock, and the dev_*() wrappers in dev_api.c take that lock around the netif_*() functions. ether_setup() supplies the Ethernet defaults, and rtnl_newlink_create() in net/core/rtnetlink.c creates software devices through their rtnl_link_ops.
Device features, ethtool and the netdev familynet/core/dev.c, net/ethtool/netlink.c, net/ethtool/features.c, net/core/netdev-genl.c, Documentation/netlink/specs/netdev.yaml__netdev_update_features() computes the enabled feature set from hw_features, wanted_features and the dependencies between flags, passes it through the driver’s ndo_fix_features, and applies it with ndo_set_features. ethnl_set_features() is the ethtool -K path into it. The ethtool netlink family (ethtool_genl_family) routes most commands through ethnl_default_doit() and ethnl_default_dumpit() with per-command request operations. netdev-genl.c implements the newer netdev family, generated from netdev.yaml: netdev_nl_dev_get_doit() reports XDP features, netdev_nl_napi_get_doit() and netdev_nl_napi_set_doit() expose per-NAPI settings, netdev_nl_queue_get_doit() the queue-to-NAPI mapping, and netdev_nl_bind_rx_doit() binds a devmem dma-buf. Adapter-wide configuration (firmware, health reporters, ports, eswitch mode) lives in the devlink family under net/devlink/.
Multiqueue and queue objectsinclude/linux/netdevice.h, include/net/netdev_rx_queue.h, include/net/netdev_queues.h, net/core/net-sysfs.c, net/core/dev.c, net/core/netdev_rx_queue.cstruct netdev_queue holds the per-TX-queue state (qdisc, xmit lock, BQL, stop bits) and struct netdev_rx_queue the per-RX-queue state (RPS map, flow table, mp_params). netif_set_real_num_tx_queues() changes how many queues are in use. netdev_core_pick_tx() calls the driver’s ndo_select_queue or netdev_pick_tx(), which honours the socket’s cached queue and the XPS map. net-sysfs.c implements /sys/class/net/*/queues/, where store_rps_map() and xps_cpus_store() parse the CPU masks. netdev_queues.h has the stop and wake helpers (netif_txq_maybe_stop(), netif_txq_completed_wake()) and struct netdev_queue_mgmt_ops, which lets the core restart a single RX queue: netdev_rx_queue_restart() calls netdev_rx_queue_reconfig(), which allocates the new ring memory, stops the queue, starts it with the new memory, and restarts it with the old memory on failure. netif_get_num_default_rss_queues() picks the default queue count.
NAPI and the receive softirqnet/core/dev.c, include/linux/netdevice.h, include/net/busy_poll.hStart with napi_schedule_prep() and ____napi_schedule(), which put a NAPI instance on the per-CPU poll list and raise NET_RX_SOFTIRQ. net_rx_action() works through that list until netdev_budget (300 packets) or netdev_budget_usecs runs out, calling napi_poll() → __napi_poll(), which invokes the driver’s poll function with a weight of usually NAPI_POLL_WEIGHT (64). The driver finishes with napi_complete_done(), which arms the napi_watchdog() hrtimer instead of re-enabling the interrupt when IRQ deferral is configured. napi_threaded_poll() is the kthread loop of threaded NAPI (netif_set_threaded()), and napi_busy_loop(), reached from sk_busy_loop(), polls from the application’s own context. netif_napi_add_weight_locked(), napi_enable_locked() and napi_disable_locked() are the lifecycle, and napi_threaded_poll_loop() the shared body of the threaded and threaded-busy-poll kthreads.
GROnet/core/gro.c, include/net/gro.h, net/ipv4/af_inet.c, net/ipv4/tcp_offload.c, net/ipv4/udp_offload.cnapi_gro_receive() calls gro_receive_skb(), and dev_gro_receive() hashes the flow into one of the GRO_HASH_BUCKETS lists of the NAPI’s struct gro_node and calls the protocol’s gro_receive callback chain. inet_gro_receive() checks the IP header, then tcp4_gro_receive() → tcp_gro_receive() decides whether the segment continues a held packet. A list holds at most MAX_GRO_SKBS (8) packets before gro_flush_oldest() completes the oldest, and __gro_flush() runs at the end of every NAPI poll. udp4_gro_receive() covers UDP GRO for tunnels and QUIC, and napi_gro_frags() is the variant for drivers that pass page fragments without an skb. netif_elide_gro() in include/linux/netdevice.h decides when GRO is skipped, and net/core/gro_cells.c (gro_cells_receive()) gives tunnel devices their own per-CPU NAPI so decapsulated packets get GRO too.
RPS, RFS and the backlognet/core/dev.c, include/net/rps.hget_rps_cpu() picks the target CPU from the RX queue’s RPS map by skb->hash, or, with RFS, from the global socket flow table that sock_rps_record_flow() updates whenever an application touches a socket. set_rps_cpu() moves a flow only after its old CPU has drained the flow’s packets, so ordering is kept, and calls ndo_rx_flow_steer for accelerated RFS. enqueue_to_backlog() appends the packet to the target CPU’s input_pkt_queue, bounded by netdev_max_backlog (1000), and signals that CPU with an IPI. There, process_backlog() runs as a pseudo-NAPI poll function. rps_may_expire_flow() lets drivers retire hardware filters, and rps_flow_is_active() stops a flow from being moved while its old CPU still has packets queued. skb_flow_limit() is the per-CPU flow limiter that drops packets of a dominant flow once the backlog is half full.
Protocol demultiplexingnet/core/dev.c, include/linux/netdevice.h__netif_receive_skb_core() is the heart of receive; read it in order. It runs generic XDP, strips the VLAN tag, and delivers to the ptype_all taps through deliver_skb(). Then come sch_handle_ingress() for tc and its BPF programs, nf_ingress(), and the rx_handler that a bridge, bond or macvlan installs with netdev_rx_handler_register(). Last is the ptype_base hash, filled by dev_add_pack(), which delivers to ip_rcv() or ipv6_rcv(). netif_receive_skb_list_internal() and __netif_receive_skb_list_core() are the list variants that let IP process a batch with one handler call. netif_rx(), which queues to the backlog, remains the entry point for sources that are not NAPI drivers. Inside the core loop, do_xdp_generic() → netif_receive_generic_xdp() runs generic XDP after netif_skb_check_for_xdp() copies the skb into system page_pool pages if needed; sch_handle_ingress() runs tcx_run() before classic tc; vlan_do_receive() in net/8021q/vlan_core.c hands tagged frames to VLAN devices; and skb_pfmemalloc_protocol() limits which handlers may see packets allocated from emergency reserves.
IPv4 input and forwardingnet/ipv4/ip_input.c, net/ipv4/ip_forward.c, net/ipv4/ip_fragment.c, net/ipv4/icmp.cFollow ip_rcv() → ip_rcv_core() (header and checksum validation) → the NF_INET_PRE_ROUTING hook → ip_rcv_finish(), whose ip_rcv_finish_core() attaches a dst through a route hint, early demux or ip_route_input_noref(), then parses options in ip_rcv_options(). The dst’s input method then decides the packet’s fate. ip_local_deliver() reassembles fragments with ip_defrag() and passes NF_INET_LOCAL_IN, and ip_protocol_deliver_rcu() calls the transport handler from inet_protos[]. ip_forward() checks the TTL and MTU and passes NF_INET_FORWARD, and ip_forward_finish() hands the packet to dst_output(). ip_list_rcv() is the batched version, which groups consecutive packets that share a dst. In icmp.c, icmp_rcv() dispatches through icmp_pointers[]; icmp_unreach() → icmp_socket_deliver() hands errors such as Fragmentation Needed to the protocol’s err_handler; and __icmp_send() applies the global and per-peer rate limits (icmpv4_global_allow(), icmpv4_xrlim_allow()).
IPv4 outputnet/ipv4/ip_output.cConnected sockets transmit through ip_queue_xmit() → __ip_queue_xmit(), which reuses the route cached in the socket, builds the IP header (DF, TTL, and the ID from ip_select_ident_segs() in include/net/ip.h) and calls ip_local_out(), where __ip_local_out() runs NF_INET_LOCAL_OUT. Datagram sockets build packets with ip_append_data() and ip_make_skb() instead. Both reach ip_output(), which runs NF_INET_POST_ROUTING, and ip_finish_output(). Its helper __ip_finish_output() validates GSO packets in ip_finish_output_gso() and fragments through ip_fragment() → ip_do_fragment() when a non-GSO packet exceeds the MTU. ip_finish_output2() makes sure there is headroom for the link-layer header, finds the neighbour with ip_neigh_for_gw(), and calls neigh_output().
Routing and the FIBinclude/net/ip_fib.h, net/ipv4/fib_trie.c, net/ipv4/fib_frontend.c, net/ipv4/route.c, net/ipv4/nexthop.c, net/ipv4/fib_semantics.c, net/core/fib_rules.cfib_lookup() (inline in ip_fib.h) consults the policy-routing rules (fib_rules_lookup() in net/core/fib_rules.c), or goes straight to the main table when there are no custom rules, and fib_table_lookup() walks the LC-trie. fib_table_insert() and resize() show how the trie stays compact as routes change. In route.c, ip_route_input_slow() and ip_route_output_key_hash_rcu() turn a fib_result into a struct rtable. rt_cache_route() caches that rtable in the nexthop (nhc_rth_input, nhc_pcpu_rth_output), the replacement for the old routing cache. rt_cache_valid() and rt_genid_bump_ipv4() invalidate those cached routes by generation number, and update_or_create_fnhe() records per-destination exceptions such as a learned PMTU. fib_validate_source() implements reverse-path filtering. In fib_semantics.c, fib_select_path() → fib_select_multipath() picks an ECMP nexthop by flow hash, and fib_add_ifaddr() in fib_frontend.c creates the local, broadcast and prefix routes for a new address. net/l3mdev/l3mdev.c holds the VRF hooks, and nexthop.c holds the separate nexthop objects of Linux 5.3.
Neighbours and ARPnet/core/neighbour.c, include/net/neighbour.h, net/ipv4/arp.cneigh_output() (inline) is the transmit fast path: if the entry is connected and has a cached hardware header, neigh_hh_output() copies it in front of the packet and calls dev_queue_xmit(). Otherwise neigh_resolve_output() calls __neigh_event_send(), which queues the packet on the entry and starts resolution. The NUD state machine lives in neigh_timer_handler() (probe, delay and failure transitions) and neigh_update() (new information from replies or netlink), and neigh_periodic_work() garbage-collects stale entries, while neigh_alloc() enforces gc_thresh2/gc_thresh3 by calling neigh_forced_gc(). In arp.c, arp_tbl is the IPv4 table, arp_solicit() sends requests, and arp_rcv() → arp_process() answers requests and feeds replies into neigh_update().
IPv6net/ipv6/ip6_input.c, net/ipv6/ip6_output.c, net/ipv6/route.c, net/ipv6/ip6_fib.c, net/ipv6/ndisc.c, net/ipv6/addrconf.cRead these files side by side with their IPv4 counterparts. Input is ipv6_rcv() → ip6_rcv_core() → ip6_rcv_finish() → ip6_input() → ip6_protocol_deliver_rcu(), which also dispatches extension headers through the protocol table; Hop-by-Hop options are parsed early by ipv6_parse_hopopts(). Output is ip6_xmit() → ip6_output() → ip6_finish_output2(). Only the sender fragments, in ip6_fragment(); ip6_forward() answers an oversized packet with ICMPv6 Packet Too Big. ip6_fib.c is the radix tree (fib6_add(), fib6_node_lookup()), and ip6_pol_route() in route.c hands out per-CPU route copies made by rt6_make_pcpu_route(). rt6_insert_exception() stores PMTU and redirect exceptions, and ip6_dst_check() validates a cached route against the fn_sernum cookie from fib6_get_cookie_safe(). In Linux 7.0, ip6_xmit() no longer inserts a Hop-by-Hop jumbo option for BIG TCP packets; it sets payload_len to 0. Neighbour discovery replaces ARP in ndisc.c (ndisc_rcv(), ndisc_send_ns(), nd_tbl), and addrconf.c handles address autoconfiguration and duplicate address detection (addrconf_dad_work()).
Transmit path and qdiscsnet/core/dev.c, include/net/sch_generic.h, net/sched/sch_generic.c, net/sched/sch_api.c, net/sched/sch_mq.c__dev_queue_xmit() runs the egress hooks (sch_handle_egress()), picks a queue with netdev_core_pick_tx(), and calls __dev_xmit_skb(), which either bypasses an empty work-conserving qdisc or enqueues and runs __qdisc_run(). That loops through qdisc_restart() → sch_direct_xmit() → dev_hard_start_xmit() → xmit_one(), and validate_xmit_skb() does software GSO and checksums just before the driver. net_tx_action() reschedules qdiscs that ran out of quota. In sch_generic.c, dev_activate() attaches the default qdisc (on multiqueue devices mq from sch_mq.c, with one child per queue) and dev_watchdog() detects stuck queues. struct Qdisc_ops and register_qdisc() are the plug-in interface. Queue selection continues in netdev_pick_tx(), get_xps_queue() and skb_tx_hash(); sk_tx_queue_get() holds the Linux 6.19 reselection timeout. In __dev_xmit_skb(), follow the lockless path (qdisc_run_begin() and its MISSED retry, in sch_generic.h) and the 6.19 defer_list batching for locked qdiscs. dequeue_skb() shows the dequeue order (gso_skb, skb_bad_txq, then the qdisc) and the bulk dequeue bounded by BQL.
Fair-queueing qdiscsnet/sched/sch_fq_codel.c, include/net/codel_impl.h, net/sched/sch_fq.c, net/sched/sch_generic.cfq_codel_enqueue() hashes each packet into one of its flows with fq_codel_classify() and drops from the fattest flow when the qdisc is over its limit. fq_codel_dequeue() runs deficit round robin over new and old flows and applies CoDel through codel_dequeue(), whose codel_should_drop() compares each packet’s sojourn time with the 5 ms target. sch_fq.c keeps one flow per socket in red-black trees: fq_enqueue() reads each packet’s departure time (skb->tstamp, set by TCP pacing), and fq_dequeue() calls fq_check_throttled() to release flows whose time has come. For contrast, pfifo_fast_dequeue() in sch_generic.c is the old three-band default. fq_init() lists the fq defaults: a 10,000-packet limit, 100 packets per flow, a 10 s horizon and three weighted bands.
Byte Queue Limits and TX completioninclude/linux/netdevice.h, include/linux/dynamic_queue_limits.h, lib/dynamic_queue_limits.c, include/net/netdev_queues.hDrivers call netdev_tx_sent_queue() as they post packets and netdev_tx_completed_queue() from TX completion; these wrap dql_queued() and dql_completed(). Read dql_completed() slowly. It raises the limit when the hardware ran dry while packets were still waiting (starvation), and lowers it gradually when the limit stayed larger than needed for a hold time, converging on the smallest amount of in-flight data that keeps the link busy. When dql_avail() goes negative the stack stops the queue, and the completion path wakes it again. netif_txq_completed_wake() and netif_txq_maybe_stop() package the stop-and-wake race handling that drivers used to get wrong. __netdev_tx_sent_queue() is the batching variant that tells the driver when to ring the doorbell, and dql_check_stall() is the stall detector behind stall_thrs.
GSO, TSO and BIG TCPinclude/net/gso.h, net/core/gso.c, net/core/skbuff.c, net/ipv4/tcp_offload.c, net/ipv4/udp_offload.c, net/core/dev.cnetif_skb_features() intersects the device’s features with what a packet needs. When the device cannot take the packet as it is, validate_xmit_skb() calls skb_gso_segment() (inline in gso.h) → __skb_gso_segment() → skb_mac_gso_segment(). The protocol callbacks do the real work: tcp4_gso_segment() → tcp_gso_segment() fixes sequence numbers and checksums segment by segment, and __udp_gso_segment() does the same for UDP GSO. The generic splitter is skb_segment() in skbuff.c, one of the longest and most intricate functions in the core. netif_set_tso_max_size() is where a driver advertises support for packets larger than 64 KB, which BIG TCP requires. gso_features_check() in dev.c withdraws offloads per packet (segment count, size, DF, IPv6 extension headers), and skb_segment_list() handles fraglist GSO by reusing the chained skbs.
TCP connection setupnet/ipv4/tcp_ipv4.c, net/ipv4/tcp_input.c, net/ipv4/tcp_minisocks.c, net/ipv4/inet_connection_sock.c, net/ipv4/syncookies.c, net/ipv4/tcp_fastopen.c, net/ipv4/inet_hashtables.c, net/ipv4/inet_timewait_sock.cOn the active side, tcp_v4_connect() finds a route, picks a port through __inet_hash_connect(), and calls tcp_connect() to send the SYN. On the passive side, a SYN reaching a listener goes through tcp_rcv_state_process() to tcp_conn_request(), which creates a small request socket, hashes it with inet_csk_reqsk_queue_hash_add(), and answers. tcp_try_fastopen() handles TCP Fast Open, and when the queue is full a syncookie carries the state in the sequence number until cookie_v4_check() rebuilds it from the ACK. The final ACK finds the request socket: tcp_check_req() validates it, tcp_v4_syn_recv_sock() → tcp_create_openreq_child() creates the full socket, and inet_csk_complete_hashdance() queues it for inet_csk_accept(). In inet_hashtables.c, __inet_hash_connect() shows the RFC 6056 port offset and the even/odd split with inet_csk_get_port(), and __inet_lookup_established() the lockless RCU walk of ehash. secure_tcp_seq_and_ts_off() chooses the ISN, cookie_init_timestamp() hides SYN-cookie options in the timestamp, and tcp_timewait_state_process() with tcp_twsk_unique() shows how TIME_WAIT answers late segments and when its 4-tuple may be reused.
TCP send pathnet/ipv4/tcp.c, net/ipv4/tcp_output.ctcp_sendmsg() takes the socket lock and calls tcp_sendmsg_locked(), which copies user data into the tail skb or new skbs (page fragments, or pinned user pages for MSG_ZEROCOPY), sleeps in sk_stream_wait_memory() when the send buffer is full, and finally calls tcp_push(). tcp_write_xmit() is where TCP decides what may leave: the congestion and receive windows, Nagle, tcp_tso_autosize() for the burst size, tcp_small_queue_check() for TSQ, and pacing. __tcp_transmit_skb() builds the TCP header and calls ip_queue_xmit(). When a packet leaves the host, tcp_wfree() schedules tcp_tsq_workfn() to send more, and tcp_release_cb() runs work deferred while a task owned the socket. tcp_mtu_probe() sends PLPMTUD probes, tcp_event_new_data_sent() moves a sent skb to the rtx rbtree, and tcp_ecn_send_syn() negotiates ECN or AccECN.
TCP receive pathnet/ipv4/tcp_ipv4.c, net/ipv4/tcp_input.c, net/ipv4/tcp.ctcp_v4_rcv() looks up the socket and either processes the segment with tcp_v4_do_rcv() or, if a task owns the socket, appends it to the backlog with tcp_add_backlog(), which coalesces adjacent segments. For an established socket, tcp_rcv_established() has a fast path guarded by header prediction (pred_flags) and a slow path through tcp_ack() and tcp_data_queue(). Out-of-order data goes into an rbtree in tcp_data_queue_ofo(), and tcp_ofo_queue() drains it when the hole fills. On the reader’s side, tcp_recvmsg_locked() copies to user space, tcp_cleanup_rbuf() decides whether to send a window update, and tcp_rcv_space_adjust() autotunes the receive buffer. tcp_zerocopy_receive() maps pages instead of copying. __tcp_ack_snd_check() decides between an immediate, delayed, deferred or compressed ACK, tcp_rcvbuf_grow() sizes the receive buffer, and tcp_inbound_hash() checks TCP-MD5 and TCP-AO signatures.
TCP timers and loss recoverynet/ipv4/tcp_timer.c, net/ipv4/tcp_recovery.c, net/ipv4/tcp_input.c, net/ipv4/tcp_output.ctcp_init_xmit_timers() sets up the retransmit, delayed-ACK and keepalive timers. tcp_write_timer_handler() dispatches the retransmit timer’s several meanings: the RTO (tcp_retransmit_timer(), which backs off exponentially from an initial RTO of one second), the RACK reordering timeout, the tail loss probe (tcp_send_loss_probe(), armed by tcp_schedule_loss_probe()), and the zero-window probe (tcp_probe_timer()). Loss detection on ACK arrival is in tcp_fastretrans_alert() and in RACK, where tcp_rack_detect_loss() marks a packet lost once a packet sent after it has been acknowledged and a reordering window has passed. tcp_retransmit_skb() resends, and tcp_delack_timer_handler() and tcp_keepalive_timer() cover the other timers. tcp_identify_packet_loss() shows that SACK flows use only RACK since 6.17, with tcp_newreno_mark_lost() for the rest, and tcp_write_timeout() applies tcp_retries1, tcp_retries2, tcp_syn_retries and the linear SYN timeouts.
Congestion controlinclude/net/tcp.h, net/ipv4/tcp_cong.c, net/ipv4/tcp_cubic.c, net/ipv4/tcp_bbr.c, net/ipv4/bpf_tcp_ca.cstruct tcp_congestion_ops in tcp.h is the plug-in interface. tcp_register_congestion_control() adds an algorithm, and tcp_set_congestion_control() and tcp_init_congestion_control() attach one to a socket. tcp_cong.c also has the Reno building blocks: tcp_slow_start(), tcp_cong_avoid_ai() and tcp_reno_cong_avoid(). Compare CUBIC, a window-growth function (cubictcp_cong_avoid() → bictcp_update(), with HyStart in hystart_update()), with BBR, a model-based algorithm. bbr_main() is installed as cong_control and so replaces the window logic entirely, updating the bandwidth and RTT model in bbr_update_model() and setting the pacing rate with bbr_set_pacing_rate(). bpf_tcp_ca.c lets a BPF struct_ops program act as an algorithm.
UDPnet/ipv4/udp.c, net/ipv4/udp_offload.c, net/core/sock_reuseport.cOn transmit, udp_sendmsg() finds a route and, in the common uncorked case, builds the whole datagram with ip_make_skb() and sends it with udp_send_skb(). On receive, udp_rcv() → __udp4_lib_rcv() finds the socket with __udp4_lib_lookup() in the port and address hashes, and udp_queue_rcv_skb() → __udp_enqueue_schedule_skb() charges and queues the datagram under the receive-queue lock alone, without the socket lock. udp_recvmsg() dequeues through __skb_recv_udp(), which keeps a separate reader queue to reduce contention. udp_lib_get_port() binds ports. For load spreading, reuseport_add_sock() groups sockets and reuseport_select_sock() picks one by hash or with a BPF program. __udp_gso_segment() and udp4_gro_receive() implement UDP GSO and GRO. udp4_lib_lookup4() searches the 4-tuple hash added in 6.13, the per-NUMA udp_prod_queue batching (6.18) is at the top of __udp_enqueue_schedule_skb(), and udp_queue_rcv_one_skb() diverts tunnel traffic to encap_rcv.
Netfilter hooksinclude/linux/netfilter.h, net/netfilter/core.c, net/netfilter/nf_queue.c, net/netfilter/nfnetlink_queue.cNF_HOOK() calls nf_hook(), which tests a static key and returns at once when nothing is registered at that hook. Otherwise it fetches the namespace’s struct nf_hook_entries array, and nf_hook_slow() runs the callbacks in priority order, acting on each verdict (NF_ACCEPT, NF_DROP, NF_QUEUE, NF_STOLEN). struct nf_hook_ops describes one registration. nf_register_net_hook() → __nf_register_net_hook() builds a new array with nf_hook_entries_grow() and publishes it with RCU, so the packet path never takes a lock. nf_queue() hands a packet to user space for NFQUEUE, and nf_reinject() resumes the traversal with the verdict that comes back; an NF_REPEAT verdict calls the same entry again. nf_remove_net_hook() shows why unregistration cannot fail: it overwrites the entry with accept_all() before __nf_hook_entries_try_shrink() attempts a smaller copy. In nfnetlink_queue.c, __nfqnl_enqueue_packet() enforces the queue length and fail-open policy.
nf_tablesnet/netfilter/nf_tables_core.c, net/netfilter/nft_chain_filter.c, net/netfilter/nf_tables_api.c, net/netfilter/nft_set_hash.c, net/netfilter/nft_set_rbtree.c, net/netfilter/nft_set_pipapo.c, net/netfilter/nfnetlink.c, net/netfilter/nft_chain_route.c, net/netfilter/nf_tables_trace.cThe nf_tables data path is small. A base chain registers a hook whose callback (nft_do_chain_ipv4(), nft_do_chain_inet() and so on) calls nft_do_chain(), an interpreter over rules made of expressions that read and write a register file. The most common expressions, such as nft_payload_eval(), nft_cmp_eval() and nft_immediate_eval(), are called directly rather than through a function pointer. The control path is nf_tables_api.c: nf_tables_newtable(), nf_tables_newrule() and nf_tables_newset() stage objects in a transaction, and nf_tables_commit() or nf_tables_abort() applies or discards the whole batch atomically. Sets are pluggable. Compare nft_rhash_lookup(), nft_rbtree_lookup() and nft_pipapo_lookup(); pipapo is a bitmap-based algorithm for matching ranges across several fields. nft_select_set_ops() picks among them from each backend’s estimate(). nfnetlink_rcv_batch() frames the batch, takes commit_mutex and replays it after module autoload (-EAGAIN). nf_tables_validate() catches loops and over-deep jumps. nf_route_table_hook4() is the route chain type, and nft_trace_notify() emits the events that nft monitor trace prints.
x_tables and flowtablesnet/netfilter/x_tables.c, net/ipv4/netfilter/ip_tables.c, net/netfilter/nft_flow_offload.c, net/netfilter/nf_flow_table_core.c, net/netfilter/nf_flow_table_ip.cipt_do_table() is the legacy iptables engine: a linear walk over a blob of rules that user space replaces wholesale through xt_replace_table(), with matches and targets registered by xt_register_match() and xt_register_target(). Reading it after nft_do_chain() shows why nf_tables replaced it. The flowtable is a software fast path for forwarded traffic. Once a connection is established, an nf_tables flow add rule (nft_flow_offload.c) calls flow_offload_add(). From then on nf_flow_offload_ip_hook(), registered at the ingress hook, looks each packet up and sends it straight to the output device, skipping the routing lookup and the remaining netfilter hooks.
Connection tracking and NATnet/netfilter/nf_conntrack_core.c, include/net/netfilter/nf_conntrack.h, net/netfilter/nf_conntrack_proto_tcp.c, net/netfilter/nf_nat_core.c, net/netfilter/nf_nat_masquerade.c, net/netfilter/nf_conntrack_netlink.c, net/netfilter/nf_conntrack_expect.c, net/netfilter/nf_conntrack_helper.cnf_conntrack_in() runs at PRE_ROUTING and LOCAL_OUT. resolve_normal_ct() looks the tuple up and calls init_conntrack() for a new flow, and later code reads the entry and state from the skb with nf_ct_get(). A new entry becomes visible only when __nf_conntrack_confirm() inserts it at the last hook the packet passes, so dropped packets leave no state behind. nf_conntrack_tcp_packet() and tcp_in_window() track TCP state and sequence windows, and gc_worker() expires old entries on an adaptive schedule. nf_conntrack_init_start() sizes the hash, __nf_conntrack_find_get() shows lockless lookup over a SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU cache, and nf_ct_resolve_clash() and nf_ct_resolve_clash_harder() keep parallel UDP first packets from being dropped. nf_ct_expect_related_report() registers an expectation for a helper. For NAT, nf_nat_setup_info() chooses a mapping with get_unique_tuple() on a connection’s first packet (find_appropriate_src(), find_best_ips_proto() and nf_nat_l4proto_unique_tuple() in turn), and nf_nat_packet() rewrites every later one; nf_nat_masquerade_ipv4() is the masquerade case. ctnetlink_new_conntrack() serves the netlink interface used by conntrack -I.
Netlink, rtnetlink and generic netlinknet/netlink/af_netlink.c, net/core/rtnetlink.c, net/netlink/genetlink.c, Documentation/netlink/specs/, tools/net/ynl/, include/net/netlink.h, include/uapi/linux/netlink.hnetlink_sendmsg() delivers a request to the kernel socket made by __netlink_kernel_create(), whose input function usually calls netlink_rcv_skb() to split it into messages. Replies go back with netlink_unicast() and events with netlink_broadcast(). Large dumps are resumable: __netlink_dump_start() records a callback, and netlink_dump() calls it again each time the reader drains the socket. rtnetlink_rcv_msg() dispatches the routing family to handlers registered with rtnl_register_many(), such as rtnl_newlink() and rtnl_dump_ifinfo(), many of them under rtnl_lock(). Generic netlink multiplexes the newer families: genl_register_family() adds one, genl_rcv_msg() dispatches, and ctrl_getfamily() resolves a name to an ID. The YAML specs generate both kernel parsers and ynl clients. netlink_autobind() assigns port ids, __netlink_ns_capable() checks the capability of both the socket opener and the sender, and netlink_ack() builds the extended ACK. __nla_validate_parse() applies an nla_policy with the strictness the caller asked for.
page_pool and netmemnet/core/page_pool.c, include/net/page_pool/types.h, include/net/netmem.h, net/core/page_pool_user.c, net/core/netmem_priv.hpage_pool_create() sets up a pool for one RX queue, with a lockless cache for its NAPI context (PP_ALLOC_CACHE_SIZE, 128 entries) and a ptr_ring for buffers freed elsewhere. page_pool_alloc_netmems() tries __page_pool_get_cached(), which refills from the ring with page_pool_refill_alloc_cache(), then a memory provider if one is attached, and finally __page_pool_alloc_netmems_slow(), which allocates in bulk and DMA-maps. Freed buffers return through page_pool_put_unrefed_netmem() → __page_pool_put_page(), which syncs only the bytes the device may have written (page_pool_dma_sync_for_device()) and recycles with page_pool_recycle_in_cache() or page_pool_recycle_in_ring(). netmem.h defines netmem_ref and struct net_iov, and page_pool_user.c reports pools over the netdev family. page_pool_register_dma_index() records each mapped page in pool->dma_mapped and stores the index in pp_magic; page_pool_destroy() → page_pool_scrub() unmaps everything still in flight, and page_pool_release_retry() waits for the pages themselves. netmem_is_pp() in netmem_priv.h checks PP_SIGNATURE. The per-CPU system_page_pool is created in net/core/dev.c and used by skb_cow_data_for_xdp() and xdp_build_skb_from_zc() (net/core/xdp.c).
Memory providers: devmem TCP and io_uring zcrxinclude/net/page_pool/memory_provider.h, net/core/netdev_rx_queue.c, net/core/devmem.c, net/ipv4/tcp.c, io_uring/zcrx.c, net/core/sock.cstruct memory_provider_ops is the interface: a page_pool whose RX queue has mp_params set gets its buffers from the provider instead of the page allocator. net_mp_open_rxq() → __net_mp_open_rxq() installs a provider after checking header split, hds_thresh, XDP and AF_XDP; dev_get_min_mp_channel_count() is the reverse check that XDP, ethtool and AF_XDP make. netdev_rx_queue_reconfig() (via netdev_rx_queue_restart()) rebuilds the queue through the driver’s netdev_queue_mgmt_ops. In devmem.c, net_devmem_bind_dmabuf() maps a dma-buf into net_iovs, net_devmem_bind_dmabuf_to_queue() attaches it, and mp_dmabuf_devmem_alloc_netmems() is the allocation callback. tcp_recvmsg_dmabuf() returns fragment tokens instead of data, and sock_devmem_dontneed() in sock.c takes them back. For transmit, net_devmem_get_binding() checks that the route leaves through the bound device. netdev_nl_sock_priv_destroy() in netdev-genl.c unbinds when the netlink socket closes. In io_uring/zcrx.c, io_register_zcrx_ifq() registers user memory and a refill ring, io_pp_zc_alloc_netmems() refills from the buffers the application has returned (io_zcrx_ring_refill()), and io_zcrx_recv() posts completions that point into that memory, copying with io_zcrx_copy_chunk() whatever landed elsewhere. io_zcrx_ctrl() handles IORING_REGISTER_ZCRX_CTRL: zcrx_export(), import_zcrx() and zcrx_flush_rq().
AF_XDPnet/xdp/xsk.c, net/xdp/xsk_queue.h, net/xdp/xsk_buff_pool.c, net/xdp/xdp_umem.cxsk_create() makes the socket. xsk_setsockopt() sets up the UMEM (xdp_umem_create() pins its pages with xdp_umem_pin_pages()) and the four rings, which xsk_mmap() maps into user space. xsk_bind() attaches the socket to one queue, creating the buffer pool with xp_create_and_assign_umem(); xp_assign_dev() tries zero-copy mode and falls back to copy mode unless XDP_ZEROCOPY was demanded. On receive, __xsk_map_redirect() → xsk_rcv() chooses xsk_rcv_zc() or __xsk_rcv(), and xskq_prod_reserve_desc() in xsk_queue.h posts the descriptor. On transmit, zero-copy drivers call xsk_tx_peek_desc() and copy mode uses xsk_generic_xmit(). xp_alloc() is how a zero-copy driver fills its RX ring. In copy mode, xsk_skb_metadata() applies TX metadata (checksum, launch time) to the skb, and xs->max_tx_budget (XDP_MAX_TX_SKB_BUDGET) bounds each __xsk_generic_xmit() pass.
Packet socketsnet/packet/af_packet.cpacket_create() makes an AF_PACKET socket and registers a packet_type tap, so __netif_receive_skb_core() calls packet_rcv(), or tpacket_rcv() once packet_set_ring() has set up an mmap ring, for every matching packet. Both clone or copy the skb, so the packet continues through the stack. tpacket_rcv() copies into the next ring frame, and with TPACKET_V3 fills blocks of variable-sized frames that user space retires a block at a time. fanout_demux_hash() is one of the fanout policies that spread a capture across a group of sockets. packet_snd() builds an skb from user data and injects it straight into the device transmit path, below the IP layer.
Kernel TLSnet/tls/tls_main.c, net/tls/tls_sw.c, net/tls/tls_strp.c, net/tls/tls_device.c, net/handshake/, net/tls/tls_device_fallback.ctls_init() runs on setsockopt(TCP_ULP, "tls"), and tls_build_proto() builds the tls_prots variants of TCP’s struct proto. do_tls_setsockopt_conf() takes the keys and calls tls_set_device_offload() (or tls_set_device_offload_rx()), falling back to tls_set_sw_offload(), and update_sk_prot() switches the socket over. The software path is tls_sw_sendmsg() → tls_push_record() and tls_sw_recvmsg(), with tls_strp.c finding record boundaries (tls_rx_msg_size()) and tls_decrypt_sg() decrypting. In tls_device.c, tls_device_sendmsg() leaves encryption to the NIC, and tls_device_decrypted() checks which received records the NIC already decrypted. tls_device_fallback.c holds tls_validate_xmit_skb() and tls_sw_fallback(), which encrypt in software a record leaving through another device. TLS 1.3 rekeying is the update path of do_tls_setsockopt_conf(), with tls_check_pending_rekey() and tls_finish_key_update() in tls_sw.c. net/handshake/ (handshake_nl_accept_doit()) passes in-kernel handshakes to a user-space agent.
PSPnet/psp/psp_main.c, net/psp/psp_sock.c, net/psp/psp_nl.c, include/net/psp/functions.h, Documentation/networking/psp.rstpsp_dev_create() registers a device’s struct psp_dev and its psp_dev_ops. In psp_nl.c, psp_nl_key_rotate_doit() rotates the device key, and psp_nl_rx_assoc_doit() and psp_nl_tx_assoc_doit() attach keys to a TCP socket through psp_sock_assoc_set_rx() and psp_sock_assoc_set_tx(), the latter installing psp_validate_xmit() as the socket’s sk_validate_xmit_skb. Drivers call psp_dev_rcv() to strip the encapsulation and attach SKB_EXT_PSP, and psp_dev_encapsulate() to add it. psp_sk_rx_policy_check() in functions.h is the per-packet check TCP makes on input.

Chapter 23 — eBPF

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
The bpf() system callkernel/bpf/syscall.cEvery eBPF object in the kernel is created here. __sys_bpf() is one long switch over the command codes, and reading it end to end is the fastest way to see the whole subsystem’s surface at once: create a map, load a program, attach a link, iterate objects, load BTF. Note that almost everything returns a file descriptor over an anonymous inode, so the lifetime rules you already know from Chapter 12 govern eBPF objects too. bpf_prog_load() is where the chapter’s pipeline becomes code: copy in the instructions, check the capability, call the verifier, select the runtime.
BPF tokens and delegationkernel/bpf/token.c, kernel/bpf/inode.cbpf_token_capable() is four lines and contains the whole idea: the capability check is made in the user namespace that owns the bpffs instance the token came from, not in the initial one. Then read the delegate_cmds, delegate_maps, delegate_progs and delegate_attachs mount options in kernel/bpf/inode.c — four bitmasks by which a host decides once, at mount time, exactly how much of bpf() a container may reach. It is the same structure as the capability bounding set of Chapter 20, applied to a subsystem rather than to a process.
The instruction set and the interpreterinclude/uapi/linux/bpf.h, kernel/bpf/core.cThe struct bpf_insn definition and the opcode class macros at the top of the UAPI header are the whole machine in fifty lines. Then read ___bpf_prog_run() in kernel/bpf/core.c, which is the reference semantics every JIT back end must reproduce: a computed-goto dispatch table with one label per opcode. The two reused class values, BPF_JMP32 and BPF_ALU64, sit in the slots classic BPF spent on BPF_RET and BPF_MISC — a fossil of the extension visible in the header itself.
Locking inside a BPF programkernel/bpf/rqspinlock.c, kernel/bpf/helpers.cThe comment block at the head of rqspinlock.c explains the problem better than any summary: a program holding a lock cannot be allowed to spin forever, because it may have been attached anywhere, including inside the very code path that holds the lock it wants. The answer is a queued spinlock that detects AA and ABBA deadlocks and abandons the attempt on a timer, so bpf_res_spin_lock() returns -EDEADLK or -ETIMEDOUT and the program must cope. Compare with the older bpf_spin_lock() in kernel/bpf/helpers.c, which cannot fail and is correspondingly restricted in where it may be used.
Converting classic BPFnet/core/filter.cbpf_convert_filter() translates a classic sock_filter program into the modern instruction set, so that one engine runs both and classic socket filters still work. It is worth reading beside the seccomp filter code of Chapter 20, which is the other surviving consumer of classic BPF.
The verifierkernel/bpf/verifier.c, include/linux/bpf_verifier.hAt roughly twenty-six thousand lines this is one of the largest single files in the kernel, and it should be read from the outside in. Start with bpf_check() at the bottom for the order of the passes, then check_cfg() for the control-flow rejections, then do_check() for the abstract interpretation itself. struct bpf_reg_state in the header is what the whole file manipulates: a register’s type, its signed and unsigned bounds, and its tnum of known bits. is_state_visited() is the pruning that makes the search finish at all, and BPF_COMPLEXITY_LIMIT_INSNS in include/linux/bpf.h is the million-instruction ceiling beyond which even a safe program is refused.
Precision trackingkernel/bpf/verifier.cThe comment above __mark_chain_precision() is the best explanation of the mechanism anywhere, and it is long because the idea is subtle. Pruning can only work if two states reaching the same instruction are allowed to differ, and they are allowed to differ in every register whose exact value nothing depended on. The function backtracks through the instruction history marking the ones that did matter as precise. Read it together with is_state_visited(), which is the consumer: only the precise parts of two states have to agree.
Loops and modular verificationkernel/bpf/verifier.cThree mechanisms let the verifier accept what an exhaustive path search could not. do_check_subprogs() verifies each global function independently and checks only the BTF prototype at the call site, so a program’s cost is the sum of its functions rather than the product of their paths; the comment above it works the example. process_iter_next_call() models an open-coded iterator as a state machine, with another long and worthwhile comment on the cases the heuristic cannot handle. And is_may_goto_insn() together with may_goto_depth is the countdown the verifier supplies for a loop nobody can bound.
Reference trackingkernel/bpf/verifier.cacquire_reference() and release_reference_state() are a small, self-contained illustration of the verifier enforcing protocol rather than memory safety. A program that could reach its exit on any path still holding a looked-up socket or an unsubmitted ring-buffer reservation is rejected at load. Read these two functions to see how a resource leak becomes a compile-time error.
The JITarch/x86/net/bpf_jit_comp.c, arch/arm64/net/bpf_jit_comp.cbpf_int_jit_compile() in either file shows the multi-pass structure: the same program is assembled more than once because a branch’s encoding depends on a distance not known until the code has been laid out. emit_indirect_jump() on x86_64 is where Spectre-v2 mitigation meets eBPF, and comparing the two architectures’ back ends is the clearest demonstration that the instruction set really was designed to map onto hardware.
JIT memory and hardeningkernel/bpf/core.cRead bpf_jit_binary_pack_alloc() and bpf_jit_binary_pack_finalize() together: the back end emits into an ordinary writable buffer and the finished image is copied into a shared read-only executable pack with bpf_arch_text_copy(), so the code is never writable at the address it executes from. Then look at the bpf_prog_pack allocator, which packs many small programs into shared executable huge pages instead of giving each its own, and at bpf_jit_limit, which caps how much of that memory unprivileged loading can consume. bpf_jit_blind_constants() implements the constant blinding that stops an attacker from smuggling a chosen instruction sequence into the JIT’s output as immediate data.
Finding and faulting in JIT’d codekernel/bpf/core.c, arch/x86/net/bpf_jit_comp.cTwo problems that only exist once a program is native code. bpf_prog_kallsyms_add() and is_bpf_text_address() are what let an unwinder, perf and /proc/kallsyms make sense of an address inside a generated image instead of printing a bare number. And in the x86_64 back end, the code around EX_TYPE_BPF shows how a BPF_PROBE_MEM load becomes an ordinary machine instruction plus an exception-table entry, so a fault writes zero into the destination register and execution resumes — the native half of a promise the verifier made.
Maps: the generic layerkernel/bpf/syscall.c, include/linux/bpf.hmap_create() and struct bpf_map_ops are the whole abstraction. The ops table is why a hash table, a ring buffer, an XDP redirect target and a table of other maps can all be operated through one interface. map_check_btf() is the hinge between maps and the type system: it is what lets a map’s keys and values be typed rather than opaque, and therefore what lets the verifier reason about a spin lock or a timer embedded in a value.
Map implementationskernel/bpf/arraymap.c, kernel/bpf/hashtab.c, kernel/bpf/ringbuf.cRead these three in order. kernel/bpf/arraymap.c is the simplest possible bpf_map_ops and also, through map_mmap, the mechanism behind global variables and read-only data in eBPF C. kernel/bpf/hashtab.c shows the per-CPU and preallocation strategies that keep updates off the slab allocator on a hot path. kernel/bpf/ringbuf.c is the reserve-then-commit discipline that replaced the per-CPU perf buffer, and its interaction with the verifier’s reference tracking is the best example of the two mechanisms cooperating.
Special fields inside a map valueinclude/linux/bpf.h, kernel/bpf/btf.c, kernel/bpf/helpers.cenum btf_field_type is the list of things a value may contain besides data: spin locks, timers, workqueue items, kptrs, and the list and tree nodes that let a program build intrusive data structures in map memory. btf_parse_fields() is where the kernel finds them by walking the value’s BTF, and the bpf_obj_new(), bpf_kptr_xchg() and list/rbtree kfuncs are how a program manipulates them. This is the mechanism that turns a map value from a blob into a typed structure the kernel is jointly responsible for.
Arena: maps that are not key/valuekernel/bpf/arena.cThe header comment is the clearest explanation of the scheme anywhere: a sparse region mapped into user space at one address and into the kernel at another, with the program addressing it by the low thirty-two bits of a pointer so that a single BPF_ADDR_SPACE_CAST instruction converts between the two views. Pages are faulted in on demand from either side. Read it to understand why an instruction exists purely to serve one map type, and what the verifier gives up in exchange.
bpffs and pinningkernel/bpf/inode.cA small filesystem whose only purpose is to hold references. Pinning a map or a link under /sys/fs/bpf gives it a name in the filesystem namespace so it survives the exit of the process that created it, which is how an eBPF deployment persists across restarts and how unrelated processes rendezvous on the same objects.
Program types and attach typesinclude/uapi/linux/bpf.hThe enum bpf_prog_type and enum bpf_attach_type lists side by side explain the two-level scheme: a broad type fixes the context and the available helpers, and the expected attach type narrows it to one specific hook. Nothing in the kernel knows about ELF section names — SEC("xdp") and SEC("fentry/...") are a libbpf convention that resolves to these two numbers before the load call is made.
Multiple programs on one hookkernel/bpf/mprog.c, include/linux/bpf_mprog.hA small library that exists because two unrelated tools may both want a program on the same hook and neither may displace the other. The header comment lays out the ordering model: an array revised under RCU so a running program sees one complete sequence or the other, with attachment requests naming a position relative to an existing entry by descriptor or id. Read it before net/core/filter.c’s tcx code, which is its main consumer.
Extension programskernel/bpf/verifier.c, kernel/bpf/trampoline.cFollow BPF_PROG_TYPE_EXT through check_attach_btf_id(). An extension attaches to a global function in another loaded program and replaces it, with the verifier checking that the signatures match. It is the clearest demonstration that the modular verification of global functions is a contract and not merely an optimization, and it reuses the trampoline machinery built for fentry.
Linkskernel/bpf/syscall.c, include/linux/bpf.hbpf_link replaced a dozen per-subsystem attach interfaces with one object. Read link_create() and link_update(): the latter swaps the program behind a live attachment atomically, which is what lets a busy hook be upgraded without a gap. Contrast it with the older BPF_PROG_ATTACH path, still present for compatibility, where ownership of an attachment was implicit.
Tracing attachmentkernel/trace/bpf_trace.cThis is where eBPF meets the kernel’s tracing infrastructure (kprobes, tracepoints, perf events; see this chapter’s §7.1 and Chapter 10’s §7.3). bpf_trace_run() and the bpf_tracing_func_proto() helper-availability callback are the two halves worth reading: one is the call path, the other is how the kernel decides which helpers a tracing program may use. The per-program-type func_proto callback returning NULL is the entire mechanism by which an XDP program is prevented from calling a tracing helper.
BPF trampolineskernel/bpf/trampoline.cThe machinery behind fentry/fexit and behind BPF LSM and struct_ops. A trampoline is generated code that saves the traced function’s real arguments into a form an eBPF program can receive with proper types, calls the program, and restores. Read it to understand why fentry costs so much less than a kprobe and why BTF is a prerequisite rather than a convenience.
Changing behavior from a tracing hookkernel/trace/bpf_trace.c, kernel/bpf/trampoline.cThe three places where a tracing program stops being an observer. bpf_override_return() and its ALLOW_ERROR_INJECTION check, bpf_probe_write_user() with the warning it prints on first use, and the BPF_TRAMP_MODIFY_RETURN arm of the trampoline, which is what lets an fmod_ret program substitute a function’s result. Reading them together makes clear that eBPF’s guarantee is about memory and termination, not about non-interference.
Attaching to many functions at oncekernel/trace/bpf_trace.c, kernel/trace/fprobe.cbpf_kprobe_multi_link_attach() and the fprobe layer beneath it. One call takes a list of symbols and produces one link covering all of them, patching the sites in a batch rather than one attach operation per function. The session variant is here too: it pairs the entry and return probes of a single call so a value computed on entry reaches the return probe without a map keyed by thread.
XDPinclude/net/xdp.h, net/core/filter.cbpf_prog_run_xdp() in include/net/xdp.h is deliberately tiny — it is inlined into every driver’s receive loop, which is the point. The redirect actions in net/core/filter.c are where the map types return: a devmap, cpumap or xskmap lookup is how XDP_REDIRECT chooses its target. Compare the native path with the generic XDP_FLAGS_SKB_MODE fallback to see exactly what the socket buffer costs.
XDP metadata and multi-bufferinclude/net/xdp.h, net/core/xdp.cThe XDP_METADATA_KFUNC_xxx macro list defines the driver-supplied hints (receive timestamp, flow hash, VLAN tag) that exist because no socket buffer has been built yet to hold them; each driver implements them and the JIT inlines the call. Nearby, the fragment handling behind BPF_F_XDP_HAS_FRAGS shows the price of admitting jumbo frames: direct packet access still reaches only the first fragment.
Traffic control: tcxnet/core/dev.c, net/sched/cls_bpf.ctc_run() and tcx_run() in net/core/dev.c are the modern hook; net/sched/cls_bpf.c is the classifier it superseded. Reading them in that order shows the whole arc of the chapter in miniature: an eBPF facility bolted onto a pre-eBPF subsystem, then rebuilt as a first-class attach point with link ownership and a defined order when several programs share a hook.
Ordering cgroup programskernel/bpf/cgroup.ccompute_effective_progs() flattens a whole ancestry into one array each time an attachment changes, so that running the hook costs a walk of a flat list and nothing more. It also answers the question the attach flags raise: the walk goes from the cgroup upward, so multi-attached programs run innermost first, while BPF_F_PREORDER places a program in a second group filled from the opposite end of the same array and therefore run first and outermost first.
Socket-layer hooksnet/core/sock_map.c, net/core/skmsg.cThe third answer to “how early on the path?” — above the stack rather than below it. A sockmap holds sockets as map values, and skmsg.c is where a verdict program’s redirect hands a payload straight to another socket, so data moving between two local sockets never descends into the protocol stack. Read it after XDP and tc to see the same attach model applied at the opposite end of the path.
cgroup hookskernel/bpf/cgroup.cThe attach flags are the interesting part. BPF_F_ALLOW_OVERRIDE and BPF_F_ALLOW_MULTI decide what happens when programs attached at different levels of the cgroup tree all apply to one process, and the rule they implement — a descendant may tighten an ancestor’s policy but not loosen it — is the same containment principle the rest of the cgroup hierarchy of Chapter 3 uses.
What an LSM program may returnkernel/bpf/bpf_lsm.cbpf_lsm_get_retval_range() is where the “can only deny” property stops being a convention and becomes a load-time check: the verifier proves the program cannot return a value outside the range its hook permits. The two BTF_SET lists nearby are equally instructive — one names the hooks eBPF may not attach to at all, the other the hooks at which a sleepable program is allowed, which is what a policy that must resolve a path or read user memory needs.
BPF LSMsecurity/bpf/hooks.c, kernel/bpf/bpf_lsm.cThirty-nine lines in security/bpf/hooks.c are all it takes to turn every LSM hook in the kernel into an eBPF attach point, because the trampoline machinery does the rest. Read it beside include/linux/lsm_hook_defs.h from Chapter 20. The safety argument is structural rather than clever: an LSM hook runs after the ordinary permission checks have passed, so a program attached there can only deny.
When a loaded scheduler misbehaveskernel/sched/ext.cThe containment that makes sched_ext defensible, none of it a verifier property. scx_watchdog_workfn() and the stall check beside it notice a runnable task that has waited too long; scx_exit() records why a scheduler was ejected, including the deliberate self-abort and the SysRq key an operator can reach from a console; and scx_disable_workfn() is the unwinding that returns every task to the built-in scheduler. Read it with SCX_OPS_SWITCH_PARTIAL, which decides how much of the machine was at risk in the first place.
sched_extkernel/sched/ext.c, include/linux/sched/ext.hA complete scheduling class whose policy is supplied from user space. struct scx_dispatch_q in the header is the central data structure: callbacks move runnable tasks into dispatch queues and the kernel pulls from them, keeping policy in eBPF and mechanism in the kernel. Find the watchdog that reverts the system to the built-in scheduler when a runnable task goes unscheduled — it is the admission that verifying a program safe is not the same as verifying it correct.
BTFkernel/bpf/btf.c, include/uapi/linux/btf.hThe UAPI header is short and worth reading in full: twenty type kinds, numbered 0 through 19, referencing one another by index with a single shared string table. That compactness is why a complete description of the kernel’s own types can ship inside the kernel image and be exposed at /sys/kernel/btf/vmlinux. In kernel/bpf/btf.c, btf_check_all_metas() is the validation every loaded BTF blob passes, and bpf_core_apply() is the kernel side of CO-RE.
CO-RE inside the kernelkernel/bpf/btf.c, kernel/bpf/verifier.cbpf_core_apply() and the loop in the verifier that walks attr->core_relos show that CO-RE is not purely a user-space technique: a loader may hand the kernel the symbolic accesses and let it resolve them against its own BTF before verification begins. Read it to see the relocation kinds actually applied, including the bitfield shifts and the type-compatibility check that a description in terms of field offsets leaves out.
Tags: annotations that survive into BTFkernel/bpf/btf.c, include/uapi/linux/btf.hBTF_KIND_TYPE_TAG and BTF_KIND_DECL_TAG are the two BTF kinds that are not C types. The code that matches the tag values user and percpu is the bridge between a kernel header’s annotations and the verifier’s pointer flags: a field declared __user is typed as a user pointer in a program that reads it, without the kernel keeping a separate list. Declaration tags carry kfunc flags the same way.
Helperskernel/bpf/helpers.c, include/uapi/linux/bpf.hThe numbered helper list in the UAPI header is a stable ABI, which is both its virtue and the reason it stopped growing. Each helper’s struct bpf_func_proto declares the types the verifier will enforce at every call site. The per-program-type availability callback is what makes a helper visible to one kind of program and invisible to another, decided at load time rather than checked at run time.
Inlining a helper callkernel/bpf/verifier.c, kernel/bpf/arraymap.cThe fixup pass near the end of verifier.c rewrites some helper calls into straight-line code before the JIT ever sees them. array_map_gen_lookup() is the one to read: a bounds check and an indexed load replace a call, which is why a map lookup on a hot path costs almost nothing. The verifier can only do this because it already tracked which map the call refers to — the analysis that proves the call safe is the same one that makes it unnecessary.
kfuncskernel/bpf/btf.c, include/linux/btf.hThe flag definitions at the top of include/linux/btf.h are a compact statement of what the verifier can be asked to enforce about a kernel function: that its result must be null-checked, that it acquires or releases a reference, that it may sleep, that it is destructive. Note what is no longer there — KF_TRUSTED_ARGS was deleted once strict argument checking became the default for every kfunc, though the long comment explaining the rule survives. register_btf_kfunc_id_set() is how a subsystem, or a module, adds callable functions without touching a central table.
What a kfunc argument’s name meanskernel/bpf/verifier.c, kernel/bpf/btf.cSearch for btf_param_match_suffix. Because BTF records parameter names and not only types, a suffix on an argument’s name tells the verifier what the argument is for — the size of the preceding buffer, a value that must be constant, an iterator. The portability format doubles as an annotation channel, which is how the kfunc contract keeps growing without a registry to record it in.
struct_opskernel/bpf/bpf_struct_ops.c, net/ipv4/bpf_tcp_ca.cThe inversion of the whole subsystem: the kernel calling into eBPF. net/ipv4/bpf_tcp_ca.c is the example to read first, because struct tcp_congestion_ops is small enough to hold in mind, and it shows the full sequence — register the operations structure with its BTF, check each supplied program against the signature of the slot it fills, and only then make the function pointers live.
Other struct_ops implementationsnet/sched/bpf_qdisc.c, net/smc/smc_hs_bpf.c, kernel/sched/ext.cRead after the TCP example to see how far the pattern has been taken: a queueing discipline, an SMC handshake policy, and a whole CPU scheduler, each registering an operations structure that eBPF may fill in. bpf_struct_ops_map_link_update() in kernel/bpf/bpf_struct_ops.c is the other half of the story — how a live implementation is swapped for another while the kernel is calling into it.

Chapter 24 — io_uring

TopicPrimary filesWhy they matter
The three system callsio_uring/io_uring.c, include/uapi/linux/io_uring.hThe whole interface is io_uring_setup and io_uring_enter in the UAPI header, plus io_uring_register. Read io_uring_create() first: it allocates the context, sizes the rings, and fills the offsets the application will mmap with. The UAPI header is unusually rewarding on its own — it is the ABI, so the ring offsets, the SQE and CQE layouts, the setup flags and the opcode enumeration are all there in one file.
Submission and the two speedsio_uring/io_uring.cio_submit_sqes() is the loop that consumes the submission queue, and io_issue_sqe() the point where a request is tried without blocking. The chapter's central claim — that io_uring completes inline whenever it can and defers only when it must — is visible here as a single -EAGAIN retry path, not as a general-purpose thread pool.
Ring memory and the mmap offsetsio_uring/memmap.cHow the shared rings are allocated and handed to user space. Read alongside Chapter 9, because the ring pages are ordinary kernel memory mapped into the process, and the magic IORING_OFF_* offsets are how one file descriptor exposes three distinct regions.
